ShadeTraced and the light loop branch on RV_IRRADIANCE_FILL in several places: Realtime lights skipped (pbr_fragment.glsl:2963-2970), no light cull, the field fed back vs hitDirect only (3214-3301), and the sky probe (slot 0) at bake hits vs the nearest probe at frame hits. Static-only shadows are intended bake semantics (Lightmass does the same), not a defect. Sharing the hit shader also means the bake inherits the frame's approximations (unoccluded sky and ambient at hits, simplified hit BSDF), where a separate path-traced baker converges to a reference. So 'better' is not justified. It is 'consistent by construction, less accurate than a reference baker'.
- **C-BB1** (partly true). Claim: Could beat both RageV and the shipped baseline: Gather the bake per pixel. The realtime layer's one ray per traced pixel reads, at its hit, the baked field's stored light (bounce plus far direct from baked lamps) as well as the realtime cache. Its result replaces the receiver-side field read wherever the gather has an estimate. The bake's content is unchanged; only where it is sampled moves, from the receiver's cell to the hit point one bounce away.. Why: Unity and UE get contact-scale baked bounce only through lightmaps, which RageV cannot represent. id, Snowdrop and Anvil have no bake. This gives baked lamps per-pixel contact bounce and indirect shadows from a volume-only bake. It uses the ray the realtime layer already casts, at roughly the cost of one field fetch per hit.
  Evidence: ENGINE-NOTES 7ct (ENGINE-NOTES.md:13679-13711) supports the fixture half: 0.73 vs 1.48 vs 5.73. The same note shows the hybrid WORSE than realtime on the showroom's bright bay (1.00 vs 0.51). pbr_fragment.glsl:2095-2200: today's L2 field read is 1 mask + 9 bounce + 9 direct + 1 alive = ~20 fetches on the fast path. The careful path is 8 corners x ~19 = ~150 fetches. The '32 fetches, +0.55 ms' figure the claim cites (pbr_fragment.glsl:1660-1666) is a stale L1-era comment.
  Correction: The reasoning holds but the evidence is mixed, so this needs a measurement gate. (a) It must be gated on the showroom bright bay, where the one real-scene measurement had it losing. (b) 'Roughly the cost of one field fetch per hit' is wrong: it is ~20 fetches at best and ~150 on the careful path, and must be re-measured. (c) 'Anvil has no bake' is inaccurate: the phase-1 survey records a baked diffuse GI path shipping as an alternative on some platforms (not layered). (d) It reopens the owner's 'Baked means baked' ruling (Scene.cpp:1887-1901), because it needs the gather to run in baked scenes.
- **C-BB2** (plausible). Claim: Could beat both RageV and the shipped baseline: A differential correction for moving objects over the bake. When a realtime-layer ray hits a dynamic surface, it subtracts the radiance the baked field says arrived from that direction (L2 SH turned back into low-order radiance) and adds the dynamic surface's own radiance under all lights.. Why: UE and Unity leave dynamic objects neither bouncing baked light nor blocking baked bounce, apart from AO. Fully realtime engines get it right but pay for all light live. This keeps the bake and still makes a moving car block and bounce baked lamps' light.
  Evidence: The field stores L2 SH (pbr_fragment.glsl:1690-1715), and the bake excludes dynamic geometry (irradiance_fill.rvshader:357-361). No shipped precedent was found. The reasoning follows from differential rendering.
  Correction: Unmeasured, with real risks. The field stores irradiance, so recovering radiance means deconvolving L2 (ringing, negatives). The subtraction must use both the bounce set and the far-direct set. With 1 ray per traced pixel, a car covering a small solid angle will be noisy. It also requires the gather to run in baked scenes. It needs a gate (RT-22's moving cube against --reference-render, no region brighter than the reference, with --aa=none).
- **C-BB3** (partly true). Claim: Could beat both RageV and the shipped baseline: Drive each cell of the realtime cache by measured change. When a cell is re-shaded, compare the fresh estimate with the stored mean using the cell's own variance; restart the cell's average when the difference exceeds the noise, and otherwise accumulate up to a sample cap.. Why: Shipped caches react either by a fixed hysteresis or accumulation cap (Lumen caps its surface cache at 4 frames; SHaRC has a max-frames setting and a responsive mode) or by events (Snowdrop invalidation). Both trade lag against noise with a constant. Per-cell change testing is frame-rate independent and needs no event plumbing, and it extends RageV's measured-change idea from pixels to world cells.
  Evidence: The shipped baseline already has per-probe change detection. RTXGI DDGIVolume.h (fetched): probeIrradianceThreshold 0.25 'determines if a large lighting change has happened ... the hysteresis will be reduced', and probeBrightnessThreshold 0.10. Lumen's 4-frame cap (lumen_2022.txt:1405) and Snowdrop's event invalidation (snowdrop_gdc2024.txt:240-251) are as the claim states.
  Correction: The 'why' misstates the baseline. Shipped DDGI caches do react to measured change, but with a fixed ratio threshold. What is new is only a noise-relative (variance-based) threshold and a sample-count cap. The cited evidence (the pixel re-trace, 77% to 12.6%) is a different mechanism: an exact same-ray replay, not a statistical test on a cell's variance. It must be measured against a DDGI-style ratio threshold, not against a fixed hysteresis.

### C.8 Design changes the verification required

Each was applied to the roadmap's items on 2026-09-25 (section 9 lists them).

- **Realtime radiance cache.** Restating the baseline: the per-cell restart must be built as an A/B arm against RTXGI-DDGI's per-probe ratio threshold (probeIrradianceThreshold/probeBrightnessThreshold), not presented as beyond anything shipped. The emitter_lag gate should report both arms. Drop the 'measured change is ours only' rationale. Rests on: C-BB3, C-OB1.
- **Composition of indirect light at the pixel.** The bake-gather arm (beats_both item 1) needs extra gates: the showroom bright bay (mode 2), where 7ct measured the hybrid at 1.00 against realtime's 0.51, and a cost gate priced from today's L2 read (~20 fetches fast, ~150 careful), not '+0.55 ms / one fetch'. It must also be put to the owner as reopening 'Baked means baked', because the gather has to run in baked scenes. Rests on: C-BB1, C-C17.
- **One hit-shading contract.** The rationale must not claim the bake 'cannot drift'. List every RV_IRRADIANCE_FILL branch as one to remove or keep deliberately. Add the flat u_Scene.Ambient added unoccluded at bake hits (pbr_fragment.glsl:3221) to 'replaces', next to slot-0 sky. The leak gate needs a non-black-sky and non-zero-ambient variant of the sealed-room fixtures. Static-only shadow rays in the solve are intended and stay. Rests on: C-OB4, C-C8, C-C3, C-C4.
- **Realtime gather (per-pixel bounce).** Emitter NEE: fix gi-s1 and gi-s3 first and adopt the reservoir pick, and present the result as ahead of the raster-hybrid engines only once measured, not as better than the top engines. It still clamps (kNeeClamp), so the relative outlier stage must cover the emitter term as well. The SH output stays an arm behind its gate: 'SH recovers normal detail' is unproven here. The ray-count baseline to compare against is the per-tile budget (a ceiling of 4), not a flat 4. Rests on: C-OB2, C-C5, C-C1, C-OB1.
- **Baked field storage and leak control.** Keep the cell format if measured, but drop 'better than APV/VLM': UE VLM also stores L2. Justify the extra 9-coefficient direct set by cost (2x memory and fetches) against a single L2 set with baked lamps merged in (the VLM way), measured on the full-baked showroom where the cube deficit was found. Rests on: C-OB3.
- **Stale-bake detection and bake-solve fixes.** Add the stale 'Tile 9 carries the neighbour-visibility bits' comment (pbr_fragment.glsl:1702, actually tile 18) and the stale '32 fetches against 3, +0.55 ms' cost note (pbr_fragment.glsl:1660-1666) to the rewrite list. Rests on: C-C7.
- **Roadmap delta: Section 7 additions (on-surface caches).** Change 'On-surface caches (Lumen cards, OSC-GI) and lightmaps: they need a second UV set' to: lightmaps need a second UV set; Lumen cards are UV-less projections (lumen_2022.txt:959-962) and need card placement plus a runtime capture pass, not a vertex-format change. The reason for not building them must be something else (cost or scope). Rests on: C-C9.
- **Layering rule: who owns each light path's bounce.** The claim holds. Name the leftover path explicitly: the baked reflection probe's irradiance (skyDiffuse) freezes whatever Realtime lights were on at capture into every Baked frame. Retiring the probe-as-sky term in RT mode (already listed) is what removes it, so the gate must include a probe captured with a Realtime light on. Rests on: C-C2.

### C.9 New findings from the verification

- **C-N1.** ENGINE-NOTES 7ct (ENGINE-NOTES.md:13704-13711): on the showroom's bright bay (mode 2) the 'rays end in the field' hybrid scored 1.00 against realtime's 0.51, so it was worse than realtime. It won only on the irradiance_field fixture, where it cost just +0.07 ms. The compare phase quoted only the fixture error.
- **C-N2.** The field-read cost notes are stale. pbr_fragment.glsl:1660-1666 ('32 fetches against 3, +0.55 ms') dates from the L1/Chebyshev field. Today's L2 read (pbr_fragment.glsl:2095-2200) is ~20 fetches on the fast path (mask, 9 bounce, 9 direct, alive) and ~8 x 19 on the careful per-corner path. Every per-hit field cost estimate needs re-measuring.
- **C-N3.** Bake hits also add the flat u_Scene.Ambient with no occlusion (ShadeTraced, pbr_fragment.glsl:3221). This is a second sealed-room leak next to the slot-0 sky, and the leak fixtures (AmbientIntensity 0) cannot see it.
- **C-N4.** pbr_fragment.glsl:1702 says 'Tile 9 carries the neighbour-visibility bits', but RV_FIELD_VISIBILITY_TILE is 18 (tile 9 is the first direct-light tile). This stale comment was missed by C-C7.
- **C-N5.** The NRD README explicitly recommends history confidence from a gradient between stored and re-traced previous-frame radiance using the previous RNG seed, at about 1/5 resolution. RageV's measured change is that recipe, not something beyond it.
- **C-N6.** RTXGI DDGI ships per-probe large-change detection (probeIrradianceThreshold 0.25 reduces hysteresis; probeBrightnessThreshold 0.10). The shipped baseline for cache anti-lag is not only fixed hysteresis or events.
- **C-N7.** In the editor the ray budget is bound (EditorLayer.cpp:683,843), so the GI ray count per tile is allocated between 1 and the level's ceiling (rtgi_trace.rvshader:341-346). 'High = 4 rays' is a ceiling.
- **C-N8.** Under the project's own default (RayTracedGiSource: Baked, honoured in the garage and on the bridge), neither GI measured change nor the gather's emitter NEE runs at all. The two 'ours better' GI features are inactive in the owner's configuration.
- **C-N9.** The project runs RtOptimisation: Quality, whose ChangeRays is the trace's count. The GI change record costs ~0.59 ms while moving there (RT-MEASURED-CHANGE.md:344-346), not the 0.21 ms quoted (Balanced/Performance).
- **C-N10.** The garage's single reflection probe is Update: Baked with a 42 m influence and never recaptures on a light change (Scene.cpp:3270-3306). Its irradiance feeds skyDiffuse under Baked, so any Realtime light that was on when it was captured is frozen, unshadowed, into the garage's ambient.
- **C-N11.** The phase-1 survey records that AC Shadows ships a baked diffuse GI path as an alternative on some platforms and modes (not layered). C-BB1's 'Anvil has no bake' is inaccurate.
- **C-N12.** The AC Shadows Advances 2025 PDF could not be read (WebFetch 10 MB limit; download not authorised). C-C16's numbers remain unverified from the primary source.

### C.10 The comparison's corrections to the survey

- Phase 1 said RageV 'already has lightmap UVs for Static meshes' and 'owns lightmap UVs and an atlas pipeline'. Wrong: MeshVertex has a single UV set (Mesh.h:17-22), BAKING-ROADMAP.md:30-36 calls lightmaps unrepresentable, and RT-SERIES-2.md:393 says they are not planned. The irradiance 'atlas' is a 3D probe texture. OSC-GI and Lumen-style surface caches therefore need a vertex-format change and an unwrap step.
- Phase 1 said RageV's realtime pass 'traces 1 ray per pixel at half resolution'. The project runs RTGI High: 4 rays per traced pixel at width/2 x height/2 (FrameGraphBuilder.cpp:363-383; SampleProject.rvproject:20). RageV's 'half' is the industry's 'quarter' resolution, so RageV casts 4x the shipped engines' rays at the same traced-pixel count.
- Phase 1 said the realtime pass 'treats a miss as sky'. A first-bounce miss adds nothing (rtgi_trace.rvshader:598-602); sky reaches the pixel through the probe term. Only the GiBounces=2 second ray returns raw sky on a miss (rtgi_trace.rvshader:625-626).
- Phase 1 said Snowdrop fixes probes stuck in geometry with a validity volume. In the GDC 2024 text, the Average and Validity volumes serve mainly fog and clouds (snowdrop_gdc2024.txt:337-341). Probe relocation exists as an option but 'didn't gave any significant benefit in Avatar so it was not used' (lines 176-182).
- Phase 1 said 'the cache fallback alone cut tracing by 52%' (Snowdrop). The table shows tracing 4.621 ms, then 3.054 ms (-34%) with screen-space tracing, then 1.462 ms (-52% against that step) with the cache fallback, -68% overall (snowdrop_gdc2024.txt:1153-1160). The 52% is the step after screen tracing, not the fallback alone against the baseline.
- Phase 1 attributed screen-lighting reuse at hits only to id. Snowdrop's hit shader also reprojects the hit to the previous frame and reuses that lighting (snowdrop_gdc2024.txt:660-676), and its RT world uses one untextured material per mesh (lines 36-57).
- Phase 1 said 'No PC native-resolution numbers are published' for Lumen. The 2022 talk gives a PC figure, Lake House on an RTX 2080 Ti within its 1080p measurement context: final gather 4.01 ms at Epic and 8.01 ms at 1 rpp (lumen_2022.txt:2972-2990). It is not native 1440p+, so the substance of the claim stands.
- Phase 1's Lumen rays-per-pixel fractions (1/16 at High, 1/4 at Epic) cannot be confirmed from the extracted text: the glyphs are lost. The talk confirms Epic has 4x High's rays per pixel and that ArchVis uses 1 rpp (lumen_2022.txt:2896-2935, 2972-2990). The per-pass costs 2.77/4.63, 1.03/1.17 and 3.05/5.52 ms, the 1024^2/512^2 surface-cache budgets, the 4-frame cap, the 32x32 world probes and the priority queue are all confirmed.
- Phase 1's questions assumed the baked field might be L1 or ambient-cube with Chebyshev visibility. The code stores L2 SH (9 coefficients) for bounce and separately for baked direct light, with a 6-neighbour visibility bitmask (IrradianceVolume.h:136-159; irradiance_fill.rvshader:488-534). The Chebyshev depth moments were removed, and RageV's own comments (IrradianceVolume.h:25-44; pbr_fragment.glsl:1645-1653, 2033-2041) and BAKING-ROADMAP.md:95-104 still describe the old design. This is a documentation defect in RageV.
- Correction to RT-SERIES-2 RT2-26: 'cells that fall inside walls move out (probe relocation), which fixes the leak case' is stated as a sure fix. RageV measured relocation on 2026-08-26 as improving one fixture and worsening the sun-lit ones (irradiance_fill.rvshader:138-148), so it must be an arm with an adoption rule.
- Correction to RT-SERIES-2 section 6: the 1.0 ms realtime-layer proposal at native is below every shipped figure at about 1M traced pixels (Snowdrop 3.88 ms on Xbox Series X; AC 2.65 ms on an RTX 4070, per phase 1). A 1.5-2.0 ms line is realistic.

### C.11 Questions left open

As the survey recorded them. Where the verification later read a primary talk the survey could not fetch, the verification's verdict above supersedes the line here.

- Lumen's cost at native 1440p or higher on PC RTX 40/50 GPUs: Epic publishes only console 1080p budgets with TSR, so no primary anchor exists for a 2560x1600 native comparison.
- How the owner's RTX 5070 Ti Laptop compares with the RTX 4070/4080/5080 behind every published number: no primary source measured it. All scaling has to be measured on the owner's machine.
- Cyberpunk 2077 RT Overdrive: the per-pass ms for ReSTIR GI and SHaRC, and how SHaRC is configured. The GDC 2024 talk needs a login; the 2023 ReSTIR course slides carry no timings.
- Alan Wake 2: its raster (non-path-traced) GI architecture and its path-traced indirect technique. The GDC 2024 talk needs a login, and Remedy's public article gives no details.
- UE Stationary lights: whether changing colour or intensity at runtime also rescales their baked indirect light. The documentation wording is ambiguous; my reading is that the bounce stays baked.
- No shipped engine was found that layers a ray-traced bounce for Realtime lights only on top of a bake, as D4 does. The nearest is Unity running baked GI alongside Enlighten realtime GI (CPU radiosity), which Unity advises against. The additivity claim needs RageV's own diff proof.
- The exact final-gather resolution fractions in id Tech 8 (half or quarter): the glyphs were lost in PDF text extraction. The speaker notes confirm it depends on the quality setting.
- Indiana Jones' default (non-path-traced) GI is described only as an early iteration of the id Tech 8 system; its details are not published.
- Shipped costs for Path of Exile 2's radiance cascades and for GIBS in College Football 25 are not published.
- Whether Activision's Neural Light Grid shipped in a Call of Duty title; the page read does not say.
- How HDRP's ray-traced GI interacts with lightmaps and mixed lights; the pages read do not document it.
- ReSTIR PT Enhanced and Split Radiance Cascades were measured on hardware and in frameworks (Falcor, CUDA/OptiX) far from a Vulkan fragment/compute ray-query engine; the port cost is unknown.
- Claims the verification could not settle without a measurement or a source: C-C16, C-BB2 (each is gated in the roadmap item named above).

## D. Denoising, temporal reconstruction, anti-aliasing, upscaling, post

**In short.** RageV's per-signal accumulators, demodulation and half-float rounding match what ships. It is behind on what lets a signal stand alone (no history fix for young pixels, no outlier stage on the diffuse signals) and on TAA (a hand-widened min/max box). The primary talks show Lumen and MegaLights rely on their final temporal pass, so rule 3 is stricter than Epic's design. The verified verdicts, with the candidates tied to their items, are in the roadmap's section 11.

**Counts.** Comparison topics: 11 equal, 8 behind, 4 missing, 3 not comparable, 1 better (claimed). Verification of 22 claims: 12 confirmed, 6 partly true, 2 plausible, 2 refuted.

**The survey, in its own summary.** What the top titles ship: every ray-traced signal gets its own denoiser before the final temporal pass. NVIDIA's NRD is the reference open implementation. It has three denoisers: ReBLUR and ReLAX for diffuse and specular, and SIGMA for shadows. It ships in Cyberpunk 2077 and Watch Dogs: Legion, and Alan Wake 2 uses it when Ray Reconstruction is off. Each NRD denoiser keeps:; a per-pixel count of accumulated frames (default cap 30);; a disocclusion test based on plane distance (1% of view depth by default);; a short "fast" history (6 frames) that bounds the long one;; a history-fix pass that rebuilds young pixels from their neighbours;; firefly suppression;; a blur whose radius shrinks as history grows and follows the hit distance;; its own final stabilisation pass (up to 63 frames). Measured cost on an RTX 4080 at 1440p: ReBLUR diffuse+specular 2.55 ms, ReLAX 3.2 ms, SIGMA 0.40 ms. Engines with their own denoisers follow the same pattern: idTech8 (0.52 ms to denoise plus 0.45 ms for the temporal upscale, 4K on a 4080), Battlefield V reflections, AMD's FidelityFX Denoiser, and Lumen. But several of them still say the final TAA is an extra temporal filter (idTech8 says so outright; AMD says TAA can clean up afterwards). So the owner's no-TAA-reliance rule is stricter than common practice. NRD's own structure, which finishes stabilising each signal inside the denoiser, is the shipped design that meets it. The neural alternatives are vendor-locked:; **DLSS Ray Reconstruction** (NVIDIA): replaces the denoisers and the upscaler with one network. It works in Vulkan and at DLAA (native resolution). NVIDIA lists about 2.6-2.7 ms on an RTX 5070 at 1440p in Performance mode; the DLAA cost is not published. It is a black box and merges denoising with AA.; **FSR Ray Regeneration** (AMD): DX12 and RDNA 4 only, 8.1 ms for four signals at 1440p on an RX 9070 XT.; **Intel XeSS**: no denoiser. Final TAA done well is the Karis 2014, Salvi 2016 and INSIDE recipe: jitter, a variance clip in YCoCg, velocity taken from the nearest depth, and a Catmull-Rom history fetch. UE5's TSR adds a flicker analysis (period 3 frames, adjusted to frame rate) and thin-geometry detection. For the rest of the post chain, engines ship a histogram auto-exposure with adaptation speed in f-stops per second, pre-exposed scene colour, Jimenez-style mip-chain bloom with a Karis average and no hard threshold, and a filmic tonemapper: UE uses an ACES fit, Blender uses AgX, and GT7 released its own in 2025. Research frontier: temporal gradients (A-SVGF; NRD's history-confidence input) are the best-supported way to shorten history only where the lighting really changed. Filter-adapted blue noise and a faster à-trous schedule reduce noise and cost cheaply. The vendor-neutral neural denoisers are still papers, with no shipped cost at 1600p/60.

**The comparison, in its own summary.** Where RageV is ahead. It already runs the A-SVGF method that phase 1 listed as a frontier idea: "measured change" re-lights last frame's sample with the same random numbers and shortens a history only where the light really changed. It covers the direct light, GI, reflections and the glass reflection, at 0.048 ms parked and about 0.56 ms moving at 1600x900. Epic's MegaLights and Lumen use rules of thumb for this, and NRD only accepts such a signal as an optional input. RageV's histories are rounded onto the half-float grid stochastically, the same trick MegaLights uses to store lighting in R11G11B10F. Its TAA checks object id, depth and normal and searches the neighbouring texels, which is more than a Karis/UE4-style TAA does. Where they are ahead. Every shipped denoiser rebuilds young pixels from their neighbours (NRD's history fix, MegaLights' disocclusion boost, Lumen's double-strength bilateral). RageV sets the young blur to zero on all four signals, and its code says TAA carries the direct light's noise. The diffuse-kind signals have no outlier stage. In TAA:; the colour box is min/max, widened up to 8x on rough surfaces;; the depth test is a fixed 2%;; brightness weighting works on absolute radiance;; velocity is not dilated. There is no pre-exposure and no blue-noise sampling, and the random numbers freeze without TAA's jitter. There is no upscaler, and the tone curve is a per-channel ACES fit. Checked against the primary talks: Lumen's reflections and MegaLights both leave their last noise to TAA/TSR. So the owner's no-reliance rule is stricter than Epic's shipped design as well, not only idTech8's. What could beat both:; Every signal complete before the anti-aliasing pass, with the reflection added after it. The industry adds it before and filters it twice.; Measured change as the one rule for lighting change, AO included, feeding the AA pass through one generic lane.; History rejection from the predicted previous depth, with a one-texel tolerance instead of a fixed 1-2%.; Filter-adapted noise, as an arm on top of blue noise. Roadmap changes:; RT2-7 splits: 7a decouples the random numbers from the jitter; 7b adopts blue noise (STBN).; A new item before RT2-28 makes every diffuse signal stand alone.; RT2-28 becomes one decided design.; RT2-8 is kept, with its period read from the jitter setting.; RT2-29 gets named steps.; RT2-45 gets separate adaptation speeds and two look arms for the owner.; RT2-2 gets a "signal stands alone" command and runs at native resolution.

**The verification, in its own summary.** Of 21 claims: 11 confirmed (D-C1, C3, C4, C5, C7, C8, C11, C12, C13, C14, C16, C17 all hold, some with small corrections to line numbers or scope). 6 are partly true: D-C2 (the reflection still has a live variance-scaled blur), D-C6 (the sky test is dead, but crossings are reported as object id, not depth), D-C9 (0.56 ms is the Balanced cost; the owner's Quality costs about 0.94 ms moving), D-C10 (GI clamps each ray at trace time), D-C15 (FSR2's separation is an empirical constant, not precision-based) and D-OB1 (RageV matches NRD's documented gradient practice; it is better only than Epic's heuristics, and that is unmeasured). 2 are plausible and need gates: D-BB1 (the weight rides through TAA, so a 'zero diff' is impossible as designed, and prior after-resolve arms were grainier) and D-BB2 (equal to NRD's recommended practice apart from share weighting). 2 are refuted: D-BB3 (NRD already predicts the previous depth with a slope-aware, frustum-scaled threshold) and D-BB4 (MegaLights cites the FAST paper itself for its noise). Five target_design components need changes: the final temporal pass, reflection-after-AA, the diffuse accumulator v2, the noise service and the post chain. Key files: C:/Users/ism19/Code/RageV/RageVEditor/assets/shaders/taa_resolve.rvshader, reflection_composite.rvshader, reflection_accumulate.rvshader, rtgi_trace.rvshader; C:/Users/ism19/Code/RageV/RageV/src/RageV/Renderer/FrameGraphBuilder.cpp, Renderer3D.cpp, AutoExposure.cpp, RenderSettings.h.

### D.1 What the top engines ship

- **NVIDIA RTX stack: NRD (ReBLUR / ReLAX / SIGMA / REFERENCE).** Separate denoisers for each signal, run on demodulated radiance and normalised hit distance, with unjittered matrices.; **ReBLUR stages:** tile classify, pre-blur, temporal accumulation with a per-pixel frame count, history fix, blur, post-blur, then a temporal stabilisation copy.; **Specular reprojection:** uses both surface motion and virtual motion from the hit distance.; **ReLAX:** SVGF-style, with an à-trous filter (5 iterations), a colour-box history clamp and anti-lag.; **SIGMA:** shadow denoiser using the distance to the occluder.; **Optional history-confidence input:** a gradient between last frame's stored radiance and last frame's sample re-shaded this frame.; **SH variants:** keep normal-map detail.; **After NRD:** the output goes to TAA or an upscaler.
  Numbers: RTX 4080 at 1440p:; ReBLUR diffuse+specular: 2.55 ms (2.15 ms in performance mode; 3.40 ms with SH); ReLAX diffuse+specular: 3.20-3.25 ms (4.80 ms with SH); SIGMA shadow: 0.40 ms Memory at 1440p: ReBLUR diffuse+specular 262.6 MB, SIGMA 56.4 MB. Defaults (v4.18):; maxAccumulatedFrameNum 30; maxFastAccumulatedFrameNum 6; maxStabilizedFrameNum 63; historyFixFrameNum 3; disocclusionThreshold 0.01; planeDistanceSensitivity 0.02
  Status: Shipped: Cyberpunk 2077 and Watch Dogs: Legion (per the RTG2 chapter), and Alan Wake 2 as the non-RR path (secondary source). Source available under the NVIDIA RTX SDKs licence.
  Sources: <https://github.com/NVIDIA-RTX/NRD>; <https://github.com/NVIDIA-RTX/NRD/blob/master/README.md>; <https://github.com/NVIDIA-RTX/NRD/blob/master/Include/NRDSettings.h>; <https://link.springer.com/chapter/10.1007/978-1-4842-7185-8_49>; <https://github.com/NVIDIA-RTX/NRD-Sample>
- **NVIDIA DLSS Ray Reconstruction (DLSS 3.5 / 4 / 4.5).** One network that denoises and upscales, replacing both the denoisers and DLSS-SR/TAA.; **Inputs:** noisy colour, diffuse and specular albedo, normal plus roughness, motion, depth; optional specular hit distance or specular motion.; **Optional guides:** colour before transparency, a responsivity mask, a depth-of-field guide.; **Modes:** Performance, Balanced, Quality, Ultra Performance and DLAA (no upscale). No dynamic resolution.; **Placement:** must come before tonemapping, with depth of field after it.; **Sampling:** at least 32 jitter positions are recommended; checkerboarding and screen-space dithered or correlated sampling should be avoided.; **API:** Vulkan is supported (nvpro sample).
  Numbers: Performance mode (half the pixels in), presets D/E, 1080p / 1440p / 4K output:; RTX 5070: 1.50 / 2.58 / 5.49 ms; RTX 5080: 0.91 / 1.51 / 3.17 ms; RTX 4080: 0.97 / 1.66 / 3.62 ms The default/F preset on the RTX 5070 is 1.59 / 2.72 / 5.84 ms. Memory at 1440p: 212-268 MB. The DLAA cost is not published.
  Status: Shipped: Cyberpunk 2077, Alan Wake 2, and Indiana Jones (added by patch, secondary source). DLSS 4.5 RR (second-generation transformer) was reviewed as a small image-quality gain at similar cost (secondary source).
  Sources: <https://github.com/NVIDIA/DLSS/blob/main/doc/DLSS-RR%20Integration%20Guide.pdf>; <https://github.com/nvpro-samples/vk_denoise_dlssrr>; <https://www.kitguru.net/components/graphic-cards/dominic-moass/nvidia-dlss-4-5-ray-reconstruction-analysis-a-welcome-iq-boost/>
- **Unreal Engine 5.x (Lumen, MegaLights, TSR).** - **Lumen:** screen-probe gather with its own temporal and spatial filtering. Reflections use ray reuse between neighbours plus temporal accumulation and a bilateral filter.; **MegaLights:** stochastic direct lighting with spatio-temporal blue noise and its own denoiser; the docs admit blur and ghosting as lighting gets more complex.; **TSR, the final pass:** history rejection by shading, a flicker temporal analysis (period 3 frames, adjusted below 60 Hz), thin-geometry detection, a reprojection field, and history resurrection. On Epic settings the history is kept at 200% screen percentage. It also works as plain AA at 100% or more.; **Translucency:** rendered after depth of field by default, with a dedicated blur.; **Post:** histogram auto-exposure (64 bins, speed in f-stops per second), local exposure (bilateral), Standard (Gaussian mip sum) or Convolution (FFT) bloom, and an ACES-fitted filmic tonemapper (Slope 0.88, Toe 0.55, Shoulder 0.26, WhiteClip 0.04).
  Numbers: TSR: an average of 0.24 ms for history resurrection on consoles at 60% dynamic resolution, which Epic says keeps UE 5.4 at 5.1 cost. The 200% history makes the history update 4x more expensive. Absolute TSR ms and Lumen/MegaLights denoiser ms were not published in the pages fetched.
  Status: Shipped (Fortnite and many UE5 titles).
  Sources: <https://dev.epicgames.com/documentation/unreal-engine/temporal-super-resolution-in-unreal-engine>; <https://dev.epicgames.com/documentation/unreal-engine/thin-geometry-detection-with-temporal-super-resolution>; <https://dev.epicgames.com/documentation/unreal-engine/temporal-super-resolution-frequently-asked-questions-for-unreal-engine>; <https://dev.epicgames.com/documentation/unreal-engine/megalights-in-unreal-engine>; <https://advances.realtimerendering.com/s2025/content/MegaLights_Stochastic_Direct_Lighting_2025.pdf>; <https://advances.realtimerendering.com/s2022/SIGGRAPH2022-Advances-Lumen-Wright%20et%20al.pdf>; <https://www.unrealengine.com/en-US/tech-blog/unreal-engine-5-goes-all-in-on-dynamic-global-illumination-with-lumen>; <https://dev.epicgames.com/documentation/en-us/unreal-engine/auto-exposure-in-unreal-engine>; <https://dev.epicgames.com/documentation/en-us/unreal-engine/bloom-in-unreal-engine>; <https://dev.epicgames.com/documentation/en-us/unreal-engine/color-grading-and-the-filmic-tonemapper-in-unreal-engine>
- **idTech8 (DOOM: The Dark Ages).** - **GI final gather:** 1 ray per pixel at 1/2 or 1/4 resolution, reading caches only; each pixel stores 2-band SH.; **Denoise:** a separable bilateral Gaussian weighted by normal, depth and hit-distance differences.; **Upscale:** a 4-tap Poisson bilateral upscale, then a temporal filter with a per-pixel valid-frame counter (Mattausch 2010), stored as RGB9E5; R11G11B10F is avoided for colour shift.; **TAA:** the talk states that TAA acts as a further temporal filter at the end of the frame.; **RT reflections:** have their own temporal upscale (about 2 ms) and were disabled on consoles.
  Numbers: On a PC with an RTX 4080 at 4K: denoise 0.52 ms, upscale plus temporal 0.445 ms, the whole GI about 1.7 ms. On PS5 at 1440p: denoise 0.266 ms, upscale 0.69 ms.
  Status: Shipped.
  Sources: <https://advances.realtimerendering.com/s2025/content/SOUSA_SIGGRAPH_2025_Final.pdf>; <https://advances.realtimerendering.com/s2025/index.html>
- **Cyberpunk 2077 RT Overdrive (REDengine 4).** ReSTIR DI (RTXDI) plus ReSTIR GI, SHaRC, and the NRD radiance denoisers.; **ReSTIR and the denoiser:** ReSTIR's spatio-temporal reuse lets the denoiser skip its pre-blur, run more conservatively, and use a shorter history and smaller blur.; **Later:** DLSS RR as the alternative path. The final pass is DLSS, FSR, XeSS or TAA.
  Numbers: The NRD2 layout slide says up to 64 frames can be accumulated with linear weights. No ms figures in the course slides.
  Status: Shipped.
  Sources: <https://intro-to-restir.cwyman.org/presentations/2023ReSTIR_Course_Cyberpunk_2077_Integration.pdf>; <https://www.nvidia.com/en-us/geforce/news/cyberpunk-2077-ray-tracing-overdrive-update-launches-april-11/>; <https://link.springer.com/chapter/10.1007/978-1-4842-7185-8_49>
- **Alan Wake 2 (Northlight).** Path tracing with OMM and SER. DLSS RR produces the final image; without RR it falls back to NRD denoisers (per secondary coverage).
  Numbers: Not published in the sources fetched.
  Status: Shipped.
  Sources: <https://www.nvidia.com/en-us/on-demand/session/gdc24-gdc1003/>; <https://www.tomshardware.com/features/alan-wake-2-will-punish-your-gpu>
- **Metro Exodus Enhanced Edition (4A).** Own denoisers, moved from recurrent blur to a nonlinear neighbourhood estimate, with output in SH. Probe-grid multi-bounce with temporal feedback. DLSS 2 for upscaling. An earlier talk recommends feeding the blurred output back as the temporal history.
  Numbers: Not published.
  Status: Shipped.
  Sources: <https://www.4a-games.com.mt/4a-dna/in-depth-technical-dive-into-metro-exodus-pc-enhanced-edition>; <https://developer.download.nvidia.com/video/gputechconf/gtc/2019/presentation/s9985-exploring-ray-traced-future-in-metro-exodus.pdf>
- **Battlefield V (Frostbite).** RT reflections in three steps: 1. A spatial filter that reuses neighbours' rays weighted by the BRDF (after Stachowiak 2015). 2. A temporal filter. 3. An image filter whose kernel comes from a lookup table of angle and roughness.
  Numbers: Spatial 1.45 ms, temporal 0.24 ms, image filter 1.00 ms; the whole reflection pipeline 6.29 ms. The GPU and resolution are not stated in the slide text (RTX 2080 Ti era).
  Status: Shipped.
  Sources: <https://developer.download.nvidia.com/video/gputechconf/gtc/2019/presentation/s91023-it-just-works-ray-traced-reflections-in-battlefield-v.pdf>
- **AMD FidelityFX / FSR (Hybrid Reflections, Denoiser, FSR 2/3/4, Redstone).** - **FFX reflection denoiser:** reproject (either mirror parallax matched by neighbourhood similarity, or surface motion plus distance checks; a 2x2 slow path at edges; an 8x8 average for outliers), then a spatial pass (7x7, 15 taps, guided by variance), then a temporal pass (outlier removal, history clipped to 9x9 Gaussian statistics, blend by sample count).; **FFX shadow denoiser:** tile classification plus 3 edge-avoiding à-trous passes. The docs say TAA can further stabilise the result.; **FSR 2 (MIT):** TAAU with luma-instability detection, locks for thin features, reactive and transparency masks, and a depth-clip test based on depth-precision separation.; **FSR 4:** ML-based, RDNA 4 only (RDNA 3 via SDK 2.3).; **FSR Ray Regeneration:** an ML denoiser at render resolution, decoupled from upscaling. DX12 and RDNA 4 only.
  Numbers: FSR 2 at 4K Quality: 0.7 ms on an RX 7900 XTX. Ray Regeneration on an RX 9070 XT, 540p / 1080p / 1440p:; 4 signals: 1.10 / 4.26 / 8.10 ms; indirect diffuse+specular: 0.87 / 3.37 / 6.52 ms; indirect specular only: 0.71 / 2.63 / 5.34 ms FFX Denoiser: no ms published.
  Status: FSR 2/3/4: shipped widely. Ray Regeneration: shipped in Call of Duty: Black Ops 7 (secondary source). Hybrid Reflections: SDK sample.
  Sources: <https://gpuopen.com/manuals/fidelityfx_sdk/fidelityfx_sdk-page_techniques_denoiser/>; <https://gpuopen.com/manuals/fidelityfx_sdk/samples/hybrid-reflections/>; <https://gpuopen.com/fidelityfx-denoiser/>; <https://github.com/GPUOpen-Effects/FidelityFX-FSR2>; <https://gpuopen.com/amd-fsr-rayregeneration/>; <https://gpuopen.com/learn/amd-fsr-sdk-2-3-blog/>; <https://www.techpowerup.com/review/amd-fsr-4-redstone/>
- **Unity HDRP.** Separate RT denoisers for each effect (temporal plus bilateral). RT reflection temporal filtering was moved to pre-exposed space, and a rejection rule for pixels moving in world space was added. The path tracer uses the OIDN or OptiX plugin (offline-grade). Final pass: TAA, DLSS or FSR. Tonemap choices: Neutral, ACES, custom or external LUT.
  Numbers: Not published.
  Status: Shipped (engine).
  Sources: <https://docs.unity.cn/Packages/com.unity.render-pipelines.high-definition@10.0/changelog/CHANGELOG.html>; <https://docs.unity3d.com/6000.4/Documentation/Manual/com.unity.rendering.denoising.html>
- **Intel XeSS 3.** Super resolution (cross-vendor through a DP4a path on SM 6.4), frame generation and low latency. No ray-reconstruction or denoiser component was found.
  Numbers: Not published in the README.
  Status: Shipped (SR and FG).
  Sources: <https://github.com/intel/xess>
- **Snowdrop (Avatar), Anvil (AC Shadows), EA SEED (GIBS, PICA PICA).** Each has its own RTGI denoisers followed by TAA or an upscaler. Details were not verified: the AC Shadows 2025 talk PDF (14 MB) and other slides could not be fetched.
  Numbers: Not verified.
  Status: Shipped (Avatar, AC Shadows); PICA PICA is a demo.
  Sources: <https://advances.realtimerendering.com/s2025/index.html>

### D.2 The techniques behind them

- **Per-pixel history length counter (age-based blend)** (shipped; shipped in NRD ReBLUR/ReLAX, idTech8, SVGF/A-SVGF, FidelityFX Denoiser; confidence high). Each pixel stores how many frames its history holds (n). The new sample blends in with weight 1/(n+1) up to a cap, so young pixels converge quickly and old ones stay stable. The counter is reset or reduced on disocclusion, low confidence or anti-lag.
  Fixes: Slow convergence after disocclusion, and the one-size-fits-all fixed alpha. Cost: Negligible: one extra channel. idTech8's whole temporal-plus-upscale pass is 0.445 ms at 4K on an RTX 4080 (Sousa, SIGGRAPH 2025). Fails: The cap in frames ties lag to frame rate. NRD suggests seconds x FPS to keep lag constant in time. Without a clamp or anti-lag, a long cap ghosts.
  Leans on TAA or an upscaler: no. This is the signal's own accumulator. Fit with Vulkan ray queries: Fits: a compute pass after the traced pass.
  Sources: <https://github.com/NVIDIA-RTX/NRD/blob/master/Include/NRDSettings.h>; <https://advances.realtimerendering.com/s2025/content/SOUSA_SIGGRAPH_2025_Final.pdf>; <https://research.nvidia.com/publication/2017-07_spatiotemporal-variance-guided-filtering-real-time-reconstruction-path-traced>
- **Geometric disocclusion test (plane distance, normal, depth precision)** (shipped; shipped in NRD, FSR 2, every TAA; confidence high). Reproject to last frame and compare that frame's surface with the one expected:; **NRD:** plane distance divided by view depth, against disocclusionThreshold (1% by default, 2-10x more for PSR on curved surfaces), plus normal and roughness terms.; **FSR 2:** a depth separation derived from depth-buffer precision (Akeley).; **Others:** object IDs. NRD reprojects history with a custom bilinear or bicubic filter that weights each tap by its own occlusion test.
  Fixes: Ghosting from histories that belong to a different surface. Cost: Part of the accumulation pass; not separately published. Fails: A relative-depth fraction still depends on on-screen size at grazing angles and on thin objects (RageV's 2% problem). NRD's own default is also a fixed fraction.
  Leans on TAA or an upscaler: no.  Fit with Vulkan ray queries: Fits.
  Sources: <https://github.com/NVIDIA-RTX/NRD/blob/master/Include/NRDSettings.h>; <https://github.com/GPUOpen-Effects/FidelityFX-FSR2>; <https://github.com/NVIDIA-RTX/NRD/blob/master/README.md>
- **Specular reprojection by virtual motion from hit distance** (shipped; shipped in NRD, FidelityFX Hybrid Reflections, DLSS RR (guide input); confidence high). A reflection is reprojected with the motion of the reflected point: its virtual position lies along the view ray, stretched by the hit distance and corrected for curvature. It is blended with surface-motion reprojection according to a confidence that depends on roughness and parallax. FFX instead picks between mirror parallax (tested by neighbourhood similarity) and surface motion with distance checks. DLSS RR takes specular hit distance or specular motion vectors as optional guides.
  Fixes: Reflections smearing when the camera moves; the only other options are rejecting the history or using a very short one. Cost: Inside the accumulation pass. Fails: Curved reflectors and mixed hits (a rough lobe covers many hit distances). A single hit distance per texel is noisy, so NRD offers hit-distance reconstruction over 3x3 or 5x5.
  Leans on TAA or an upscaler: no.  Fit with Vulkan ray queries: Fits: hit distance is written from the ray query.
  Sources: <https://github.com/NVIDIA-RTX/NRD/blob/master/README.md>; <https://gpuopen.com/manuals/fidelityfx_sdk/fidelityfx_sdk-page_techniques_denoiser/>; <https://github.com/NVIDIA/DLSS/blob/main/doc/DLSS-RR%20Integration%20Guide.pdf>
- **ReBLUR (NRD recurrent blur denoiser)** (public_sdk; shipped in Cyberpunk 2077, Watch Dogs: Legion; confidence high). Passes: tile classify, pre-blur (default radius 30 px diffuse, 50 px specular), temporal accumulation with a per-pixel count (cap 30), history fix for pixels younger than 3 frames (wide strided reconstruction), blur and post-blur whose radius shrinks with history age and follows the hit-distance lobe footprint, then temporal stabilisation (up to 63 frames). Anti-lag compares against luminance sigma. A fast history (6 frames) clamps the slow one at 2 sigma. Firefly suppression is on by default. Input must be demodulated, with normalised hit distance.
  Fixes: One-ray noise in diffuse and specular, with low lag. The per-signal stabilisation removes the need for TAA to finish the job. Cost: RTX 4080 at 1440p: 2.55 ms for diffuse+specular, 2.15 ms in performance mode, 3.40 ms with SH. Memory 262.6 MB at 1440p. Fails: Blotchy low-frequency residue at a short history. Lag on light changes when no confidence input is given. Needs correct demodulation. Default thresholds are hand-set fractions.
  Leans on TAA or an upscaler: partly. It ends in its own stabilisation pass. NRD notes that its temporal part suppresses jitter, and expects TAA or an upscaler afterwards for geometric AA, not for noise. Fit with Vulkan ray queries: Good: compute-only HLSL compiled to SPIR-V, integrated through NRI or directly. Independent of how rays are traced.
  Needs: Unjittered matrices, 2.5D motion, viewZ, normal+roughness, demodulated radiance, normalised hit distance.
  Sources: <https://github.com/NVIDIA-RTX/NRD>; <https://github.com/NVIDIA-RTX/NRD/blob/master/Include/NRDSettings.h>; <https://link.springer.com/chapter/10.1007/978-1-4842-7185-8_49>
- **ReLAX (NRD, SVGF-derived)** (public_sdk; shipped in Cyberpunk 2077 (RTXDI/ReSTIR paths); confidence high). Temporal accumulation (cap 30, fast history 6), then a history clamp to a colour box, a variance estimate made spatially for young pixels (under 3 frames), and a 5-iteration edge-stopping à-trous filter with luminance, normal (lobe fraction 0.5) and depth (0.003) stops. Anti-lag has acceleration and reset. Aimed at ReSTIR-style inputs.
  Fixes: Noise from dense, higher-quality (ReSTIR) input, with sharper results than ReBLUR. Cost: RTX 4080 at 1440p: 3.20-3.25 ms for diffuse+specular, 4.80 ms with SH. Fails: The à-trous passes can leave checker-like patterns and over-blur at low sample counts. Firefly suppression is off by default.
  Leans on TAA or an upscaler: partly. Expects TAA or an upscaler afterwards for AA. Fit with Vulkan ray queries: Good.
  Sources: <https://github.com/NVIDIA-RTX/NRD/blob/master/Include/NRDSettings.h>; <https://github.com/NVIDIA-RTX/NRD>
- **SVGF (Schied 2017)** (paper; shipped in The ancestor of ReLAX and many in-house denoisers; confidence high). Reprojects with a per-pixel history counter and exponential moving average, estimates variance over time (and spatially for young pixels), then runs a 5-level edge-avoiding à-trous filter whose luminance weight is driven by that variance.
  Fixes: 1-spp path-traced noise. Cost: About 10 ms (±15%) at 1080p on 2017 hardware (the paper's claim). Fails: The fixed alpha lags and ghosts. Blur at low history.
  Leans on TAA or an upscaler: partly. The paper ran TAA after the filter. Fit with Vulkan ray queries: Good.
  Sources: <https://research.nvidia.com/publication/2017-07_spatiotemporal-variance-guided-filtering-real-time-reconstruction-path-traced>
- **A-SVGF temporal gradients / NRD history-confidence input** (public_sdk; shipped in NRD (optional input); confidence high). A sparse set of last frame's samples (one per 3x3 stratum) is re-shaded this frame with the same random seed at forward-projected positions. The difference, normalised and bilaterally reconstructed, sets a per-pixel temporal alpha (the maximum over 3x3). NRD accepts such a gradient as IN_DIFF/SPEC_CONFIDENCE, and its README suggests about 1/5 of the render resolution, the previous RNG seed, and spatial blurring of the gradients.
  Fixes: The trade between lag and noise. History is cut only where lighting really changed (a lamp switch, a moving light), not by fixed ramps. Cost: On a Titan Xp at 1080p, the temporal filter takes under 2 ms, and gradient sampling plus reconstruction under 1 ms (Schied 2018). Fails: Needs a coherent primary sample space (the same seed, the same light choice). Changing the light selection registers as a gradient. The paper's version cannot handle stochastic primary visibility. It needs the previous frame's scene to re-shade.
  Leans on TAA or an upscaler: no.  Fit with Vulkan ray queries: Good: re-tracing last frame's sample is an extra ray query in compute. It needs last frame's light and material data, or the current scene as an approximation.
  Sources: <https://cg.ivd.kit.edu/publications/2018/adaptive_temporal_filtering/adaptive_temporal_filtering.pdf>; <https://github.com/NVIDIA-RTX/NRD/blob/master/README.md>
- **History fix (spatial rebuild of young pixels)** (shipped; shipped in NRD, FidelityFX Denoiser; confidence high). For pixels whose history is younger than N frames (NRD: 3), a wide strided spatial filter (base stride 14 px) rebuilds the value from valid neighbours instead of showing the raw sample. FFX and SVGF raise variance or blur for young pixels.
  Fixes: Noise bursts in newly revealed regions (disocclusion trails behind moving objects). Cost: Inside ReBLUR's budget. Fails: Brief blur on revealed regions.
  Leans on TAA or an upscaler: no.  Fit with Vulkan ray queries: Good.
  Sources: <https://github.com/NVIDIA-RTX/NRD/blob/master/Include/NRDSettings.h>; <https://gpuopen.com/manuals/fidelityfx_sdk/fidelityfx_sdk-page_techniques_denoiser/>
- **Fast-history clamp (dual history inside the denoiser)** (shipped; shipped in NRD ReBLUR/ReLAX; confidence medium). Keep a second, short accumulation (about 6 frames) next to the long one. Clamp the long history to the fast history's mean ± k·sigma (NRD: k = 2) so the long history cannot drift from what recent frames show.
  Fixes: Lag and ghosting of long histories, without a neighbourhood clamp on noisy one-ray input (which would re-inject noise). Cost: One extra history; included in the NRD numbers. Fails: With very noisy input the fast history is noisy too, so k must sit on the fast history's own sigma.
  Leans on TAA or an upscaler: no. This moves the ghost control TAA normally does into the signal's own accumulator, which is exactly the owner's rule. Fit with Vulkan ray queries: Good.
  Sources: <https://github.com/NVIDIA-RTX/NRD/blob/master/Include/NRDSettings.h>; <https://github.com/NVIDIA-RTX/NRD/blob/master/README.md>
- **Firefly and outlier suppression before accumulation** (shipped; shipped in NRD, FidelityFX, CoD:AW bloom; confidence high). Bound each new sample relative to its neighbourhood before it enters history:; **NRD:** anti-firefly with a minimum relative scale of 2.; **FFX:** a weight based on similarity to an 8x8 average.; **Bloom (Jimenez):** a Karis average, weight 1/(1+luma), in the first downsample.
  Fixes: Rare bright samples (lamp hits caught by chance on rough surfaces) that would otherwise sit in history for many frames. Cost: NRD: +0-2% for ReBLUR, +7-10% for ReLAX. Fails: Biased darkening of genuine small highlights. It must be applied to the part of the signal that is not handled explicitly (for example the NEE lamp term), or small lights lose energy.
  Leans on TAA or an upscaler: no.  Fit with Vulkan ray queries: Good.
  Sources: <https://github.com/NVIDIA-RTX/NRD/blob/master/README.md>; <https://gpuopen.com/manuals/fidelityfx_sdk/fidelityfx_sdk-page_techniques_denoiser/>; <https://www.iryoku.com/next-generation-post-processing-in-call-of-duty-advanced-warfare/>
- **SIGMA shadow denoiser** (public_sdk; shipped in NRD titles; confidence high). Takes a shadow visibility or pseudo-translucency mask plus the distance to the occluder, with the penumbra estimated from the light size. It filters spatially over a penumbra-sized kernel, then applies a short temporal stabilisation (default 5 frames; 0 turns history off for many-light setups).
  Fixes: Soft-shadow noise from one ray per light, with contact hardening kept. Cost: 0.40 ms at 1440p on an RTX 4080; 56.4 MB at 1440p. Fails: Works per light or per few lights. Many-light stochastic shadows belong in the radiance denoiser instead (the MegaLights and ReSTIR approach).
  Leans on TAA or an upscaler: no.  Fit with Vulkan ray queries: Good.
  Sources: <https://github.com/NVIDIA-RTX/NRD>; <https://github.com/NVIDIA-RTX/NRD/blob/master/README.md>
- **BRDF-weighted spatial ray reuse (stochastic SSR resolve)** (shipped; shipped in Battlefield V, Lumen reflections, FidelityFX SSSR; confidence medium). Each pixel resolves its reflection from neighbours' ray hits, weighted by its own BRDF divided by the neighbour's pdf (ratio estimator). Then a temporal filter and a bilateral image filter sized from a lookup table of roughness and angle.
  Fixes: Glossy-reflection noise at under 1 ray per pixel. Cost: Battlefield V: spatial 1.45 ms, temporal 0.24 ms, image filter 1.00 ms (the whole pipeline 6.29 ms); GPU and resolution not stated in the slide text. Fails: Bias at silhouettes and at contact; halos from bright hits shared with neighbours (RageV's kEmitterShare problem).
  Leans on TAA or an upscaler: partly. Battlefield V ran TAA afterwards. Fit with Vulkan ray queries: Good.
  Sources: <https://developer.download.nvidia.com/video/gputechconf/gtc/2019/presentation/s91023-it-just-works-ray-traced-reflections-in-battlefield-v.pdf>; <https://advances.realtimerendering.com/s2022/SIGGRAPH2022-Advances-Lumen-Wright%20et%20al.pdf>
- **FidelityFX reflection and shadow denoisers** (public_sdk; shipped in FidelityFX SDK samples; some titles (not verified); confidence medium). - **Reflections:** reproject (fast path of mirror parallax or surface motion, slow path of 2x2 interpolation at edges), then a 7x7 variance-guided spatial pass with 15 taps, then a temporal pass that clips history to 9x9 Gaussian statistics and blends by sample count. Variance is boosted early in history.; **Shadows:** tile classification with a disocclusion mask and neighbourhood clamp, then 3 edge-avoiding à-trous passes.
  Fixes: Noise in RT or SSR reflections and single-light shadows. Cost: Not published. Fails: The docs say TAA may be needed to finish.
  Leans on TAA or an upscaler: partly.  Fit with Vulkan ray queries: Good.
  Needs: MIT licence; DX12 and Vulkan.
  Sources: <https://gpuopen.com/manuals/fidelityfx_sdk/fidelityfx_sdk-page_techniques_denoiser/>; <https://gpuopen.com/fidelityfx-denoiser/>
- **DLSS Ray Reconstruction** (shipped; shipped in Cyberpunk 2077, Alan Wake 2, Indiana Jones (patch); confidence high). A transformer network (presets D/E; the older F preset is a CNN) takes noisy colour plus guides and outputs a denoised, anti-aliased and optionally upscaled frame, replacing both the denoisers and DLSS-SR.
  Fixes: Hand-tuned denoiser artefacts (blur, lag, boiling), especially in reflections and at low sample counts. Cost: Performance mode, 1440p output: 2.58 ms on an RTX 5070, 1.51 ms on an RTX 5080, 1.66 ms on an RTX 4080 (preset D/E). DLAA cost not published. 212-268 MB at 1440p. Fails: A black box. Hallucinated detail or an oil-paint look, particle ghosting (needs guides), no dynamic resolution. Depth of field must follow it. Dithered or correlated sampling breaks it.
  Leans on TAA or an upscaler: yes. It is itself the fused denoiser and TAA/upscaler, so the signals have no separate accumulator of their own. This conflicts with the letter of the owner's rule unless adopted as a whole-pipeline replacement. Fit with Vulkan ray queries: Fits: Vulkan supported (nvpro vk_denoise_dlssrr). Independent of pipeline versus ray query. The input must not be pre-denoised.
  Needs: NVIDIA RTX GPU, NGX runtime, driver 580+ for DLSS 4.5 RR.
  Sources: <https://github.com/NVIDIA/DLSS/blob/main/doc/DLSS-RR%20Integration%20Guide.pdf>; <https://github.com/nvpro-samples/vk_denoise_dlssrr>
- **AMD FSR Ray Regeneration** (shipped; shipped in Call of Duty: Black Ops 7 (secondary source); confidence high). An ML denoiser at render resolution, decoupled from upscaling. Signals: direct and indirect diffuse and specular; since v1.2 also AO, specular occlusion and dominant-light visibility; a checkerboard mode.
  Fixes: RT noise on RDNA 4. Cost: RX 9070 XT at 1440p: 8.10 ms for 4 signals, 6.52 ms for indirect only, 5.34 ms for specular only. Fails: Very expensive per ms. DX12 only; RDNA 4 only.
  Leans on TAA or an upscaler: no.  Fit with Vulkan ray queries: Not usable: DX12 and RDNA 4 only.
  Sources: <https://gpuopen.com/amd-fsr-rayregeneration/>; <https://gpuopen.com/learn/amd-fsr-redstone-developers-neural-rendering/>
- **Classic high-quality TAA (Karis 2014)** (shipped; shipped in UE4, most engines; confidence high). - Jitter with a sub-pixel sequence (Halton).; Reproject history by motion.; Constrain history to the current frame's 3x3 neighbourhood box in YCoCg.; Blend at about 0.04-0.1 with luminance weighting (1/(1+luma)) against flicker.; Sharpen or resample the history.
  Fixes: Geometric and shading aliasing, and sub-pixel shimmer. Cost: Not published per title. FSR 2 (a TAAU) takes 0.7 ms at 4K Quality on an RX 7900 XTX, an upper-bound reference. Fails: Ghosting when the box is too wide. Flicker on sub-pixel detail when it is too tight. Blur from bilinear history. Used as a catch-all denoiser, it smears.
  Leans on TAA or an upscaler: yes. It is the TAA. Fit with Vulkan ray queries: Fits.
  Sources: <https://advances.realtimerendering.com/s2014/index.html>; <https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/abs/10.1111/cgf.14018>
- **Variance clipping in YCoCg (Salvi 2016), clip toward centre, velocity dilation, Catmull-Rom history** (shipped; shipped in INSIDE, Uncharted 4, CoD (Filmic SMAA), UE; confidence high). Build the box from the neighbourhood mean ± gamma·sigma (about 1-1.25) instead of min/max, and clip the history along the line toward the centre (INSIDE) rather than clamping per channel. Take motion from the nearest-depth pixel in 3x3 so edges follow the foreground. Fetch history with a 5-tap Catmull-Rom (Filmic SMAA) to avoid blur that builds up over frames.
  Fixes: Ghosting and noise-driven box inflation, edge trails, and blur. Cost: A few texture taps; well under 1 ms at 1440p (not published per title). Fails: Still has to trade flicker on sub-pixel features against ghosting (hence TSR's and FSR's analyses).
  Leans on TAA or an upscaler: yes.  Fit with Vulkan ray queries: Fits.
  Sources: <https://github.com/playdeadgames/temporal>; <https://advances.realtimerendering.com/s2016/index.html>; <https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/abs/10.1111/cgf.14018>
- **Short-history AA (Filmic SMAA T2x)** (shipped; shipped in Call of Duty titles; confidence medium). Morphological edge AA (SMAA) in each frame plus a 2-frame temporal resolve. The history is short, so there is little ghosting and TAA cannot act as a long-memory denoiser.
  Fixes: Ghosting and softness of long TAA. It enforces TAA-only-for-AA by design. Cost: Not published here. Fails: Less supersampling and more residual shimmer. Any noise from the lighting signals shows through, so they must already be stable.
  Leans on TAA or an upscaler: no. This is the purest 'final pass does AA only' design shipped. Fit with Vulkan ray queries: Fits.
  Sources: <https://advances.realtimerendering.com/s2016/index.html>
- **TSR flicker temporal analysis (behaviour-based flicker floor)** (shipped; shipped in UE5 TSR; confidence medium). TSR analyses how each pixel's luminance oscillates over time. Where a pixel keeps swinging with a short period (cvar Period, default 3 frames, from 0 = clearest to 3 = most stable), it relaxes shading rejection so the history can average the swing, accepting some ghosting. It is on by default at High and above. AdjustToFrameRate rescales the period below a frame-rate cap (60 Hz) so the result does not depend on frame rate.
  Fixes: Shimmer on sub-pixel or specular detail that per-frame rejection cannot tell from real change. This is the same problem as RageV's RT2-8. Cost: Not published. Fails: Epic calls it a last resort. It can ghost on real periodic changes (blinking lights).
  Leans on TAA or an upscaler: yes. It lives in the final AA pass, which is allowed under the owner's rule if the pass only anti-aliases. Fit with Vulkan ray queries: Fits.
  Sources: <https://dev.epicgames.com/documentation/unreal-engine/temporal-super-resolution-in-unreal-engine>; <https://dev.epicgames.com/documentation/unreal-engine/thin-geometry-detection-with-temporal-super-resolution>
- **TSR thin-geometry detection** (shipped; shipped in UE 5.4+; confidence medium). Estimates, over time, how often a pixel is covered by thin geometry, spreads the result spatially, detects depth edges and high-contrast lines, and relaxes rejection there (weight cap 0.037). Relaxation is reduced near translucency.
  Fixes: Flicker on foliage, cables and hair that aggressive rejection causes. Cost: Several extra compute passes; ms not published. Fails: Fails on normal-map detail on flat surfaces and on uniform loss of coverage. Epic suggests the flicker analysis as the fallback.
  Leans on TAA or an upscaler: yes.  Fit with Vulkan ray queries: Fits.
  Sources: <https://dev.epicgames.com/documentation/unreal-engine/thin-geometry-detection-with-temporal-super-resolution>
- **FSR 2 luma instability, locks, and reactive/transparency masks** (shipped; shipped in FSR 2/3 titles; confidence medium). - A short luma history detects flicker.; Locks protect thin features from being averaged away.; The app supplies masks: reactive (favour the current frame, for example particles) and transparency and composition (reduce history protection, for example RT reflections).; The depth-clip test uses depth-precision separation.
  Fixes: Ghosting on content without motion vectors, and loss of thin features. Cost: The whole FSR 2 takes 0.7 ms at 4K Quality on an RX 7900 XTX. Fails: The masks are app-authored hints, i.e. per-surface inputs, which is exactly what RT2-28 is removing from RageV's TAA.
  Leans on TAA or an upscaler: yes.  Fit with Vulkan ray queries: Fits (Vulkan backend).
  Needs: MIT licence.
  Sources: <https://github.com/GPUOpen-Effects/FidelityFX-FSR2>
- **Temporal upscaling (TAAU: TSR, FSR 2/3, DLSS-SR, XeSS-SR)** (shipped; shipped in UE5 TSR, FSR, DLSS, XeSS; confidence high). The TAA resolve writes an output-resolution history from jittered lower-resolution input, with a kernel scaled to the input pixel spacing. Jitter is chosen to cover the upscale ratio.
  Fixes: Pixel-count cost: traced passes scale with pixels. Cost: FSR 2: 0.7 ms at 4K Quality on an RX 7900 XTX. DLSS-SR/RR: see tables. XeSS-SR runs cross-vendor through DP4a. Fails: Thin geometry and water glitter lose stability. Every signal's grid must be defined relative to the internal resolution.
  Leans on TAA or an upscaler: yes.  Fit with Vulkan ray queries: Fits.
  Sources: <https://dev.epicgames.com/documentation/unreal-engine/temporal-super-resolution-in-unreal-engine>; <https://github.com/GPUOpen-Effects/FidelityFX-FSR2>; <https://github.com/intel/xess>
- **Pre-exposure of scene colour and histories** (shipped; shipped in UE (pre-exposure), Unity HDRP (RT reflection temporal filter in pre-exposed space); confidence medium). Lighting is written already multiplied by the (previous frame's) exposure. Histories are rescaled when exposure changes, so half-float precision and any luminance weighting (Karis 1/(1+Y)) act on display-relative values.
  Fixes: Brightness-dependent weighting and half-float precision loss in very bright or very dark scenes. This is RT2-28's Compress/Expand point. Cost: Negligible. Fails: Must rescale every history by the exposure ratio, or the image flickers when exposure adapts.
  Leans on TAA or an upscaler: no.  Fit with Vulkan ray queries: Fits.
  Sources: <https://docs.unity.cn/Packages/com.unity.render-pipelines.high-definition@10.0/changelog/CHANGELOG.html>; <https://dev.epicgames.com/documentation/en-us/unreal-engine/auto-exposure-in-unreal-engine>
- **Histogram auto-exposure with frame-rate-independent adaptation, plus local exposure** (shipped; shipped in UE5, HDRP; confidence high). Build a log-luminance histogram (UE: 64 bins). Average between low and high percentiles (UE recommends about 70-80 and 80-95). Clamp to min and max EV100. Adapt at speeds given in f-stops per second, separately up and down. An optional compensation curve maps EV to a bias. Local exposure splits the image into base and detail with a bilateral grid and compresses the base contrast.
  Fixes: Unstable or brightness-dependent exposure, and frame-rate-dependent adaptation. Cost: Not published; typically a few tenths of a ms. Fails: The histogram range must come from the EV limits, or pixels are dropped (RageV's 0.0001 cut-off).
  Leans on TAA or an upscaler: no.  Fit with Vulkan ray queries: Fits.
  Sources: <https://dev.epicgames.com/documentation/en-us/unreal-engine/auto-exposure-in-unreal-engine>
- **Physically based mip-chain bloom (Jimenez 2014)** (shipped; shipped in CoD: Advanced Warfare, UE (threshold -1 = off), many engines; confidence high). Downsample with a 13-tap filter, using a Karis-weighted average on the first step, then upsample with 3x3 tent filters. Blend into the scene as an energy-conserving lerp at a small strength, with no hard threshold: in real HDR only truly bright pixels bloom visibly. UE also offers FFT convolution bloom for cinematics.
  Fixes: Bloom that flickers and has fixed thresholds in absolute units. Cost: Not published here; typically well under 1 ms at 1440p. Fails: Without a threshold, the bloom strength must be small. Fireflies without the Karis average.
  Leans on TAA or an upscaler: no.  Fit with Vulkan ray queries: Fits.
  Sources: <https://www.iryoku.com/next-generation-post-processing-in-call-of-duty-advanced-warfare/>; <https://dev.epicgames.com/documentation/en-us/unreal-engine/bloom-in-unreal-engine>
- **Tonemapping / display transform choices** (shipped; shipped in UE5 (ACES fit), Blender (AgX), Bevy (Tony, not verified here), GT7; confidence medium). - **UE5:** an ACES-fitted filmic curve (Slope 0.88, Toe 0.55, Shoulder 0.26, WhiteClip 0.04).; **Blender 4.0 (default):** AgX, where bright saturated colours move smoothly to white.; **Tony McMapface:** a parameter-free LUT that keeps hue (with a deliberate Bezold-Brücke shift) and adds no contrast or saturation; MIT/Apache.; **Khronos PBR Neutral:** reproduces base colour exactly in mid-tones, compresses highlights with no hue shift; for product viewing.; **GT7 (Polyphony, SIGGRAPH 2025):** a physically based tonemapper with released C++ code.; **Frostbite (Fry 2017):** display mapping in ICtCp (not verified here).
  Fixes: Hue skews (per-channel ACES turns saturated lights orange or yellow) and inconsistency between SDR and HDR. Cost: A LUT or a few ALU; negligible. Fails: A per-channel curve shifts hue. A LUT needs HDR input shaping. The choice is a matter of taste, for the owner's eye.
  Leans on TAA or an upscaler: no.  Fit with Vulkan ray queries: Fits.
  Sources: <https://dev.epicgames.com/documentation/en-us/unreal-engine/color-grading-and-the-filmic-tonemapper-in-unreal-engine>; <https://developer.blender.org/docs/release_notes/4.0/color_management/>; <https://github.com/h3r2tic/tony-mc-mapface>; <https://github.com/KhronosGroup/ToneMapping/blob/main/PBR_Neutral/README.md>; <https://blog.selfshadow.com/publications/s2025-shading-course/>
- **Noise shaped for the accumulator (spatio-temporal blue noise, FAST noise)** (shipped; shipped in UE5 (STBN), FAST: research code; confidence medium). Random numbers for rays come from noise textures optimised so the error left after a known spatial filter (Gaussian) and temporal filter (exponential moving average) is minimal. STBN is used in UE (MegaLights). FAST (EA SEED, I3D 2024) optimises for a given filter pair; BSD-3 licence.
  Fixes: Residual noise at equal ray count; faster convergence of the per-signal accumulators. Cost: A texture fetch per random number. Fails: DLSS RR warns against screen-space dithered or shared patterns; it prefers decorrelated hashes. Using noise tuned to one filter with a different filter loses the gain.
  Leans on TAA or an upscaler: no.  Fit with Vulkan ray queries: Good.
  Sources: <https://github.com/electronicarts/fastnoise>; <https://advances.realtimerendering.com/s2025/content/MegaLights_Stochastic_Direct_Lighting_2025.pdf>
- **Demodulation before denoising** (shipped; shipped in NRD (required), DLSS RR (albedo guides); confidence high). Divide diffuse by albedo and specular by pre-integrated specular reflectance (the environment BRDF) before accumulating and filtering, then multiply back after. Texture detail then never enters history or blur.
  Fixes: Blurred textures and texture-driven history rejection. Cost: Negligible. Fails: Near-zero albedo; metals need the specular albedo path.
  Leans on TAA or an upscaler: no.  Fit with Vulkan ray queries: Fits.
  Sources: <https://github.com/NVIDIA-RTX/NRD/blob/master/README.md>; <https://github.com/NVIDIA/DLSS/blob/main/doc/DLSS-RR%20Integration%20Guide.pdf>
- **Fast à-trous GPU schedule (Dolp, Hanika, Dachsbacher, I3D 2024)** (paper; shipped in not shipped; confidence medium). Permute invocation indices so each wavelet iteration reads undilated taps, improving cache use.
  Fixes: The cost of à-trous (SVGF/ReLAX-style) spatial passes. Cost: 1.3x to 3.8x faster for common wavelet configurations (paper claim; per-GPU ms not captured here). Fails: Only helps à-trous-style filters.
  Leans on TAA or an upscaler: no.  Fit with Vulkan ray queries: Good.
  Sources: <https://cg.ivd.kit.edu/english/fast-svgf.php>; <https://jo.dreggn.org/home/2024_atrous.pdf>

### D.3 The frontier (published, not yet shipped)

- **DLSS 4.5 Ray Reconstruction (second-generation transformer) at DLAA** (NVIDIA DLSS-RR Integration Guide; KitGuru, 2026; evidence moderate). Better stability and detail than NRD plus TAA and than DLSS 4 RR, at similar cost.
  Evidence: NVIDIA publishes only Performance-mode timings (RTX 5070: 2.58 ms at 1440p output). A secondary review measured negligible cost change at 4K on an RTX 5090 and 4090, and a modest gain in image quality. No independent ms at DLAA 2560x1600 on a laptop GPU. Could beat: RageV's own accumulators plus TAA on quality. Probably not on ms at native resolution (the DLAA cost is unknown). Cost: Estimated at 3-5 ms at DLAA 2560x1600 on a 5070 Ti Laptop. This is an extrapolation, not measured. Risk: Vendor lock-in; a black box; fuses AA with denoising, so there is no per-signal accumulator. No dynamic resolution. Depth of field must follow it.
  Sources: <https://github.com/NVIDIA/DLSS/blob/main/doc/DLSS-RR%20Integration%20Guide.pdf>; <https://www.kitguru.net/components/graphic-cards/dominic-moass/nvidia-dlss-4-5-ray-reconstruction-analysis-a-welcome-iq-boost/>
- **Temporal gradients / history confidence (A-SVGF, NRD confidence input)** (Schied, Peters, Dachsbacher, HPG 2018; NRD README, 2018; evidence strong). Cutting history only where the re-shaded sample disagrees gives much less lag and ghosting at equal or better noise than fixed or hand-ramped alphas.
  Evidence: Measured on a Titan Xp at 1080p: the filter takes under 2 ms, and gradient estimation under 1 ms. SSIM against a 1024-spp reference beat SVGF on animated sequences. NRD exposes it as an input and recommends it for responsiveness. Could beat: RageV's hand-set trust tests and memory ramps in the reflection chain (RT2-23's 'one rule' goal), and the TAA change maps. Cost: About 1 ms at 1080p on 2018 hardware for gradients. Likely far less on RTX 50, but not measured. Risk: Needs a stable RNG seed and light choice per pixel across frames, and re-tracing last frame's sample.
  Sources: <https://cg.ivd.kit.edu/publications/2018/adaptive_temporal_filtering/adaptive_temporal_filtering.pdf>; <https://github.com/NVIDIA-RTX/NRD/blob/master/README.md>
- **Joint neural denoise + supersample, vendor-neutral (Intel HPG 2022; AMD I3D 2025)** (Intel/momentsingraphics; AMD GPUOpen, 2025; evidence weak). One small network for denoising and upscaling beats analytic denoisers plus TAA on quality.
  Evidence: Papers and a blog preview only. No inference ms found for either in this pass. AMD's shipped successor (Ray Regeneration) costs 8.1 ms at 1440p for 4 signals on an RX 9070 XT. Could beat: NRD on quality in theory; unlikely on ms at 1600p/60 without tensor-core access. Cost: Not published in what was fetched. Risk: Training data and pipeline; cross-vendor matrix hardware access in Vulkan (VK_NV_cooperative_vector is NVIDIA-only and unratified).
  Sources: <https://momentsingraphics.de/HPG2022.html>; <https://gpuopen.com/learn/neural_supersampling_and_denoising_for_real-time_path_tracing/>; <https://docs.vulkan.org/refpages/latest/refpages/source/VK_NV_cooperative_vector.html>
- **U-Net denoiser inference compiled to Vulkan (denox, PACMCGIT 2026)** (Vulkan U-Net denoising paper and code, 2026; evidence weak). OIDN-class U-Nets run fully on the GPU inside a renderer, faster than TensorRT, by optimising dispatch schedules.
  Evidence: Paper claim; no ms captured here. The network is single-frame, and temporal stability is not addressed. GPLv3. Could beat: Nothing in RageV's frame budget yet. A possible offline or reference denoiser for ground-truth comparison. Cost: Not published in what was fetched. Risk: GPL licence; no temporal stability; the cost of U-Nets at 4 MP.
  Sources: <https://dl.acm.org/doi/10.1145/3820016>; <https://github.com/kistenklaus/denox>
- **Adaptive sampling + pyramidal denoiser instead of super-resolution (Bálint et al.)** (arXiv 2602.08642, 2026; evidence weak). At under 1 spp, learned sample placement plus a pyramidal gather denoiser beats uniform sparse sampling or super-resolution.
  Evidence: arXiv preprint; no GPU ms in the abstract. Could beat: Render-scale plus TAAU (RT2-29) as a way to save ray budget. Cost: Not published. Risk: Research code; needs training.
  Sources: <https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.08642>
- **Filter-adapted spatio-temporal sampling (FAST) / STBN** (EA SEED, 2024; evidence moderate). Noise optimised for the actual spatial Gaussian and temporal EMA gives lower error after filtering at the same sample count.
  Evidence: I3D 2024 paper with code; STBN is used in UE MegaLights. Could beat: White or hash noise feeding RageV's accumulators, at zero extra rays. Cost: A texture fetch. Risk: Conflicts with DLSS RR's sampling guidance if RR is ever the path.
  Sources: <https://github.com/electronicarts/fastnoise>
- **Fast à-trous schedule** (Dolp, Hanika, Dachsbacher, 2024; evidence moderate). Wavelet denoiser passes 1.3-3.8x faster, with the same output.
  Evidence: I3D 2024 paper. Could beat: The ms of any à-trous-style blur in RageV. Cost: Implementation only. Risk: Low.
  Sources: <https://cg.ivd.kit.edu/english/fast-svgf.php>

### D.4 The survey's recommended baseline and candidates

The survey's view before the comparison looked at RageV's code. The roadmap's items took these as their starting baselines where the comparison and verification agreed.

- **Baseline: A separate NRD-class denoiser for each lighting signal (diffuse GI, specular/reflection, direct/emissive, AO, the transmissive layer when RT2-31 lands), each carrying:; a per-pixel history count (blend 1/(n+1), capped in time rather than frames);; a geometric disocclusion test (plane distance with a tolerance from depth precision or the projected texel);; demodulated input;; hit-distance-driven specular reprojection (virtual plus surface motion, chosen by confidence);; a fast-history clamp;; history fix for young pixels;; firefly suppression before accumulation;; a blur whose radius shrinks with history age and follows the lobe footprint;; its own final stabilisation. SIGMA-class for single-light RT shadows. Integrate NRD itself (Vulkan-capable, source available) as a measured reference arm.** Why: This is what Cyberpunk, Watch Dogs: Legion and Alan Wake 2 (non-RR path) ship. It is the only shipped structure that finishes stabilising each signal before TAA, which is the owner's rule. Shipped by: NVIDIA NRD, Cyberpunk 2077, Watch Dogs: Legion, Alan Wake 2 (fallback), idTech8 (simpler form), FidelityFX Denoiser (simpler form). Measured by: For each signal, with TAA switched off:; frame-to-frame per-pixel diff images on a parked camera;; ghost length in texels on the swing/orbit tests;; frames to 90% after a light switch;; check_glint_flicker.py on the bridge. Also GPU ms per pass at 2560x1600 on the 5070 Ti Laptop, against NRD's 2.55 ms (ReBLUR) and 0.40 ms (SIGMA) at 1440p on an RTX 4080, scaled for pixels and GPU. Run an A,B,B,A against an NRD arm on the same inputs.
- **Baseline: A final temporal pass that does AA only: jittered TAA with a YCoCg variance clip (clip toward the centre), velocity from the nearest depth in 3x3, a Catmull-Rom history fetch, pre-exposed input, a disocclusion test from reprojected depth, and no inputs specific to a surface or signal. Flicker handling by behaviour, as in TSR's temporal analysis, with the period in time, not frames.** Why: Karis 2014, Salvi 2016, INSIDE and Filmic SMAA are the shipped recipe. TSR's flicker analysis is the shipped form of RT2-8's behaviour-based floor. Shipped by: UE5 TSR, UE4 TAA, INSIDE, Uncharted 4, CoD Filmic SMAA T2x. Measured by: Edge shimmer and parked edge shake at RT-20's numbers; ghost length on the car's stop; bridge cables unchanged; bridge water at 0.80% or better. TAA cost under 0.5 ms at 2560x1600 (to be measured; FSR 2 takes 0.7 ms at 4K on a 7900 XTX as an upper reference).
- **Baseline: The post chain in shipped general forms:; histogram auto-exposure, with range from the EV limits, percentile metering, and speed in EV per second;; pre-exposure of scene colour and all histories;; Jimenez mip-chain bloom with a Karis-averaged first downsample and an energy-conserving blend, with no threshold or a threshold in exposed units;; a tonemapper chosen by the owner's eye among the UE ACES fit, AgX, Tony McMapface, the GT7 tonemapper and PBR Neutral, preferring a hue-preserving form;; motion blur normalised to a shutter time.** Why: Shipped by UE, HDRP and CoD. It removes every absolute-brightness and frame-rate dependence that RT2-45 lists. Shipped by: UE5, Unity HDRP, CoD: Advanced Warfare, Blender, GT7. Measured by: Bloom at two light scales with matched exposure gives the same picture (diff image). Exposure converges in the same time at 30 and 120 FPS. Diff images of the tonemap candidates, for the owner.
- **Baseline: Upscaling as an option only: own TAAU on the same final pass, with DLSS-SR/FSR behind a setting, and every signal grid relative to the internal resolution.** Why: Every top engine ships it. The owner's target is native. Shipped by: UE5 TSR, FSR 2/3, DLSS, XeSS. Measured by: RT2-29's gates: arms at 0.67 and 0.75 against native with per-pixel diffs, plus glint and cable metrics.
- **Candidate: Temporal-gradient history confidence (A-SVGF style) feeding every accumulator, replacing the hand-set trust ramps and change maps** Why better: One measured rule: history is shortened where the re-shaded old sample disagrees. It is general by construction (no metres, no speeds) and directly serves the owner's general-solutions rule. Evidence: Schied 2018: under 1 ms for gradients at 1080p on a Titan Xp, lower lag and better SSIM than SVGF. NRD recommends it for responsiveness. Risk: Needs a stable per-pixel RNG seed and light choice across frames (the ReSTIR interplay in RT2-12). Measured by: Frames to 90% after a lamp switch; ghost length on the orbit and swing; parked noise (diff images) against the current rules; ms at 2560x1600.
- **Candidate: DLSS Ray Reconstruction at DLAA as a measured arm (not the default)** Why better: May beat hand-built denoisers on reflections and at one ray per pixel. Evidence: Vendor tables (about 2.6 ms at 1440p output in Performance mode on an RTX 5070); secondary reviews. Risk: Breaks the per-signal accumulator rule (fused AA plus denoise), vendor-locked, black box, no dynamic resolution. Needs the owner's ruling. Measured by: ms at DLAA 2560x1600 on the 5070 Ti Laptop; the four-way test; per-pixel diffs against a 16-ray truth.
- **Candidate: FAST / STBN sample noise matched to each accumulator's exponential moving average and spatial kernel** Why better: Lower residual noise at the same rays and the same denoiser. Evidence: FAST I3D 2024; STBN in UE MegaLights. Risk: Incompatible with RR's sampling guidance. Measured by: Parked-camera noise (per-pixel variance after N frames) at equal ms, per signal.
- **Candidate: Fast à-trous schedule for any wavelet blur** Why better: The same output 1.3-3.8x faster. Evidence: Dolp et al., I3D 2024. Risk: Low. Measured by: Bit-identical output and ms of the blur passes.
- **Candidate: Dual (fast/slow) histories inside each signal instead of relying on TAA's neighbourhood box for ghost control** Why better: Keeps ghost control where the noise is understood. The final TAA box can then be tight (AA only). Evidence: NRD defaults (fast 6 frames, clamp at 2 sigma). Risk: Extra memory: one more history per signal. Measured by: Ghost length and parked noise with TAA off against today.

### D.5 RageV against them, topic by topic

- **Per-signal temporal accumulator with a per-pixel history count.** Comparison: equal. Verified: equal. Confidence: high.
  RageV: One shared accumulator for every stochastic signal. frames = min(past.a+1, memory), blend 1/frames, full memory 64 frames, capped by measured change.
