Code: `RageVEditor/assets/shaders/reflection_accumulate.rvshader:1759`, `RageVEditor/assets/shaders/reflection_accumulate.rvshader:1808`, `RageV/src/RageV/Renderer/Renderer3D.h:342-400`, `RageV/src/RageV/Renderer/Renderer3D.h:347`
  Top engines: NRD ReBLUR/ReLAX: per-pixel accumulated-frame count, blend 1/(n+1), cap 30 by default, also in frames. idTech8: a valid-frame counter. MegaLights: accumulated lighting plus moments, with a maximum frame count lowered by confidence. Sources: <https://github.com/NVIDIA-RTX/NRD/blob/master/Include/NRDSettings.h>; `build/rt_research/sources/megalights_2025.txt:794-859`; <https://advances.realtimerendering.com/s2025/content/SOUSA_SIGGRAPH_2025_Final.pdf>
  Why: Same structure. Both cap in frames. RageV's lag on real changes is governed by measured change, not by the cap, so the frame-count cap does not tie lag to frame rate.
- **Detecting real lighting change (temporal gradients / anti-lag).** Comparison: better (claimed). Verified: D-OB1, equal: matches NRD's documented practice; ahead only of Epic's heuristics, unmeasured. Confidence: medium.
  RageV: Measured change: 1 texel in 9 is re-lit with last frame's record and random numbers; the differences are filtered a-trous on the block grid; history is capped at 1/share. Covers direct, GI, reflection and glass reflection. Measured 0.048 ms parked, ~0.56 ms moving at 1600x900. AO has none. Code: `RageVEditor/assets/shaders/change_filter.rvshader:1-111`, `RageVEditor/assets/shaders/include/change_record.glsl:22-35`, `RageV/src/RageV/Renderer/FrameGraphBuilder.cpp:1901-1935`, `RageV/src/RageV/Renderer/FrameGraphBuilder.cpp:2222-2250`, `RageV/src/RageV/Renderer/FrameGraphBuilder.cpp:2526-2556`, `RageV/src/RageV/Renderer/FrameGraphBuilder.cpp:2941-2972`, `RageVEditor/assets/shaders/reflection_accumulate.rvshader:1785-1802`, `docs/RT-MEASURED-CHANGE.md:326-353`
  Top engines: NRD takes an optional history-confidence input (A-SVGF gradient). MegaLights derives confidence from how far the history sits outside a 5x5 variance box. Lumen's screen probes speed up where traces hit fast-moving objects. A-SVGF (Schied 2018) is the published method. Sources: <https://github.com/NVIDIA-RTX/NRD/blob/master/README.md>; `build/rt_research/sources/megalights_2025.txt:835-855`; `build/rt_research/sources/lumen_2022.txt:2572-2592`; <https://cg.ivd.kit.edu/publications/2018/adaptive_temporal_filtering/adaptive_temporal_filtering.pdf>
  Why: RageV ships the published gradient method with measured cost. The shipped Epic paths use rules of thumb that cannot tell grain from change. Gap: AO has no record, so an occluder change is caught only through motion.
- **Accumulator history validity (disocclusion) test.** Comparison: equal. Verified: equal. Confidence: medium.
  RageV: A 3x3 SVGF neighbour search. Exact object-id refusal; facing < 0.8; plane distance < 0.05 m + 1% of eye distance; roughness difference > 0.5; confidence ramps. The change filter uses a 0.1 m plane edge stop. Code: `RageVEditor/assets/shaders/reflection_accumulate.rvshader:1106-1245`, `RageVEditor/assets/shaders/reflection_accumulate.rvshader:1153`, `RageVEditor/assets/shaders/change_filter.rvshader:92`
  Top engines: NRD: plane distance over view depth against a fixed 1% threshold, plus normal and roughness terms, with a bilinear/bicubic fetch that weights each tap by its own occlusion test. FSR2: depth separation derived from depth precision. Sources: <https://github.com/NVIDIA-RTX/NRD/blob/master/Include/NRDSettings.h>; <https://github.com/GPUOpen-Effects/FidelityFX-FSR2>
  Why: RageV's tests are richer (object id, material, per-object bilinear). But its tolerances are in metres, which breaks the general-solutions rule. NRD's form is scale-free but is still a fixed fraction.
- **Fast-history clamp (dual history).** Comparison: missing. Verified: missing. Confidence: medium.
  RageV: None. RT2-0 arm H (a fast-history clamp plus a young fill) was tried and dropped: parked flicker doubled. Code: `docs/RT-SERIES-2.md:416`
  Top engines: NRD: a 6-frame fast history clamps the slow one at 2 sigma. MegaLights has no fast history; it lowers the maximum frame count by distance from a 5x5 variance box. Lumen's screen probes use no neighbourhood clamp at all, only depth and normal rejection. Sources: <https://github.com/NVIDIA-RTX/NRD/blob/master/Include/NRDSettings.h>; `build/rt_research/sources/megalights_2025.txt:826-855`; `build/rt_research/sources/lumen_2022.txt:2572-2592`
  Why: Missing against NRD only; it is not universal. For diffuse signals, measured change already covers lighting change. It stays a reflection (RT2-23) candidate.
- **History fix: spatial rebuild of young or disoccluded pixels.** Comparison: behind. Verified: behind. Confidence: high.
  RageV: YoungRadius = 0 on reflections, direct, AO and GI. Only reflectors moving on their own get MovingRadius 4. The direct light's comment says the temporal average and TAA carry the noise instead. Code: `RageV/src/RageV/Renderer/Renderer3D.cpp:7355`, `RageV/src/RageV/Renderer/Renderer3D.cpp:7373`, `RageV/src/RageV/Renderer/Renderer3D.cpp:7407-7411`, `RageV/src/RageV/Renderer/Renderer3D.cpp:7900`, `RageV/src/RageV/Renderer/Renderer3D.cpp:7922`

### D.6 Candidates that could beat both

- **D-BB1: Every signal is complete before composition, and the traced reflection is added after the anti-aliasing pass (the pass never averages it)**
  Why better than both: Lumen, MegaLights and idTech8 composite before TAA/TSR and rely on it for residual noise and to hide misregistration. That is double filtering with one motion per pixel. RageV today does the same (the reflection composite runs before TAA, which fetches by the surface's motion). Adding the reflection after the pass removes the double filter and the one-motion limit, and makes rule 3 true by construction: switching TAA's memory off cannot change the reflection. Evidence: docs/HANDOFF.md:56-61: with the frame filter's memory off, the floor under a swing was as sharp as settled. REFL-01. reflection_composite.rvshader's header (1-31) already describes the after-filter design. Risk evidence against it: RT-6.1 measured the after-resolve arm grainier (8.86 against 5.98 frame-to-frame change, Renderer3D.cpp:7343-7352), before the reflection had its own history fix and stabilisation. Risk: The reflection's own young-pixel grain shows directly. It needs RT2-23's history fix and stabilisation first. Fog must be carried in the reflection's weight once fog moves into the composition (RT2-33). Cost: About zero: the composite pass already exists and moves in the graph. It removes the reflection motion lane.
  The comparison's gate: At the owner-speed swing (spin_measure.py --swing=15 --rate=2), motion speckle (speckle_count.py >32 and >64) and the pole edges score no worse than the 16-ray reference measured in the same build (today 494 and 8.83). With spin_measure --no-frame-memory, the reflection region's diff image against the memory-on run is zero. Parked: edge_shake.py and parked_stats.py no worse than today. Cost: no increase, A,B,B,A at 2560x1600.
  Verification: **plausible.** 'Rule 3 true by construction' is false as designed: the reflection weight rides in the scene's alpha through TAA (taa_resolve.rvshader:1150, 1170-1171, clamped and averaged). A --no-frame-memory diff of the reflection region cannot be exactly zero unless the weight is computed after the pass. Every measured after-resolve arm so far was grainier. The idTech8 part is unsupported. This needs a measurement gate, not an assumption.
  In the roadmap: RT2-28 step 28c (D23 if it fails).
- **D-BB2: Measured change (A-SVGF gradients) as the one lighting-change rule for every accumulator, AO included, and the only change input to the AA pass, through one lane weighted by each signal's share of the pixel**
  Why better than both: Epic's shipped paths guess change: MegaLights from the distance to a variance box, Lumen from 'traces hit fast movers'. NRD only accepts such an input. RageV measures it by re-lighting with the same random numbers. Weighting each signal's changed share by its share of the pixel (the fix for reflections-s5) gives the AA pass one generic lane instead of two signal-specific maps. Evidence: docs/RT-MEASURED-CHANGE.md:326-353 (0.048 ms parked, 0.56 ms moving at 1600x900); Schied, Peters and Dachsbacher 2018. reflections-s5: TAA restarts whole pixels by one signal's changed share (taa_resolve.rvshader:1066-1083). Risk: The same random-number sample space must hold under camera motion (resampling noise reads as change). The AO record needs last frame's AO rays replayed. Cost: About 0.05 ms parked and about 0.3 ms moving at native for the AO record (by analogy with the measured records).
  The comparison's gate: A lamp switched off: each signal reaches 90% of its new value within 2 frames (emitter_lag.py-style). The wall beside the moving-light drive: the per-pixel diff against a no-history reference falls against today. The parked frame is unchanged (diff 0 where nothing changed). The AO record costs at most 0.05 ms parked at native.
  Verification: **plausible.** Against NRD this is equal practice (NRD recommends exactly this gradient), not beyond it. The new part is the share-weighted single lane into the AA pass, which has not been measured. The AO re-light has no cost measurement, and today's check already costs about 0.94 ms moving at Quality at 1600x900, before AO and before 2560x1600.
  In the roadmap: RT2-47 and RT2-28 step 28d.
- **D-BB3: History rejection by the depth predicted for last frame (a 2.5D motion lane), with a tolerance of one texel's depth span on this surface plus the depth format's own step**
  Why better than both: NRD uses a fixed 1% of view depth, FSR2 a precision-based separation with no slope term, and RageV's TAA a fixed 2% of raw depth and its accumulator 0.05 m + 1%. A prediction removes the camera-translation term. A one-texel tolerance on the surface is scale-free and needs no hand-set fraction, which the general-solutions rule requires. Evidence: The audit's live row: at speed most of the floor and both poles fail kDepthTolerance and show the raw frame (RT-SERIES-2.md:2319). The TAA 2% test is a named RT-24 suspect. taa_resolve.rvshader:414. Risk: Objects moving along the view direction need the previous depth written by the raster: a new lane, and a G-buffer format change (RT2-6, RT2-21). Cost: One extra float per pixel in the velocity lane. The test itself is cheaper than today's 3x3 search, which stays.
  The comparison's gate: spin_measure edges score no worse than the 16-ray reference (8.83 today) at the owner swing. The taa-refusal debug view's depth-refusal count on the poles falls. Parked edge_shake.py at RT-20's numbers. The bridge cable band and check_glint_flicker.py (0.80% or less) unchanged. The four-way test.
  Verification: **refuted.** 'NRD uses a fixed 1% of view depth' is wrong: NRD already uses a predicted previous depth with a slope-aware tolerance scaled by the frustum's size. That is the shipped baseline, not something that beats it. The only real departure is a one-texel tolerance plus the format step in place of NRD's 1% of frustum size / NoV. That is an unmeasured arm, and it should be tested against NRD's form as the baseline.
  In the roadmap: refuted as beyond the baseline; RT2-28 step 28a builds NRD's form first, the one-texel form is an arm.
- **D-BB4: Noise adapted to RageV's own accumulators (FAST), as an arm on top of the STBN baseline**
  Why better than both: STBN (MegaLights' baseline) is optimised for a generic temporal average and a Gaussian. FAST optimises the noise for the actual temporal and spatial filters in use. RageV's filters are known exactly, and their short memory at speed is where the noise pattern matters most. Evidence: FAST, I3D 2024 (code, BSD-3). MegaLights cites Donnelly et al. 2024 for its STBN and uses it engine-wide (megalights_2025.txt:445, 469-471). Risk: RageV's effective memory ranges from 1 to 64 frames, so noise fitted to one filter may lose elsewhere. It conflicts with the DLSS-RR arm's sampling guidance (that arm switches to hashes). Cost: A texture fetch per random number; offline mask generation.
  The comparison's gate: At equal rays, the per-pixel variance after 4 and after 32 frames per signal (--capture-signals, parked) lower than with STBN. The swing grain score and speckle count no worse. Adopted only on both.
  Verification: **refuted.** The premise that MegaLights' baseline is generic STBN and FAST would go beyond it contradicts MegaLights' own citation: Epic cites FAST. At most, 'FAST masks optimised for RageV's own filters' is a tuning arm. RageV's filters are adaptive (variable history length, variance-driven blur, clamps), so 'known exactly' overstates what a fixed-filter optimiser can target. RageV has no STBN baseline yet.
  In the roadmap: refuted as beyond the baseline; RT2-7 step 7b uses the published masks, tuned masks are an arm.

- **D-C1** (confirmed). Claim: The reflection is composited before TAA, and TAA fetches the reflective pixels' history by the surface's motion, so the reflection is temporally filtered twice.
  Evidence: FrameGraphBuilder.cpp:3772-3826 adds the ReflectionComposite pass before the 'TAA resolve' pass (3916+), and TAA reads the composite's lane 1 as its velocity (the ternary on reflectionMotion). In the working tree, reflection_composite.rvshader:130-137 (RT-24, 2026-09-24) sets o_Motion = u_Velocity, the surface's motion, no longer the image motion. So the reflection is averaged once by its accumulator and again by TAA, which fetches by the surface's motion.
  Correction: The line cited (112-120) is the moving-layer subtraction; o_Motion is at about 130-137. The shader's header (1-31) still describes an after-filter design, so it is stale and says the opposite of the code.
- **D-C2** (partly true). Claim: Every signal's young-pixel blur is zero (reflections, direct, AO, GI), and the direct light's comment says TAA carries its noise.
  Evidence: Renderer3D.cpp:7355 (reflection YoungRadius 0), 7407-7411 (direct 0; the comment says 'the temporal average and TAA carry the noise'), 7900 (AO 0), 7922 (GI 0). Water lamps are also 0 (7480). But the reflection still runs a spatial filter by default: MovingRadius = 4 (7373), and FrameGraphBuilder.cpp:1443-1445 builds the three blur passes whenever ReflectionNoiseBlur is on, which is the default (EngineConfig.h:436). That is the uncertainty-scaled blur, and it runs every frame.
  Correction: All the diffuse signals have no young blur, and the direct light's comment does lean on TAA. The reflection does have a spatial history fix: a 4-texel blur scaled by its own variance, on by default, and widened wherever something moves.
- **D-C3** (confirmed). Claim: MegaLights relies on TSR to finish its denoising: a single rotated kernel 'TSR will smooth this out', 'some noise can be handled by TSR', and 'pass signal directly to TSR'.
  Evidence: megalights_2025.txt:861-891: the single sparse kernel is 'rotated per pixel' and relies on TSR to clean up filter holes, and 'some noise can be handled by TSR'. 936-961: the shading-confidence heuristic can 'pass that signal directly to TSR'.
  Correction: The spatial filter runs after the temporal accumulation and is not recurrent. TSR is the last stage, not the only one.
- **D-C4** (confirmed). Claim: Lumen's reflection pipeline ends with full-frame TAA that 'cleans it up a bit more'; its screen-probe temporal filter uses depth+normal rejection with no neighbourhood clamp.
  Evidence: lumen_2022.txt:2716-2747: the reflection pipeline is trace, spatial reuse, temporal accumulation, bilateral filter, then 'the full frame Temporal Anti Aliasing which cleans it up a bit more'. 2572-2592: the screen-probe full-resolution temporal filter rejects on depth and normal and cannot use a neighbourhood clamp. Its fix is faster accumulation where traces hit fast-moving objects.
- **D-C5** (confirmed). Claim: The TAA box is the min/max of the 3x3 (not a variance clip), widened up to 8x on rough dielectrics and by the pixel's own temporal sigma.
  Evidence: taa_resolve.rvshader:949-1001: a min/max 3x3 box in YCoCg (a same-surface subset under RT-6.8), with the history clipped toward the box centre by ClipToBox. :301 kStableWiden = 8, applied at 1137-1143 by (1-metalness) x smoothstep(0.15,0.5,roughness). :1144 widens by kTemporalSigma x temporalSigma.
  Correction: The temporal-sigma widening applies only to still pixels at outlines that are not under glass (1101-1104). It is not general.
- **D-C6** (partly true). Claim: The TAA's 'same surface' test compares raw reverse-Z depth within 2% relative. Its sky test (now.x >= 1.0) is dead because reverse-Z clears to 0, so sky crossings are refused as depth and reported under the wrong reason.
  Evidence: taa_resolve.rvshader:394-402 tests now.x >= 1.0. taa_guide.rvshader:57-60 stores raw G-buffer depth. RHITypes.h:277 sets kDepthClear = 0 (reverse-Z, where far is 0), so the sky has depth 0 and the test fires only at the near plane: it is dead. :414 is a relative 2% test on raw reverse-Z depth. But Matches() checks the object id (:407) BEFORE the depth. Geometry ids are the instance index + 1 (Renderer3D.cpp:9177, 9705 and others), so they are at least 1, and the sky's lane holds the clear value.
  Correction: The sky test is dead. A sky crossing is still refused, but it is reported as kObjectId (the id test comes first), not as kDepth. Sky against sky passes the id, depth and normal tests anyway. What is wrong is the refusal label, not the accept/refuse result.
- **D-C7** (confirmed). Claim: TAA reads velocity at the pixel with no nearest-depth dilation.
  Evidence: taa_resolve.rvshader:861-862: velocity = water lane or texture(u_Velocity, uv), read at the pixel, with no nearest-depth tap.
  Correction: There is a partial substitute: MatchingTexel runs a 9-tap search for a same-surface history texel around the reprojected point (RT-6). It picks the history position, not the motion, so it does not replace velocity dilation at silhouettes.
- **D-C8** (confirmed). Claim: There is no pre-exposure: exposure is applied only in the tonemap, and TAA's Compress/Expand and bloom's weights act on absolute radiance.
  Evidence: tonemap.rvshader:229-232 applies exposure only there. No pre-exposure exists anywhere (grep of src and shaders). taa_resolve.rvshader:783-784 has Compress/Expand = c/(1+Y) on absolute values. bloom_prefilter.rvshader:69-72 (Karis 1/(1+luma)) and 93/144-147 (threshold and clamp, defaults 1.0 and 16.0 in PostSettings.h:331,349) work on absolute radiance.
- **D-C9** (partly true). Claim: Measured change (A-SVGF-style re-light) exists for direct, GI, reflection and glass reflection but not AO, and costs 0.048 ms parked and about 0.56 ms moving at 1600x900.
  Evidence: The re-light and change-filter chains exist for direct (FrameGraphBuilder.cpp:1901-1935), GI (2222-2250), reflection (2526-2556) and glass reflection (2941-2972). No AO change record exists (grep). RT-MEASURED-CHANGE.md:326-353: parked 0.048 ms; moving, 0.91-0.94 ms for all passes at the trace's ray count, and 0.56 ms only with ChangeRays = 1. RenderSettings.h:238-244: Quality and Off use ChangeRays 0, and SampleProject.rvproject sets RtOptimisation: Quality.
  Correction: 0.56 ms moving is the Balanced/Performance cost. In the owner's project (Quality) the check costs about 0.94 ms moving at 1600x900, most of it the GI record (0.55-0.60 ms).
- **D-C10** (partly true). Claim: The firefly clamp runs only in the specular variant; diffuse kinds have no outlier stage.
  Evidence: reflection_accumulate.rvshader:1426-1449: the firefly clamp is inside #if !RV_SIGNAL_PAIR && !RV_SIGNAL_DIFFUSE, so it is specular only in the accumulator. But rtgi_trace.rvshader:371 and 660-666 clamp each GI bounce sample's peak to kIndirectClamp = 4.0 (absolute radiance), and the near-panel term is clamped at about 562-575. gi_denoise.rvshader:181-183 and 242-265 have a firefly bound on the older path.
  Correction: The diffuse accumulators have no outlier stage. GI does have one at the source: a fixed per-ray clamp on absolute radiance (4.0), which is itself a hand-set, brightness-dependent value.
- **D-C11** (confirmed). Claim: The accumulator's plane tolerance is 0.05 m + 1% of the eye distance, and the change filter's edge stop is 0.1 m per stride: tolerances in metres.
  Evidence: reflection_accumulate.rvshader:1153 planeTolerance = 0.05 + 0.01*eyeDistance, repeated at :596 and in pbr_fragment.glsl:6224. change_filter.rvshader:92 plane weight exp(-|dw|/(0.1*step)).
  Correction: The change filter's 0.1 m per stride is a soft exponential weight, not a hard stop. There are three copies of the 0.05 m + 1% tolerance, not one.
- **D-C12** (confirmed). Claim: Auto exposure's SpeedUp and SpeedDown are both set from AutoExposureSpeed.
  Evidence: FrameGraphBuilder.cpp:5406-5407 sets both from post.AutoExposureSpeed. Worse, AutoExposure.cpp:321 reads only params.SpeedUp, so SpeedDown is never used at all.
  Correction: Splitting the setting also needs AutoExposure::Dispatch to pick the rate by direction, which it cannot do today.
- **D-C13** (confirmed). Claim: MegaLights stores its lighting histories in 2xR11G11B10F with stochastic float quantization in pre-exposed space, uses a 5x5 YCoCg variance clip, and lowers the maximum frame count by the distance from the bounds.
  Evidence: megalights_2025.txt:794-859: 2xR11G11B10F lighting with stochastic float quantisation; 'store all lighting values in pre-exposed space'; a 5x5 neighbourhood with variance clipping in YCoCg; confidence = 1 - normalised distance to the bounds, used to reduce the maximum accumulated frames, never to 0.
  Correction: The code snippet shown clamps against Center±Extent, and the frame-count reduction is applied through DiffuseConfidence.
- **D-C14** (confirmed). Claim: MegaLights' denoising costs about 0.96 ms (opaque) plus 0.22 ms (translucency and fog) on PS5 at 1080p, 1 spp. The extracted table is garbled, so the row assignment is inferred.
  Evidence: Verified from word positions on the PDF page (megalights_2025.pdf, the Performance slide), which the extracted text had scrambled: the Denoising row reads 0.96 ms opaque and 0.22 ms translucency + volumetric fog. The row sums give the stated 5.51 ms total.
  Correction: The row assignment is now verified, not inferred. Also, the talk's '2080 lights per pixel' is an extraction error for '20 to 80'.
- **D-C15** (partly true). Claim: FSR2's reconstruct-and-dilate pass takes motion and depth from the nearest-depth tap in the 3x3 and tests against a reconstructed previous depth with a precision-derived separation.
  Evidence: FSR2 ffx_fsr2_reconstruct_dilated_velocity_and_previous_depth.h: finds the nearest depth in the 3x3 and loads the motion from that tap, then reprojects the depth and scatters it into the previous frame's reconstructed depth. ffx_fsr2_depth_clip.h ComputeDepthClip: fRequiredDepthSeparation = Ksep(1.37e-05) x Kfov x viewport diagonal x max(current, previous) view depth.
  Correction: The dilation and the reconstructed previous depth are right. The separation is not derived from precision: it is an undocumented empirical constant scaled by depth, field of view and resolution, with no slope term.
- **D-C16** (confirmed). Claim: NRD defaults: maxAccumulatedFrameNum 30, fast history 6, a 2-sigma clamp, disocclusionThreshold 0.01. Not checked against the source in this pass.
  Evidence: NRDSettings.h (master): maxAccumulatedFrameNum = 30 (max 63), maxFastAccumulatedFrameNum = 6, historyFixFrameNum = 3, fastHistoryClampingSigmaScale = 2.0, disocclusionThreshold = 0.01, disocclusionThresholdAlternate = 0.05. RELAX uses the same values (with a 255 maximum).
  Correction: The 2-sigma clamp clamps the slow history to the fast history's box, not to a spatial neighbourhood.
- **D-C17** (confirmed). Claim: The TAA history costs about 64 bytes per pixel (the RGBA16F colour+moments pair plus the RGBA32F guide pair), about 262 MB at 2560x1600.
  Evidence: FrameGraphBuilder.cpp:3891-3893: a TemporalHistory with RGBA16F colour plus an RGBA16F moments attachment, ping-ponged (16 B x 2). 1664-1667: a TaaGuide RGBA32F pair (16 B x 2). That is 64 B/px, and 2560x1600 x 64 = 262 MB.
- **D-OB1** (partly true). Claim: RageV is BETTER than the top engines at: Detecting real lighting change (temporal gradients / anti-lag). Ours: Measured change: 1 texel in 9 is re-lit with last frame's record and random numbers; the differences are filtered a-trous on the block grid; history is capped at 1/share. Covers direct, GI, reflection and glass reflection. Measured 0.048 ms parked, ~0.56 ms moving at 1600x900. AO has none. Theirs: NRD takes an optional history-confidence input (A-SVGF gradient). MegaLights derives confidence from how far the history sits outside a 5x5 variance box. Lumen's screen probes speed up where traces hit fast-moving objects. A-SVGF (Schied 2018) is the published method. Why: RageV ships the published gradient method with measured cost. The shipped Epic paths use rules of thumb that cannot tell grain from change. Gap: AO has no record, so an occluder change is caught only through motion.
  Evidence: RageV half confirmed: change_record.glsl, change_filter.rvshader, the four relight chains in FrameGraphBuilder, and reflection_accumulate.rvshader:1785-1802 (blendFrames capped at 1/share). AO has none. NRD README: IN_DIFF/SPEC_CONFIDENCE is the gradient between last frame's stored radiance and radiance re-traced with last frame's RNG seed, at reduced resolution and spatially blurred; the README calls this 'essential' and tells users not to rely on the built-in anti-lag. MegaLights (megalights_2025.txt:835-855) and Lumen (2582-2592) are heuristics, as stated.
  Correction: 'Better than the top engines' holds only against Epic's shipped paths. RageV implements the same gradient method that NRD's documentation prescribes as its standard integration, so against NRD it is equal. It was never measured against a variance-box distance heuristic, so 'cannot tell grain from change' is argued, not shown; MegaLights' box does take the local spread into account. At the owner's Quality preset it costs about 0.94 ms moving, not 0.56.
- **D-BB1** (plausible). Claim: Could beat both RageV and the shipped baseline: Every signal is complete before composition, and the traced reflection is added after the anti-aliasing pass (the pass never averages it). Why: Lumen, MegaLights and idTech8 composite before TAA/TSR and rely on it for residual noise and to hide misregistration. That is double filtering with one motion per pixel. RageV today does the same (the reflection composite runs before TAA, which fetches by the surface's motion). Adding the reflection after the pass removes the double filter and the one-motion limit, and makes rule 3 true by construction: switching TAA's memory off cannot change the reflection.
  Evidence: The mechanism holds: the reflection is composited before TAA and fetched by the surface's motion (D-C1). HANDOFF.md:56-58: with the frame filter's memory off, the floor was as sharp as settled. Evidence against: RT-6.1 (Renderer3D.cpp:7343-7352): after-resolve with blur 3 gave 10.70/8.86, against 9.00/5.98 before the resolve with the motion lane. RT-15's moving layer after the resolve was taken out of the default build for grain and speckle (FrameGraphBuilder.cpp:3762-3764; EngineConfig.h:411 ReflectionMovingLayer=false). Lumen and MegaLights compositing before TAA/TSR is confirmed. idTech8 has no source in sources/ or phase 1.
  Correction: 'Rule 3 true by construction' is false as designed: the reflection weight rides in the scene's alpha through TAA (taa_resolve.rvshader:1150, 1170-1171, clamped and averaged). A --no-frame-memory diff of the reflection region cannot be exactly zero unless the weight is computed after the pass. Every measured after-resolve arm so far was grainier. The idTech8 part is unsupported. This needs a measurement gate, not an assumption.
- **D-BB2** (plausible). Claim: Could beat both RageV and the shipped baseline: Measured change (A-SVGF gradients) as the one lighting-change rule for every accumulator, AO included, and the only change input to the AA pass, through one lane weighted by each signal's share of the pixel. Why: Epic's shipped paths guess change: MegaLights from the distance to a variance box, Lumen from 'traces hit fast movers'. NRD only accepts such an input. RageV measures it by re-lighting with the same random numbers. Weighting each signal's changed share by its share of the pixel (the fix for reflections-s5) gives the AA pass one generic lane instead of two signal-specific maps.
  Evidence: taa_resolve.rvshader:1066-1083 confirms that TAA restarts the whole pixel by max(direct.rg, reflection.rg), with no weighting by each signal's share (reflections-s5). The re-light method and its cost are in RT-MEASURED-CHANGE.md. The NRD README prescribes this same gradient as its confidence input.
  Correction: Against NRD this is equal practice (NRD recommends exactly this gradient), not beyond it. The new part is the share-weighted single lane into the AA pass, which has not been measured. The AO re-light has no cost measurement, and today's check already costs about 0.94 ms moving at Quality at 1600x900, before AO and before 2560x1600.
- **D-BB3** (refuted). Claim: Could beat both RageV and the shipped baseline: History rejection by the depth predicted for last frame (a 2.5D motion lane), with a tolerance of one texel's depth span on this surface plus the depth format's own step. Why: NRD uses a fixed 1% of view depth, FSR2 a precision-based separation with no slope term, and RageV's TAA a fixed 2% of raw depth and its accumulator 0.05 m + 1%. A prediction removes the camera-translation term. A one-texel tolerance on the surface is scale-free and needs no hand-set fraction, which the general-solutions rule requires.
  Evidence: NRD REBLUR_TemporalAccumulation.cs.hlsl compares the previous view-Z against Xvprev.z, the current point reprojected into last frame's view, which is a predicted previous depth. The threshold is GetDisocclusionThreshold = frustumSize x saturate(threshold / max(0.05, NoV)) (NRD Shaders/Common.hlsli), with frustumSize = minRectDim x unproject x viewZ. So it has both a prediction and a slope (NoV) term. FSR2 also reconstructs a previous depth.
  Correction: 'NRD uses a fixed 1% of view depth' is wrong: NRD already uses a predicted previous depth with a slope-aware tolerance scaled by the frustum's size. That is the shipped baseline, not something that beats it. The only real departure is a one-texel tolerance plus the format step in place of NRD's 1% of frustum size / NoV. That is an unmeasured arm, and it should be tested against NRD's form as the baseline.
- **D-BB4** (refuted). Claim: Could beat both RageV and the shipped baseline: Noise adapted to RageV's own accumulators (FAST), as an arm on top of the STBN baseline. Why: STBN (MegaLights' baseline) is optimised for a generic temporal average and a Gaussian. FAST optimises the noise for the actual temporal and spatial filters in use. RageV's filters are known exactly, and their short memory at speed is where the noise pattern matters most.
  Evidence: megalights_2025.txt:445 and its reference list (1848) cite 'Donnely et al. 2024 - Filter-Adapted Spatio-Temporal Sampling for Real-Time Rendering, i3D 2024', which is the FAST paper itself, as the source of its denoiser-optimised noise. Grep finds no STBN or blue noise anywhere in RageV's shaders or source.
  Correction: The premise that MegaLights' baseline is generic STBN and FAST would go beyond it contradicts MegaLights' own citation: Epic cites FAST. At most, 'FAST masks optimised for RageV's own filters' is a tuning arm. RageV's filters are adaptive (variable history length, variance-driven blur, clamps), so 'known exactly' overstates what a fixed-filter optimiser can target. RageV has no STBN baseline yet.

### D.8 Design changes the verification required

Each was applied to the roadmap's items on 2026-09-25 (section 9 lists them).

- **Final temporal pass: anti-aliasing only.** Rule 1 is the shipped baseline, not a beats-both item. Implement NRD's form first as the A-arm: predicted previous depth, with tolerance = frustum size x 1% / max(0.05, NoV). Then measure the one-texel + format-step tolerance against it. Drop the claim that FSR2's separation is precision-based (it is an empirical Ksep x fov x resolution x depth term). The sky-test fix should also correct the refusal label (sky crossings report kObjectId today). The 'one generic history-invalid lane' gate must budget for the change check at Quality cost (about 0.94 ms moving at 1600x900), not 0.56 ms. Rests on: D-BB3, D-C15, D-C6, D-C9.
- **Reflection composited after the anti-aliasing pass.** The gate 'reflection region diff with --no-frame-memory = zero' cannot be met while the weight rides through TAA in the scene's alpha (clamped and averaged at taa_resolve.rvshader:1150-1171). Either compute the weight after the pass from unjittered G-buffer data (and accept un-antialiased weight edges), or restate the gate as 'the picture term is unaffected; the weight's edge AA is the only TAA contribution'. List the prior negative evidence (RT-6.1: 8.86 vs 5.98 change; RT-15's moving layer after the resolve removed for grain) as the risk the grain gate must beat. Delete the idTech8 reference. Also note that the reflection keeps its 4-texel variance-scaled blur (on by default), which RT2-23 should subsume rather than ignore. Rests on: D-BB1, D-C2.
- **Diffuse-kind accumulator v2: every diffuse signal stands alone (direct visibility ratio, AO, the GI realtime layer, glass direct, water lamps).** The outlier stage must also replace or reconcile GI's existing per-ray clamp in rtgi_trace (kIndirectClamp = 4.0 absolute radiance, plus the near-panel clamp), which is an unlisted brightness-dependent constant. Otherwise GI ends up with two outlier stages. The cost gate (at most 0.6 ms in the swing for all diffuse signals at 2560x1600) is below today's measured change-check cost at the owner's Quality preset (about 0.94 ms at 1600x900, GI record 0.55-0.60 ms) before AO's re-light is added. Either the gate or the ChangeRays setting under Quality must be revisited with the owner. The 0.05 m + 1% tolerance has three copies (reflection_accumulate.rvshader:596, 1153, pbr_fragment.glsl:6224), and all must move. Rests on: D-C10, D-C9, D-C11.
- **Sample service and noise.** Re-frame FAST: MegaLights cites the FAST paper (Donnelly et al. 2024) for its noise, so FAST's default masks may be the shipped baseline, not a later beats-both arm. Treat 'STBN/FAST masks' as the baseline, and keep only 'masks tuned to RageV's specific filters' as a measured arm. Remove the claim that RageV's filters are known exactly (they are adaptive). Rests on: D-BB4.
- **Post chain on general forms.** 'SpeedUp and SpeedDown as two settings' is not enough: AutoExposure.cpp:321 reads only SpeedUp, so the adaptation must pick SpeedUp or SpeedDown by the direction of the change, or the second setting stays inert. Rests on: D-C12.

### D.9 New findings from the verification

- **D-N1.** reflection_composite.rvshader's header (lines 1-31) still describes the reflection being added AFTER the temporal filter, while the code composites it before TAA and, since RT-24, hands TAA the surface's motion. The header is stale and contradicts the code.
- **D-N2.** AutoExposure::SpeedDown is dead: AutoExposure.cpp:321 uses only params.SpeedUp for the blend rate, so auto exposure adapts symmetrically whatever SpeedDown is set to.
- **D-N3.** GI has a fixed absolute-radiance per-ray clamp (rtgi_trace.rvshader:371 kIndirectClamp = 4.0, plus the near-panel clamp near 562-575). It was missing from the compare phase's 'no outlier stage for diffuse' finding and from the brightness-dependence audit.
- **D-N4.** The owner's SampleProject runs RtOptimisation: Quality, so the measured-change check casts the trace's full ray count (ChangeRays 0). Its moving cost is about 0.94 ms at 1600x900, not the 0.56 ms the compare phase quotes.
- **D-N5.** The reflection's spatial history fix is live by default: the variance-scaled blur (ReflectionNoiseBlur = true, MovingRadius 4) builds three blur passes every frame for specular kinds (FrameGraphBuilder.cpp:1443-1445), even with nothing moving.
- **D-N6.** TAA's change input takes max() over the direct and reflection change maps (taa_resolve.rvshader:1077), so one signal's changed share restarts the whole pixel's history regardless of that signal's share of the pixel. This is the reflections-s5 mechanism, confirmed in the code.
- **D-N7.** NRD's disocclusion test already uses a predicted previous depth (Xvprev.z) and a NoV slope term, with the threshold scaled by the frustum's size: Common.hlsli GetDisocclusionThreshold = frustumSize * saturate(t / max(0.05, NoV)).

### D.10 The comparison's corrections to the survey

- Phase 1 left open whether Lumen and MegaLights rely on TSR to finish their noise removal. The primary texts settle it: they do. Lumen's reflections end with full-frame TAA that 'cleans it up a bit more' (lumen_2022.txt:2744-2747). MegaLights' spatial filter depends on TSR to fill its holes, and it passes some noise straight to TSR (megalights_2025.txt:865, 887-891, 956-961). So the owner's rule 3 is stricter than Epic's shipped design, not only idTech8's.
- Phase 1 said the Lumen and MegaLights denoiser costs were not published. The MegaLights talk's performance table gives denoising at about 0.96 ms (opaque) plus 0.22 ms on PS5, 1080p render resolution, 1 spp (megalights_2025.txt:1537-1560). The row assignment is inferred from a garbled extraction.
- Phase 1 presented idTech8's 'R11G11B10F is avoided for colour shift' as the practice. MegaLights ships its lighting histories in 2xR11G11B10F with stochastic float quantization and pre-exposure, with no issue against a 64-bit reference (megalights_2025.txt:796-826). RageV already has the stochastic rounding (half_float.glsl).
- Phase 1 called FAST 'research code' and credited UE with STBN. The MegaLights talk cites Donnelly et al. 2024 (the FAST authors) for its STBN and says it is used 'all over the engine' (megalights_2025.txt:445, 469-471). Filter-adapted spatiotemporal noise is therefore cited by a shipped engine.
- Phase 1 listed A-SVGF temporal gradients as a frontier candidate for RageV. RageV already runs them as 'measured change' for direct, GI, reflections and the glass reflection (change_filter.rvshader; docs/RT-MEASURED-CHANGE.md), with measured costs. Only AO lacks a record.
- Phase 1 implied a fast-history clamp is the shipped norm. MegaLights instead lowers the maximum frame count by the distance from a 5x5 variance box (megalights_2025.txt:835-855), and Lumen's screen-probe filter uses no neighbourhood clamp at all (lumen_2022.txt:2578-2582). The fast history is NRD's choice, not universal.
- The task names tools/scripts/garage/sharp_study.py as an instrument. No such file exists anywhere in the repository. Floor sharpness is the 'sharp' score inside spin_measure.py's swing_run.
- RT-SERIES-2 RT2-28 says TAA's per-pixel motion 'is chosen by comparing brightness'. RT-24 already changed that: reflection_composite.rvshader:112-120 always writes the surface's motion. Also, the headers of reflection_composite.rvshader (1-31) and reflection_accumulate.rvshader (49-54) still say the reflection is added after the temporal filter, while the code adds it before (FrameGraphBuilder.cpp:3772-3826). taa_resolve.rvshader:1003-1018 says the history fetch is bilinear while 1155-1157 uses Catmull-Rom.

### D.11 Questions left open

As the survey recorded them. Where the verification later read a primary talk the survey could not fetch, the verification's verdict above supersedes the line here.

- DLSS RR cost in DLAA mode at 2560x1600 on an RTX 5070 Ti Laptop: NVIDIA publishes only Performance-mode desktop numbers, so the 3-5 ms estimate is an extrapolation.
- Absolute TSR ms (PS5 or PC) and Lumen and MegaLights denoiser ms and history settings: the SIGGRAPH PDFs are over 10 MB and could not be fetched; the UE docs pages give no ms.
- Whether Lumen, MegaLights and TSR are designed to rely on TSR for final noise removal. Not stated in the docs fetched. idTech8 openly does; NRD and FFX expect TAA afterwards 'for AA' or 'to further stabilise'.
- Denoiser specifics for Assassin's Creed Shadows (14 MB talk), Avatar/Snowdrop, Indiana Jones at launch (NRD or in-house before RR was patched in), and the DOOM: The Dark Ages path-tracing update: not verified.
- Inference ms for Intel's HPG 2022 and AMD's I3D 2025 joint denoise/upscale networks, and for the Vulkan U-Net paper: not in the pages fetched.
- Exact ReBLUR internals beyond the settings header (e.g. how its anti-lag and the confidence between virtual and surface motion are computed): the RTG2 chapter was not read in full.
- Battlefield V reflection timings: the GPU and resolution are not in the slide text.
- The UE5 Motion Blur 'Target FPS' setting (normalising blur to a frame rate): recalled, not verified in the docs fetched.
- ACES 2.0 output transforms, the GT7 tonemapper's exact method (the code URL returned 404), and Bevy's default tonemapper: not verified in this pass.
- The NRD licence's interoperability clause (the fetched summary says NVIDIA RTX SDKs licence; the exact terms for non-NVIDIA hardware need reading before shipping it outside a measured arm).
- The web search budget for the session ran out mid-research. Secondary verification of shipped-title details (Cyberpunk per-mode denoiser choice, Alan Wake 2 fallback) relies on one source each.
- Claims the verification could not settle without a measurement or a source: D-BB1, D-BB2 (each is gated in the roadmap item named above).

## E. Ray-tracing infrastructure and frame architecture

**In short.** RageV picked the right trace primitive (inline ray queries), marks geometry opaque by default and uses its instance masks. Every other layer is behind: the TLAS refits even when its contents change, BLASes are built lazily with a stall and never compacted, passes are full-screen fragment shaders, and the frame has one queue, an ordered-list render graph, no memory budget and no GPU scene. The verified verdicts, with the candidates tied to their items, are in the roadmap's section 11.

**Counts.** Comparison topics: 18 behind, 3 equal, 3 not comparable, 1 better (claimed). Verification of 20 claims: 11 confirmed, 7 partly true, 1 unsupported, 1 plausible.

**The survey, in its own summary.** What the top engines ship here is mostly unglamorous. UE5 rebuilds its TLAS (the top-level ray structure listing every object) every frame. It culls objects from it by distance or by how big they look from the camera. It keeps dynamic BLAS updates (the per-mesh ray structures) inside a triangles-per-frame budget, ordered by priority and with forced rebuilds from time to time. Only BLASes the TLAS actually uses stay in memory. Far scenery beyond 200 m, out to 1 km, is swapped for simplified HLOD stand-ins, and Nanite meshes are traced through simplified fallback meshes. NVIDIA's and AMD's guides say the same things: rebuild the TLAS with the fast-to-trace flag, compact static BLASes (up to about 50% smaller), pool them, build on async compute, and use compute groups of 8x8 or more with swizzled group order when ray queries run in compute. Battlefield V showed where the cost comes from. With no culling and naive BLAS rebuilds, building the structures took 64 ms on the GPU. A 4-degree culling test, staggered refit and rebuild, the fast-build flag, skipping unchanged meshes and overlapping the builds with the G-buffer brought it to 1.15 ms. id Tech (Indiana Jones, Doom: The Dark Ages) is the Vulkan reference. Its path tracer runs in the RT pipeline with SER (TraceMain 24% faster on an RTX 5080) and opacity micromaps (7.90 to 3.58 ms). It compacts even its refit-only vegetation BLASes (1027 to 606 MB), and its whole GI costs about 1.7 ms at 4K on an RTX 4080. Its GI traces probe rays with ray queries and stores packed hit records (a visibility buffer for rays). It then shades those hits in separate indirect compute dispatches, one per shader type, on async compute, which saves about 0.5 ms on consoles. That is the shipped answer to divergent hit shading without the RT pipeline. SER is still ray-generation-only in both Vulkan EXT (Nov 2025) and DXR SM 6.9, so it needs the RT pipeline and cannot help ray queries in compute or fragment shaders. Opacity micromaps do work with ray queries. RTX Mega Geometry (cluster BLAS and partitioned TLAS) has shipped only in Alan Wake 2 (5-20% FPS, 300 MB, NVIDIA's figures). On Vulkan it exists only as NV extensions, and its D3D standard is still a preview (DXR 'Part 2', SM 6.10, Sept 2026). Work graphs are unusable on Vulkan for NVIDIA. Render graphs with pass culling, memory sharing between short-lived targets, batched barriers and async compute (Frostbite FrameGraph, UE RDG, Granite) are standard. id Tech 8 and Nanite both moved to visibility buffers. Nobody has published a fragment-versus-compute ray-query comparison, so RT2-14 is right to measure it.

**The comparison, in its own summary.** Where RageV is better or equal. It uses the right trace primitive: inline ray queries, the choice Lumen 2022 reports as a noticeable speedup on consoles. Its BLAS geometry is marked opaque by default, and only cutouts are forced into the candidate loop. Its static/moving instance masks are actually used, by the bake solve and the subtractive shadow. The survey's claim that the masks are ignored is wrong. It keeps one TLAS per frame shared by every view, with an integrity check. It names a freed BLAS through its GPU address registry before the device faults. It traces the same full-detail geometry the raster draws, so it has none of the LOD-mismatch self-intersection that both Lumen and Snowdrop fight. Where the top engines are better. Every infrastructure layer is behind them:; The TLAS is refit with ALLOW_UPDATE for up to 64 frames, even when the instances inside it changed. It is never skipped, and it is packed from a CPU list every frame.; Static BLASes are built lazily, each with a blocking submit. They are never compacted.; Skinned BLASes are refit forever, one serialized build and barrier each.; There is one entity and one BLAS per material section.; Every ray pass is a full-screen fragment pass. There is no compaction and no indirect dispatch.; Hit shading runs inline and reads textures at mip 0.; The render graph is an ordered list: no barriers, no aliasing, no compute writes.; There is one queue, no memory budget, no pipeline cache and no GPU scene. What beats both: 1. A change-driven TLAS: skipped when nothing changed, fully rebuilt when anything did, written by the GPU. UE rebuilds every frame regardless. 2. Per-texel ray lists that take the accumulators' own history as demand. They are compacted in order and dispatched indirectly. This is RageV's measured-correct per-texel allocation (its fragment version cost 1.0-1.3 ms) combined with Lumen's compaction (up to 50%) and Snowdrop's variable rate (-28%), without Snowdrop's hand-tuned constants. 3. If far-field geometry is ever needed, it goes in a second TLAS. Lumen measured a 44% near-field penalty from its shipped same-TLAS-plus-mask approach and called multiple TLASes the proper fix. Roadmap changes:; RT2-4 gains a correct TLAS skip and an A/B that removes ALLOW_UPDATE.; RT2-14 becomes three measurements, including a visibility-only split of ReflectionTrace, and no longer gates the graph.; RT2-17 and RT2-20 stop porting or migrating passes that M4 rewrites anyway. That saves about 6-9 sessions.; RT2-20 splits into three: the hit library and ports, the ray lists, and gated experiments (async, deferred hit shading, SER, fp16).; RT2-35 gets a decided policy for the TLAS and BLASes.; RT2-38 decides projected-size ray LOD, with far field only as a second TLAS.; RT2-41 gains float-precision-scaled ray offsets and scene-bounds tMax.; A new small item after RT2-11 makes emitter shells opaque again.; Two owner questions: culling tiny instances out of the ray world, and one entity per placed model.

**The verification, in its own summary.** I checked all 19 claims against the working tree and the primary sources, including the BFV and id Tech 8 PDFs fetched and read directly. Eleven are confirmed, E-BB3 is plausible, E-OB1 is unsupported, and six are only partly true: E-C4, E-C8, E-C13, E-C15, E-C16, E-BB1 and E-BB2. The code claims mostly hold:; The TLAS refits whenever its instance count is unchanged, even if contents changed, for up to 64 refits.; The TLAS build is recorded every frame, and the content key is computed after the build.; Static BLASes are built lazily with a blocking submit and are never compacted.; Emitters are forced non-opaque.; Instance masks are used, which contradicts FINDINGS:4392 but agrees with GEO-16.; Shadows off means no rays.; Skinned BLASes are refit every frame and serialized. Corrections to the code claims:; The TLAS is per frame in flight, which lengthens the refit window.; The geometry key changes every frame whenever a skinned mesh exists.; MeasuredChange is on by default.; The lit forward pass still traces rays from raster fragments.; Compute covers more than culling and skinning.; The per-texel RT-9 measurement gave no picture gain, and about 0.55 of its 1.33 ms is the rays themselves, not divergence. Industry corrections:; The Snowdrop -8% applies only to the diffuse lighting pass; the whole-signal saving is 2%.; The BFV 64 -> 1.15 ms figure is TLAS+BLAS combined, and part of it came from culling that accepts popping.; id Tech 8's per-shader async dispatch is for probe/radiance-cache rays, not per-pixel rays.; The Wächter-Binder offset does not cover positions reconstructed from depth. On the verdict and beats-both claims:; E-OB1 (robustness tooling) is unsupported: the check runs only with validation on, and the 'theirs' half is unpublished.; E-BB1 (change-driven TLAS) is partly true. It beats refit and every-frame rebuild only on static frames. It ignores partitioned TLASes at 1M instances, and it misstates the memory point.; E-BB2 (per-texel ray lists) is partly true. It misreads the RT-9 record and overstates its lead over Snowdrop and Lumen.; E-BB3 (second TLAS for the far field) is plausible, but whether current UE already separates the far field is unverified. The target-design components that need changes are the TLAS component, trace execution/ray lists, and large-world precision. The far-field component needs only a check.

### E.1 What the top engines ship

- **Unreal Engine 5.x (Lumen HWRT, MegaLights, Nanite, RDG).** TLAS rebuilt every frame from the instances left after culling (r.RayTracing.Culling mode 3 by default: cull by distance OR solid angle; radius 100 m, angle 1 degree by default); instance list compaction; group-id aggregate culling. Dynamic BLAS: refit budget r.RayTracing.DynamicGeometry.MaxUpdatePrimitivesPerFrame (10k-30k tris suggested), priority by camera distance and frames since update, periodic forced rebuilds; optional tracing feedback so only geometry that rays actually hit gets updated. World Position Offset (vertex animation) is ignored in rays unless opted in, then only within 50 m. Reference-based BLAS residency with a memory pool; 'Ray Tracing Proxies' (2 LODs, LOD1 always resident). Nanite is traced through fallback meshes (Fallback Relative Error); native Nanite RT (r.RayTracing.Nanite.Mode 1) is experimental, and the NvRTX 5.6 branch adds Mega Geometry. Far field: HLOD1 meshes from about 200 m to 1 km under a separate mask, used by Lumen and MegaLights. Budget guidance: at most about 100k instances after culling for 30 FPS on consoles. RDG: setup/execute split, pass culling, transient aliasing allocator, split barriers, async compute passes, parallel recording. Large worlds: doubles on the CPU, shaders in camera-relative 'translated world space'. Lumen targets about 8 ms for GI plus reflections at 1080p on consoles and relies on TSR for 4K output.
  Numbers: about 100k instances in the ray scene after culling as the console 30 FPS guidance; far field 200 m to 1 km; Lumen Epic about 8 ms at 1080p on PS5/XSX; switching Lumen reflections to SSR saves 1 ms on Series S.
  Status: Shipped (engine docs). Native Nanite RT is experimental; Mega Geometry is in the NvRTX branch only.
  Sources: <https://dev.epicgames.com/documentation/unreal-engine/ray-tracing-performance-guide-in-unreal-engine>; <https://docs.unrealengine.com/5.2/en-US/ray-tracing-performance-guide-in-unreal-engine/>; <https://dev.epicgames.com/documentation/en-us/unreal-engine/lumen-technical-details-in-unreal-engine>; <https://dev.epicgames.com/documentation/en-us/unreal-engine/lumen-performance-guide-for-unreal-engine>; <https://dev.epicgames.com/documentation/en-us/unreal-engine/nanite-virtualized-geometry-in-unreal-engine>; <https://dev.epicgames.com/documentation/en-us/unreal-engine/render-dependency-graph-in-unreal-engine>; <https://dev.epicgames.com/documentation/en-us/unreal-engine/large-world-coordinates-rendering-in-unreal-engine-5>; <https://advances.realtimerendering.com/s2022/index.html>
- **id Tech 7/8 (Indiana Jones and the Great Circle, DOOM: The Dark Ages) - Vulkan only.** Path tracing: TraceMain is a ray-generation shader in the RT pipeline, with SER plus reduced live state (what a shader keeps alive across a trace), and opacity micromaps (4-state for shadow rays, forced 2-state for indirect rays; baked offline on the CPU with the NVIDIA OMM SDK; 128 MB OMM budget per map). Shadow rays inside the pipeline use rayQueryEXT with the alpha test done in the proceed loop. Dynamic vegetation BLASes are built with fast build, update and compaction allowed, and are only ever refit, so they stay compacted. Refit is about 10x cheaper than rebuild (Billy Khan). id Tech 8 GI: a world-space cascaded light grid lights the RT world (about 0.1 ms on PC, 0.2 ms on consoles, async); probes trace visibility-only ray queries (64/32/16 rays per probe per frame); each hit is stored as a 128-bit packed record (SBT index, instance, primitive, barycentrics, hitT) and shaded later in an async indirect dispatch per shader type (fewer than 10 types), reusing the raster lighting code; results go to a spatial-hash world cache (14 MB). The final gather is 1 ray per pixel at half or quarter resolution, reading caches only. The GI has its own spatial and temporal filters, then TAA runs at the end as a further temporal filter. BLASes lack the finest LODs, and screen-space directional occlusion restores that detail. Raster side: visibility buffer, compute material dispatch, deferred texturing, tile classification, GPU triangle culling.
  Numbers: Indiana Jones on an RTX 5080 (4K output, 1080p path tracing, 26.9M triangles in the ray structures): OMM took TraceMain from 7.90 to 3.58 ms; SER cut TraceMain by 24% (active threads per warp from 38% to 70%); BLAS compaction took 1027 to 606 MB. Doom: The Dark Ages GI hotspot: RTX 4080 at 4K about 1.7 ms serial (world sampling 0.091, radiance cache 0.109, irradiance volumes 0.07, final gather 0.5, denoise 0.52, upscale 0.445); XSX at 1440p 1.9 ms serial and 1.4 ms with async; PS5 2.05 and 1.55 ms; Series S at 900p 2.11 and 1.71 ms. RT reflections about 2 ms, disabled on consoles.
  Status: Shipped (both games).
  Sources: <https://advances.realtimerendering.com/s2025/content/SOUSA_SIGGRAPH_2025_Final.pdf>; <https://developer.nvidia.com/blog/path-tracing-optimizations-in-indiana-jones-opacity-micromaps-and-compaction-of-dynamic-blass/>; <https://forums.developer.nvidia.com/t/path-tracing-optimization-in-indiana-jones-shader-execution-reordering-and-live-state-reductions/333330>; <https://developer.nvidia.com/blog/how-id-software-used-neural-rendering-and-path-tracing-in-doom-the-dark-ages>; <https://schedule.gdconf.com/session/rip-tear-breaking-down-the-renderingofdoom-the-dark-ages/915274>; <https://gdcvault.com/play/1035744/Rip-Tear-Breaking-Down-the>
- **Frostbite (Battlefield V RT reflections, 2018-19; FrameGraph 2017).** DXR pipeline. Hit groups are generated automatically from the vertex and pixel shaders (derivatives defined away, textures sampled at mip 0), and the payload uses the G-buffer's own layout. Frame layout: variable rate tracing, ray generation, ray binning (rays sorted into direction and screen bins with an atomic count and a prefix sum), a hybrid screen-space pass, intersection plus material, defrag, lighting, spatial, temporal and image filters. TLAS instance culling by projected bounding-sphere angle (4 degrees). BLAS: incremental updates for N frames then a full rebuild, the fast-build flag, skipping rebuilds whose bone matrices did not change, and BLAS updates overlapped with the G-buffer and shadow maps. A separate particle TLAS. FrameGraph: a graph of passes and resources with automatic transitions, fences and transient aliasing, which made async compute simple.
  Numbers: Rotterdam map, unculled: 20,200 TLAS instances and 5000 BLAS rebuilds, 64 ms GPU build. 4-degree culling: 2800 instances and 400 rebuilds, 14.5 ms. Plus staggering, fast build, redundancy skip and overlap: 1.15 ms (RayGen went from 0.71 to 0.81 ms from the staggered refits). Reflection pipeline 6.29 ms total (ray binning 0.15 ms, intersect plus material 1.98 ms). GPU and resolution are not stated in the extracted slides. FrameGraph transient aliasing: 40-50% saving (secondary citation only).
  Status: Shipped.
  Sources: <https://developer.download.nvidia.com/video/gputechconf/gtc/2019/presentation/s91023-it-just-works-ray-traced-reflections-in-battlefield-v.pdf>; <https://gdcvault.com/play/1026282/It-Just-Works-Ray-Traced>; <https://www.gdcvault.com/play/1024612/FrameGraph-Extensible-Rendering-Architecture-in>; <https://www.slideshare.net/DICEStudio/framegraph-extensible-rendering-architecture-in-frostbite>
- **Remedy Northlight (Alan Wake 2).** Path tracing with SER plus opacity micromaps. RTX Mega Geometry (cluster BLAS, partitioned TLAS) was added in patch 1.2.8 (early 2025) and applied to existing assets for speed and memory, not for more detail. Enabled automatically on RTX GPUs when RT is on.
  Numbers: SER plus OMM: RT cost 16.8 to 10.2 ms (Khronos blog; RTX 40-class at 4K, exact GPU per title not stated). Mega Geometry: 5-20% FPS and 300 MB less VRAM (NVIDIA). Secondary: Tom's Hardware on an RTX 4090 at 4K with path tracing measured about +13% and about 1 GB less VRAM.
  Status: Shipped.
  Sources: <https://www.khronos.org/blog/boosting-ray-tracing-performance-with-shader-execution-reordering-introducing-vk-ext-ray-tracing-invocation-reorder>; <https://developer.nvidia.com/blog/?p=113506>; <https://www.tomshardware.com/pc-components/gpus/testing-nvidias-rtx-mega-geometry-tech-vram-reducing-tech-a-leap-forward-for-path-traced-rendering>
- **RED Engine (Cyberpunk 2077 RT Overdrive).** Path tracing with SER, RTXDI and NRD, with DLSS 3 frame generation as the stated performance multiplier. BVH management is not published.
  Numbers: Only DLSS-inclusive FPS was published (e.g. RTX 4090 at 4K, 103 FPS with DLSS 3); no infrastructure costs.
  Status: Shipped.
  Sources: <https://www.nvidia.com/en-us/geforce/news/cyberpunk-2077-ray-tracing-overdrive-update-launches-april-11/>
- **Snowdrop (Avatar: Frontiers of Pandora).** RT GI and reflections as the main lighting path. A custom BVH on consoles and DXR on PC (GDC 2024 'Scene Representation and Custom BVH'). The details could not be extracted: the slides PDF was too large to fetch.
  Numbers: not retrieved
  Status: Shipped.
  Sources: <https://gdcvault.com/play/1034404/Ray-Tracing-in-Snowdrop-Scene>; <https://media.gdcvault.com/gdc2024/Slides/GDC+slide+presentations/Kuenlib_Quentin_Raytracing_In_Snowdrop.pdf>; <https://www.massive.se/blog/games-technology/snowdrops-ray-tracing-shines-a-light-on-pandora/>
- **Unity HDRP.** The TLAS is rebuilt from a per-frame instance list. CullInstances offers sphere culling, solid-angle culling and LOD selection by camera; instance masks are set per ray effect (e.g. reflections vs shadows); any-hit is turned off for opaque materials. The TLAS Build API takes a relative origin (a camera-relative TLAS option).
  Numbers: not published
  Status: Shipped engine features.
  Sources: <https://docs.unity3d.com/ScriptReference/Rendering.RayTracingAccelerationStructure.CullInstances.html>; <https://docs.unity3d.com/ScriptReference/Rendering.RayTracingAccelerationStructure.Build.html>; <https://docs.unity3d.com/Packages/com.unity.render-pipelines.high-definition@17.0/manual/Ray-Tracing-Settings.html>
- **NVIDIA RTX stack (best practices, Mega Geometry, OMM, SER).** TLAS: fast-to-trace flag, rebuild only. Static BLAS: fast-to-trace; compaction up to about 50%; pool BLASes at 256-byte alignment. Dynamic BLAS: refit, skip what is invisible or not moving, rebuild after large deformation, spread rebuilds across frames. Build on async compute during G-buffer or shadow raster. Inline RT in compute: groups of 8x8 or more (16x8 practical), watch the query's register use, swizzle thread-group order for coherence. Use the RT pipeline when hit shading is divergent. Mega Geometry: VK_NV_cluster_acceleration_structure (clusters of up to 256 triangles, cluster templates, GPU-indirect multi-builds; works with ray queries through rayQueryGetIntersectionClusterIdNV) and VK_NV_partitioned_acceleration_structure (rebuilds only the changed partitions; a global partition for movers; per-partition translation for large worlds). Drivers 572.16 and later; works on RTX 20 and newer, with dedicated cluster hardware on RTX 50. Mega Geometry 2.0 (Sept 2026) adds streaming cluster LOD. Displacement micromaps are deprecated in favour of clusters.
  Numbers: Thread-group swizzle: +47% on Battlefield V's denoiser (RTX 2080, 1440p). Mega Geometry: '100x faster' builds (marketing, no setup given). PTLAS and CLAS samples publish no ms.
  Status: Best practices and OMM/SER shipped in many games. Mega Geometry shipped in Alan Wake 2 only; announced for Witcher 4, Control Resonant and Gears E-Day.
  Sources: <https://developer.nvidia.com/blog/best-practices-for-using-nvidia-rtx-ray-tracing-updated/>; <https://developer.nvidia.com/blog/optimizing-compute-shaders-for-l2-locality-using-thread-group-id-swizzling/>; <https://docs.vulkan.org/features/latest/features/proposals/VK_NV_cluster_acceleration_structure.html>; <https://docs.vulkan.org/features/latest/features/proposals/VK_NV_partitioned_acceleration_structure.html>; <https://github.com/KhronosGroup/GLSL/blob/main/extensions/nv/GLSL_NV_cluster_acceleration_structure.txt>; <https://github.com/nvpro-samples/vk_lod_clusters>; <https://github.com/nvpro-samples/vk_partitioned_tlas>; <https://blog.parallax.fyi/four-new-vulkan-samples-for-rtx-mega-geometry/>; <https://www.nvidia.com/en-us/geforce/news/gdc-2026-nvidia-geforce-rtx-announcements/>; <https://videocardz.com/newz/nvidia-rtx-mega-geometry-2-0-adds-geometry-streaming-for-detailed-ray-traced-scenes>; <https://forums.developer.nvidia.com/t/optix-9-0-release/322842>
- **AMD (RDNA guide, FidelityFX, research).** Ray queries from compute on the compute queue give the best RDNA performance; keep one RayQuery object live; rebuild the TLAS every frame on the compute queue; 8x4 groups; avoid needless per-frame BLAS rebuilds. Hybrid reflections: screen-space tracing first, hardware RT for the misses, then denoise. Research: Dense Geometry Format (compressed geometry blocks; hardware support promised for future GPUs). D3D12 work graphs shipped (SM 6.8); the Vulkan version (VK_AMDX_shader_enqueue) is provisional and AMD-only.
  Numbers: Tellusim (2021): ray query and RT pipeline within about 3% on an RTX 3080 at 4K (3.25 vs 3.16 ms); on AMD, ray queries were up to about 65% faster.
  Status: Guides and SDKs are public. DGF is a paper plus SDK. Work graphs on Vulkan are a provisional extension.
  Sources: <https://gpuopen.com/learn/rdna-performance-guide/>; <https://gpuopen.com/fidelityfx-hybrid-reflections/>; <https://gpuopen.com/dgf/>; <https://docs.vulkan.org/refpages/latest/refpages/source/VK_AMDX_shader_enqueue.html>; <https://devblogs.microsoft.com/directx/d3d12-work-graphs/>; <https://tellusim.com/rt-perf/>
- **API standards (Khronos / Microsoft).** SER: VK_EXT_ray_tracing_invocation_reorder (announced Nov 2025, requires VK_KHR_ray_tracing_pipeline; reorder only in ray generation; hitObjectRecordFromQueryEXT turns a ray query's result into a hit object, but only inside RT stages) and DXR SM 6.9 MaybeReorderThread (raygen only; FromRayQuery only in RT stages). OMM: VK_EXT_opacity_micromap (applies to ray queries automatically) and VK_KHR_opacity_micromap (ratified May 2026; not an exact promotion: ray queries must opt in with an execution mode). VK_KHR_ray_tracing_position_fetch (hit triangle positions from the ray structure in ray queries; needs a data-access build flag). VK_EXT_descriptor_heap (heap-based bindless, successor to descriptor_buffer). VK_KHR_device_address_commands (Mar 2026). DXR Part 2 (preview v0.38, 8 Sept 2026, SM 6.10, Tier 2.0): clusters, PTLAS and indirect AS operations, with cluster IDs readable from RayQuery.
  Numbers: SER per Khronos: vk_gltf_renderer +47.8%, Black Myth: Wukong 15.10 to 4.08 ms, Indiana Jones 11-24%, Alan Wake 2 (with OMM) 16.8 to 10.2 ms; RTX 40/50 reorder for real, RTX 20/30 treat it as a no-op.
  Status: SER and OMM are shipped. The cluster and PTLAS standard is a D3D preview only; nothing cross-vendor exists on Vulkan.
  Sources: <https://www.khronos.org/blog/boosting-ray-tracing-performance-with-shader-execution-reordering-introducing-vk-ext-ray-tracing-invocation-reorder>; <https://docs.vulkan.org/refpages/latest/refpages/source/VK_EXT_ray_tracing_invocation_reorder.html>; <https://github.com/KhronosGroup/GLSL/blob/main/extensions/ext/GLSL_EXT_shader_invocation_reorder.txt>; <https://microsoft.github.io/hlsl-specs/proposals/0027-shader-execution-reordering/>; <https://docs.vulkan.org/refpages/latest/refpages/source/VK_EXT_opacity_micromap.html>; <https://docs.vulkan.org/refpages/latest/refpages/source/VK_KHR_opacity_micromap.html>; <https://docs.vulkan.org/refpages/latest/refpages/source/VK_KHR_ray_tracing_position_fetch.html>; <https://docs.vulkan.org/refpages/latest/refpages/source/VK_EXT_descriptor_heap.html>; <https://docs.vulkan.org/refpages/latest/refpages/source/VK_KHR_device_address_commands.html>; <https://microsoft.github.io/DirectX-Specs/d3d/Raytracing2.html>

### E.2 The techniques behind them

- **TLAS full rebuild every frame (fast-to-trace flag), skipped only when nothing changed** (shipped; shipped in Unreal Engine 5, Battlefield V, AMD RDNA guidance, NVIDIA guidance; confidence high). Rebuild the TLAS from the current instance list each frame with the fast-to-trace flag instead of refitting it. A TLAS is small relative to the BLASes, and a refit after instances move or change keeps a degraded tree.
  Fixes: Trace cost slowly degrading after refits whose contents changed; stale bounds. Cost: Instance-count dependent. BFV: 2800 instances plus 400 BLAS rebuilds came to 1.15 ms on the GPU after the optimisations (GPU and resolution not stated). UE guidance: at most about 100k instances after culling for 30 FPS consoles. No published TLAS-only ms for RTX 50. Fails: Cost grows linearly with instance count; at 100k-1M instances, partitioned TLAS or culling is needed.
  Leans on TAA or an upscaler: no. Pure infrastructure. Fit with Vulkan ray queries: Direct fit; ray queries read the TLAS like any other.
  Needs: Standard VK_KHR_acceleration_structure.
  Sources: <https://developer.nvidia.com/blog/best-practices-for-using-nvidia-rtx-ray-tracing-updated/>; <https://gpuopen.com/learn/rdna-performance-guide/>; <https://docs.unrealengine.com/5.2/en-US/ray-tracing-performance-guide-in-unreal-engine/>
- **TLAS instance culling by projected solid angle (plus distance), view-expanded** (shipped; shipped in Battlefield V, Unreal Engine 5, Unity HDRP; confidence high). Leave out instances whose bounding sphere spans less than a threshold angle from the camera (BFV 4 degrees; UE default 1 degree, OR a 100 m radius; Unity offers sphere, solid-angle and LOD tests). Large far objects stay in because their angle stays large.
  Fixes: TLAS build time and dynamic-BLAS update count (BFV: 20k to 2.8k instances, 5000 to 400 BLAS rebuilds, 64 to 14.5 ms). Cost: BFV: 64 ms to 14.5 ms of combined TLAS and BLAS build (GPU not stated). Culling itself is CPU or GPU-trivial. Fails: Popping and missing small off-screen occluders or reflectors (BFV names both). A distance radius in metres breaks the owner's no-world-scale rule; the solid-angle form is scale-invariant.
  Leans on TAA or an upscaler: no. Popping is a temporal discontinuity that no accumulator should be relied on to hide. Fit with Vulkan ray queries: Direct.
  Needs: Per-instance bounds.
  Sources: <https://developer.download.nvidia.com/video/gputechconf/gtc/2019/presentation/s91023-it-just-works-ray-traced-reflections-in-battlefield-v.pdf>; <https://dev.epicgames.com/documentation/unreal-engine/ray-tracing-performance-guide-in-unreal-engine>; <https://docs.unity3d.com/ScriptReference/Rendering.RayTracingAccelerationStructure.CullInstances.html>
- **Static BLAS: build at load under a per-frame budget, fast-to-trace flag, compaction, pooled allocation** (shipped; shipped in Indiana Jones and the Great Circle, Unreal Engine 5; confidence high). Build each static BLAS once with the fast-to-trace and compaction flags, query the compacted size, copy it into a pooled heap (256-byte aligned) and free the original. Spread builds across frames under a budget, on async compute where it helps.
  Fixes: BLAS memory (compaction typically 40-60%), first-use hitches, fragmentation. Cost: Memory: Indiana Jones 1027 to 606 MB of total BLAS (41%) on an RTX 5080. NVIDIA: up to about 50%. Compaction copy cost not published. Fails: A compacted BLAS cannot be rebuilt in place, only refit (Indiana Jones relies on this); the compaction size query needs a round trip, so it is done a frame later.
  Leans on TAA or an upscaler: no. n/a Fit with Vulkan ray queries: Direct.
  Needs: Query pool for compacted size; allocator pooling.
  Sources: <https://developer.nvidia.com/blog/path-tracing-optimizations-in-indiana-jones-opacity-micromaps-and-compaction-of-dynamic-blass/>; <https://developer.nvidia.com/blog/best-practices-for-using-nvidia-rtx-ray-tracing-updated/>
- **Dynamic/skinned BLAS policy: budgeted refits, priority, skip unchanged, periodic rebuild, tracing feedback** (shipped; shipped in Battlefield V, Unreal Engine 5, DOOM: The Dark Ages; confidence high). Refit deforming BLASes rather than rebuilding them (refit is about 10x cheaper per id). Cap the triangles updated per frame (UE suggests 10k-30k), order updates by screen importance and frames since the last update, and skip meshes whose inputs did not change (BFV compares bone matrices: 400 to 50 rebuilds). Rebuild periodically or after large deformation. UE can also skip geometry that no ray touched last frame (tracing feedback).
  Fixes: Unbounded per-frame BLAS cost and refit quality decay. Cost: BFV: the combined optimisations took 14.5 to 1.15 ms, while RayGen rose from 0.71 to 0.81 ms because of the staggered refits (GPU not stated). id: refit up to 10x faster than rebuild. Fails: Budget starvation shows stale geometry (shadows lagging animation). Refit trees degrade, so a rebuild cadence is needed. A priority based on camera distance in metres breaks the owner's scale rule; screen size is the general form.
  Leans on TAA or an upscaler: no. Stale BLAS error shows as lag, not noise. Fit with Vulkan ray queries: Direct.
  Needs: The ALLOW_UPDATE build flag; a change version per mesh.
  Sources: <https://dev.epicgames.com/documentation/unreal-engine/ray-tracing-performance-guide-in-unreal-engine>; <https://developer.download.nvidia.com/video/gputechconf/gtc/2019/presentation/s91023-it-just-works-ray-traced-reflections-in-battlefield-v.pdf>; <https://developer.nvidia.com/blog/how-id-software-used-neural-rendering-and-path-tracing-in-doom-the-dark-ages>
- **BLAS granularity: one multi-geometry BLAS per mesh LOD, merge overlapping, split sparse, instance repeats** (shipped; shipped in NVIDIA guidance, Unreal Engine 5 (segments per mesh); confidence medium). Put all material sections of a mesh LOD in one BLAS as separate geometries; find the material through the geometry index plus a per-instance offset. Merge BLASes whose world boxes overlap heavily; split those with large empty volume; instance repeated meshes.
  Fixes: Traversal cost from overlapping instances; TLAS instance count. Cost: not published (NVIDIA guidance only). Fails: Merging parts that move independently forces rebuilds.
  Leans on TAA or an upscaler: no. n/a Fit with Vulkan ray queries: Direct: rayQueryGetIntersectionGeometryIndexEXT and the instance custom index.
  Needs: Per-geometry material lookup in hit shading.
  Sources: <https://developer.nvidia.com/blog/best-practices-for-using-nvidia-rtx-ray-tracing-updated/>
- **Opacity micromaps (VK_EXT_opacity_micromap / VK_KHR_opacity_micromap)** (shipped; shipped in Indiana Jones and the Great Circle, Alan Wake 2, DOOM: The Dark Ages; confidence high). A baked per-triangle grid of tiny sub-triangles, each marked opaque, transparent or unknown (4-state) or just opaque or transparent (2-state). Traversal resolves known cells in hardware, so the shader alpha test runs only for unknown ones. A forced 2-state flag removes shader alpha tests entirely at approximate accuracy.
  Fixes: The cost of alpha-tested geometry (foliage, fences) in any-hit shaders or ray-query proceed loops. Cost: Indiana Jones TraceMain on an RTX 5080 (4K output, 1080p path tracing): 7.90 to 3.58 ms, with any-hit invocations down 67%. Alan Wake 2 SER plus OMM combined: 16.8 to 10.2 ms. The fast-to-trace flag on micromap builds gave 5%. Memory: 95% of OMMs under 200 kB; budget 128 MB per map. Fails: The 2-state form changes silhouettes (fine for rough indirect rays, not for shadows). Very large OMMs were skipped. Animated alpha is not supported, and in-place geometry deformation needs care.
  Leans on TAA or an upscaler: no. 4-state is exact; 2-state is a deterministic approximation, not noise. Fit with Vulkan ray queries: Good: EXT applies automatically inside the rayQuery proceed loop; KHR needs OpacityMicromapIdKHR declared.
  Needs: Offline or GPU bake (NVIDIA OMM SDK). Hardware acceleration on RTX 40/50. The KHR version needs a shader execution mode for ray queries.
  Sources: <https://developer.nvidia.com/blog/path-tracing-optimizations-in-indiana-jones-opacity-micromaps-and-compaction-of-dynamic-blass/>; <https://docs.vulkan.org/refpages/latest/refpages/source/VK_EXT_opacity_micromap.html>; <https://docs.vulkan.org/refpages/latest/refpages/source/VK_KHR_opacity_micromap.html>; <https://github.com/NVIDIA-RTX/OMM>; <https://www.khronos.org/blog/boosting-ray-tracing-performance-with-shader-execution-reordering-introducing-vk-ext-ray-tracing-invocation-reorder>
- **Shader Execution Reordering (VK_EXT_ray_tracing_invocation_reorder / SM 6.9)** (shipped; shipped in Cyberpunk 2077 RT Overdrive, Alan Wake 2, Indiana Jones and the Great Circle, DOOM: The Dark Ages, Black Myth: Wukong; confidence high). In a ray-generation shader, trace into a hit object (or fill one from a ray query), then call reorderThreadEXT with an optional coherence key. The hardware regroups threads so that similar hits are shaded together.
  Fixes: SIMD divergence in hit shading (materials, shaders) and incoherent memory access after incoherent rays. Cost: Indiana Jones TraceMain -24% on an RTX 5080 (active threads per warp 38% to 70%). Black Myth: Wukong 15.10 to 4.08 ms. vk_gltf_renderer +47.8%. RTX 4090/4070 Ti at 4K per Khronos. The reorder itself is a no-op on RTX 20/30. Fails: Needs the RT pipeline (raygen only), so it cannot help compute or fragment ray queries. Live state carried across the reorder cuts the gain (Indiana Jones had to reduce it). Gains are small when hit shading is uniform.
  Leans on TAA or an upscaler: no. Pure performance. Fit with Vulkan ray queries: Poor for today's inline design: only a raygen shader can reorder. A raygen shader may use ray queries and hitObjectRecordFromQueryEXT, so a pass can move to raygen without closest-hit shaders.
  Needs: VK_KHR_ray_tracing_pipeline plus the EXT, an SBT, and RTX 40/50 for real reordering.
  Sources: <https://www.khronos.org/blog/boosting-ray-tracing-performance-with-shader-execution-reordering-introducing-vk-ext-ray-tracing-invocation-reorder>; <https://github.com/KhronosGroup/GLSL/blob/main/extensions/ext/GLSL_EXT_shader_invocation_reorder.txt>; <https://microsoft.github.io/hlsl-specs/proposals/0027-shader-execution-reordering/>; <https://forums.developer.nvidia.com/t/path-tracing-optimization-in-indiana-jones-shader-execution-reordering-and-live-state-reductions/333330>
- **Deferred hit shading / ray binning in compute (software coherence for inline ray queries)** (shipped; shipped in DOOM: The Dark Ages (GI), Battlefield V (binning); confidence high). Trace visibility only, with small ray-query loops, and write a packed hit record per ray (id Tech 8: 128 bits holding SBT or material index, instance, primitive, barycentrics and hitT). Then bucket the hits by shader or material and shade each bucket with an indirect compute dispatch. BFV's variant sorts rays into direction and screen bins (atomic count plus prefix sum) before tracing.
  Fixes: Divergence and register pressure when material shading sits inside the trace loop; gives SER-like coherence without the RT pipeline, and runs the raster lighting code at hits. Cost: BFV ray binning 0.15 ms of a 6.29 ms reflection pipeline (GPU and resolution not stated). id Tech 8 radiance cache shading about 0.109 ms on an RTX 4080 at 4K for about 20k cache entries per frame; the whole GI is 1.7 ms. A general-purpose A/B against inline shading is not published. Fails: Extra bandwidth for hit records; indirect-dispatch sizing; worth it only when hit shading is expensive and divergent.
  Leans on TAA or an upscaler: no. Changes only how the work is scheduled, not the samples, so noise is unchanged. Fit with Vulkan ray queries: Excellent: built entirely on ray queries in compute.
  Needs: Compute, indirect dispatch, atomics, bindless material access.
  Sources: <https://advances.realtimerendering.com/s2025/content/SOUSA_SIGGRAPH_2025_Final.pdf>; <https://developer.download.nvidia.com/video/gputechconf/gtc/2019/presentation/s91023-it-just-works-ray-traced-reflections-in-battlefield-v.pdf>
- **Inline ray queries in compute with tuned groups and thread-group swizzle, versus RT pipeline, versus fragment** (shipped; shipped in DOOM: The Dark Ages, Unreal Engine 5 (inline Lumen passes), AMD FidelityFX; confidence medium). Trace from compute with 8x8 or 16x8 groups (8x4 on RDNA) and remap group IDs into screen tiles to improve L2 hit rates. Use the RT pipeline where hit shading diverges. Fragment-shader ray queries run in 2x2 quads with no shared memory and no indirect dispatch.
  Fixes: L2 thrash in full-screen trace and denoise passes; lets tile lists, shared-memory filters and async compute be used. Cost: Swizzle: +47% on BFV's denoiser (RTX 2080, 1440p; L2 hit rate 63% to 86%). Ray query vs pipeline: RTX 3080 at 1600x900, 0.60 vs 0.58 ms; at 4K, 3.25 vs 3.16 ms (Tellusim 2021). Fragment vs compute ray queries: not published anywhere found. Fails: Compute has no screen derivatives, so hit textures need an explicit LOD (ray cones).
