Storage-image writes may lose framebuffer compression on some GPUs (unverified). The register cost of the query object limits occupancy.
  Leans on TAA or an upscaler: no. n/a Fit with Vulkan ray queries: Native.
  Needs: A compute pass structure; explicit texture LOD.
  Sources: <https://developer.nvidia.com/blog/best-practices-for-using-nvidia-rtx-ray-tracing-updated/>; <https://developer.nvidia.com/blog/optimizing-compute-shaders-for-l2-locality-using-thread-group-id-swizzling/>; <https://tellusim.com/rt-perf/>; <https://gpuopen.com/learn/rdna-performance-guide/>
- **Render graph with pass culling, transient aliasing, batched/split barriers and async compute** (shipped; shipped in Frostbite, Unreal Engine 5 (RDG), Granite (open source), DOOM: The Dark Ages (async GI); confidence high). Passes declare their reads and writes in a setup phase. A compile step culls unused passes, gives memory to short-lived resources by lifetime, and places batched or split barriers (Granite: signal early, wait late, with VkEvent in-queue and semaphores across queues). Selected passes go to the async compute queue with fork and join fences, and command lists are recorded in parallel.
  Fixes: Per-texture unbatched barriers, VRAM wasted on short-lived targets, idle passes, and the inability to overlap AS builds or compute with raster. Cost: Frostbite: 40-50% transient VRAM saved (secondary citation; the primary slides were not read). No GPU-time numbers published by UE, Frostbite or Granite. Async in id Tech 8 GI: about 0.4-0.5 ms saved on consoles (XSX 1.9 to 1.4 ms); PC not reported. Fails: Cross-queue ownership complexity (Granite uses CONCURRENT sharing). Async overlap gains on desktop are workload-dependent and can be negative when the overlapping passes compete for the same units.
  Leans on TAA or an upscaler: no. n/a Fit with Vulkan ray queries: Needed so the TLAS build is a graph pass with correct AS-read barriers into fragment or compute stages.
  Needs: Timeline semaphores and a compute queue for async.
  Sources: <https://www.gdcvault.com/play/1024612/FrameGraph-Extensible-Rendering-Architecture-in>; <https://dev.epicgames.com/documentation/en-us/unreal-engine/render-dependency-graph-in-unreal-engine>; <https://themaister.net/blog/2017/08/15/render-graphs-and-vulkan-a-deep-dive/>; <https://advances.realtimerendering.com/s2025/content/SOUSA_SIGGRAPH_2025_Final.pdf>
- **Async compute for acceleration-structure builds and ray passes** (shipped; shipped in Battlefield V, DOOM: The Dark Ages, NVIDIA and AMD guidance; confidence medium). Record BLAS refits, compaction and the TLAS build (and independent compute such as cache updates) on a compute queue so they overlap the G-buffer or shadow raster, synchronised with timeline semaphores.
  Fixes: Serial AS build time on the graphics queue. Cost: BFV overlapped BLAS updates with the G-buffer and shadows as part of the 14.5 to 1.15 ms chain (share not isolated). id Tech 8: async saved about 0.5 ms on XSX and PS5 and about 0.4 ms on Series S; PC not reported. RTX laptop: not published. Fails: Contention can slow the raster; barriers and ownership mistakes; gains vanish when the graphics queue is already full.
  Leans on TAA or an upscaler: no. n/a Fit with Vulkan ray queries: Good.
  Needs: A compute queue, timeline semaphores, graph support.
  Sources: <https://developer.nvidia.com/blog/best-practices-for-using-nvidia-rtx-ray-tracing-updated/>; <https://gpuopen.com/learn/rdna-performance-guide/>; <https://developer.download.nvidia.com/video/gputechconf/gtc/2019/presentation/s91023-it-just-works-ray-traced-reflections-in-battlefield-v.pdf>
- **Far-field proxies under their own instance mask** (shipped; shipped in Unreal Engine 5 (Lumen, MegaLights), The Matrix Awakens; confidence high). Simplified stand-ins (UE: World Partition HLOD1) go in the same TLAS under a separate mask. A ray first traces the near field up to the trace distance and only on a miss continues against the far-field mask, out to about 1 km in UE.
  Fixes: Km-scale scenes where tracing full detail far away is too costly or would be culled; avoids empty distant reflections and shadows. Cost: not published (UE docs describe it as a cheaper cost). Fails: Visible seam at the near/far boundary if radii are misaligned (UE warns about this); proxy-versus-raster mismatch; UE's fixed metre distances break the owner's scale rule unless expressed in pixel footprint.
  Leans on TAA or an upscaler: no. n/a Fit with Vulkan ray queries: Direct: a second rayQuery with a different cull mask.
  Needs: Offline proxy generation and a mask bit.
  Sources: <https://dev.epicgames.com/documentation/unreal-engine/ray-tracing-performance-guide-in-unreal-engine>; <https://dev.epicgames.com/documentation/en-us/unreal-engine/lumen-technical-details-in-unreal-engine>
- **Ray-tracing LOD: fallback or proxy meshes and raster/RT LOD matching** (shipped; shipped in Unreal Engine 5, DOOM: The Dark Ages; confidence high). Trace a coarser mesh than the raster draws (UE Nanite fallback mesh, RT proxies; id Tech 8 BLASes lack the finest LODs) and cover the raster-to-ray mismatch with screen traces or screen-space directional occlusion. NVIDIA advises matching the LOD between raster and RT to avoid self-intersection.
  Fixes: BLAS memory and build cost for dense assets. Cost: not published as ms. UE: stripping fallbacks saves 'a lot of memory' (no number). Fails: Self-shadowing and leaks where the ray mesh sits inside or outside the raster surface; needs offsets scaled by the LOD error.
  Leans on TAA or an upscaler: partly. Discrete LOD switches pop. Hiding them with dithering needs accumulation; the owner's rule allows that only inside the signal's own accumulator. Fit with Vulkan ray queries: Direct.
  Needs: An LOD chain; per-instance LOD choice.
  Sources: <https://dev.epicgames.com/documentation/en-us/unreal-engine/nanite-virtualized-geometry-in-unreal-engine>; <https://advances.realtimerendering.com/s2025/content/SOUSA_SIGGRAPH_2025_Final.pdf>; <https://developer.nvidia.com/blog/best-practices-for-using-nvidia-rtx-ray-tracing-updated/>
- **Visibility buffer plus compute material dispatch (the RT-first raster front end)** (shipped; shipped in DOOM: The Dark Ages (hybrid VB), Unreal Engine 5 (Nanite VB, then G-buffer), Call of Duty (secondary); confidence medium). Raster writes only instance and triangle IDs (plus depth). Compute passes then rebuild attributes with analytic derivatives, classify tiles by material and shade. The same material and lighting code can serve ray hits (id Tech 8 reuses its raster lighting loop at ray hits).
  Fixes: G-buffer bandwidth at high resolution; overdraw shading; gives one material path shared by raster pixels and ray hits in compute. Cost: id Tech 8: no split published. Classic numbers (Burns and Hunt 2013; Engel GDC 2016): a much smaller buffer than a fat G-buffer (e.g. 64 MB vs 398 MB at 1080p with 8x MSAA). RTX 50 at 2560x1600: not published. Fails: Material-dispatch cost with many materials; needs analytic derivatives; the MSAA interaction changes.
  Leans on TAA or an upscaler: no. n/a Fit with Vulkan ray queries: Good: the hit-shading library is shared with compute ray passes.
  Needs: Bindless geometry and material access, GPU-driven indices.
  Sources: <https://schedule.gdconf.com/session/rip-tear-breaking-down-the-renderingofdoom-the-dark-ages/915274>; <https://jcgt.org/published/0002/02/04/paper.pdf>; <https://gdcvault.com/play/1023792/4K-Rendering-Breakthrough-The-Filtered>
- **Camera-relative (translated-world) rendering for rays** (shipped; shipped in Unreal Engine 5, Unity HDRP (relative-origin TLAS build); confidence medium). Keep double-precision positions on the CPU and subtract the camera position (UE's PreViewTranslation) before converting to float, so every shader works in small camera-relative numbers. Ray origins and TLAS instance transforms use the same frame (Unity's TLAS Build takes a relative origin; PTLAS offers per-partition translation).
  Fixes: Float jitter, shadow acne and unstable reprojection or cache cells far from the origin. Cost: Translated-world math is the same float cost; UE's double-float shader types are 'substantially' more expensive (no ms). Fails: Every cached world-space structure (histories, hash caches, light grids) must be shifted or defined relative to the view origin; the TLAS must be rebuilt, or its origin kept in steps, when the origin moves.
  Leans on TAA or an upscaler: no. n/a Fit with Vulkan ray queries: Direct: the TLAS is just built in that space.
  Needs: Double precision on the CPU; TLAS written each frame relative to the view origin.
  Sources: <https://dev.epicgames.com/documentation/en-us/unreal-engine/large-world-coordinates-rendering-in-unreal-engine-5>; <https://docs.unity3d.com/ScriptReference/Rendering.RayTracingAccelerationStructure.Build.html>; <https://docs.vulkan.org/features/latest/features/proposals/VK_NV_partitioned_acceleration_structure.html>
- **Memory budgeting and BLAS residency** (shipped; shipped in Unreal Engine 5, Indiana Jones and the Great Circle; confidence high). Read heap budgets each frame (VK_EXT_memory_budget via vmaGetHeapBudgets). Allocate optional resources with the within-budget flag and degrade when refused. Keep only BLASes referenced by the TLAS, inside a pool, and evict the rest (UE). Budget OMMs (Indiana Jones: 128 MB).
  Fixes: Out-of-memory crashes and silent paging on a 12 GB laptop GPU. Cost: Budget query negligible (VMA). BLAS eviction hitches: not published. Fails: Eviction thrash; meshes without LODs stay resident (UE caveat).
  Leans on TAA or an upscaler: no. n/a Fit with Vulkan ray queries: Direct.
  Needs: VK_EXT_memory_budget, VMA.
  Sources: <https://gpuopen-librariesandsdks.github.io/VulkanMemoryAllocator/html/staying_within_budget.html>; <https://dev.epicgames.com/documentation/unreal-engine/ray-tracing-performance-guide-in-unreal-engine>; <https://developer.nvidia.com/blog/path-tracing-optimizations-in-indiana-jones-opacity-micromaps-and-compaction-of-dynamic-blass/>
- **RTX Mega Geometry: cluster acceleration structures (VK_NV_cluster_acceleration_structure)** (shipped; shipped in Alan Wake 2; confidence medium). Meshes are pre-split into clusters (up to 256 triangles and 256 vertices each). Small cluster structures (CLAS), or instantiated templates, are built by GPU-indirect multi-builds, and a cluster BLAS is assembled from references to them. Animation and LOD only rebuild the affected clusters.
  Fixes: BLAS build time for skinned, animated, tessellated or cluster-LOD geometry; BLAS memory. Cost: Alan Wake 2: 5-20% FPS and 300 MB (NVIDIA; conditions not stated); Tom's Hardware RTX 4090 at 4K with path tracing: +13%, about 1 GB (secondary). The samples publish no ms. Fails: NVIDIA-only on Vulkan; trace speed can drop when background clusters overlap primary objects (vk_lod_clusters note); few out-of-memory checks in the samples.
  Leans on TAA or an upscaler: no. n/a Fit with Vulkan ray queries: Yes: cluster BLASes go in an ordinary TLAS, and rayQueryGetIntersectionClusterIdNV exists.
  Needs: Driver 572.16 or later; RTX 20 or newer (dedicated cluster hardware on RTX 50); meshoptimizer or nv_cluster_builder preprocessing.
  Sources: <https://docs.vulkan.org/features/latest/features/proposals/VK_NV_cluster_acceleration_structure.html>; <https://docs.vulkan.org/refpages/latest/refpages/source/VK_NV_cluster_acceleration_structure.html>; <https://github.com/KhronosGroup/GLSL/blob/main/extensions/nv/GLSL_NV_cluster_acceleration_structure.txt>; <https://github.com/nvpro-samples/vk_animated_clusters>; <https://developer.nvidia.com/blog/?p=113506>
- **Partitioned TLAS (VK_NV_partitioned_acceleration_structure)** (public_sdk; shipped in Alan Wake 2 (as part of Mega Geometry, per NVIDIA); confidence medium). Instances are written into partitions. Only partitions with changed instances are rebuilt and then combined; a 'global partition' holds frequently moving instances; partitions can carry a translation to help precision.
  Fixes: TLAS rebuild cost that grows with total instances even when few change (100k to 1M-instance scenes, foliage). Cost: Not published as ms. Sample: 1.2M static plus 170k dynamic instances; regular TLAS time stays constant while PTLAS time scales with the partitions touched. Fails: Overlapping partitions slow traces; the global partition speeds updates but slows traces; NVIDIA-only on Vulkan (D3D standard in preview).
  Leans on TAA or an upscaler: no. n/a Fit with Vulkan ray queries: Yes: traced like a regular TLAS.
  Needs: NV driver; partition layout policy.
  Sources: <https://docs.vulkan.org/features/latest/features/proposals/VK_NV_partitioned_acceleration_structure.html>; <https://github.com/nvpro-samples/vk_partitioned_tlas>; <https://microsoft.github.io/DirectX-Specs/d3d/Raytracing2.html>
- **Stochastic LOD transitions for rays (instance/ray masks)** (demo; shipped in not shipped; confidence high). Put two adjacent LODs in the TLAS with masks whose bit counts are proportional to the blend factor; each ray picks a random mask bit, so neighbouring rays see different LODs and the transition dissolves over samples.
  Fixes: LOD popping in rays. Cost: RTX 2080 Ti: discrete LOD up to 2.2x faster than no LOD; the stochastic version adds about 10% (divergence) plus a larger TLAS. Fails: Dithering noise that must be averaged.
  Leans on TAA or an upscaler: yes. Relies on frame accumulation. Acceptable to the owner only if each signal's own accumulator absorbs it; not if TAA is what hides it. Fit with Vulkan ray queries: Direct (cullMask on rayQueryInitialize).
  Needs: 8-bit masks (competes with other mask uses).
  Sources: <https://developer.nvidia.com/blog/implementing-stochastic-lod-with-microsoft-dxr/>
- **Hit-triangle position fetch (VK_KHR_ray_tracing_position_fetch)** (public_sdk; shipped in not shipped; confidence medium). Read the hit triangle's object-space vertices directly from the ray structure inside a ray query.
  Fixes: Removes the need for separate vertex-buffer lookups (or copies) to get geometric normals and positions at hits. Cost: not published Fails: Needs the data-access build flag (may cost BLAS memory); triangles only; object space only.
  Leans on TAA or an upscaler: no. n/a Fit with Vulkan ray queries: Direct.
  Needs: KHR extension (ratified 2023).
  Sources: <https://docs.vulkan.org/refpages/latest/refpages/source/VK_KHR_ray_tracing_position_fetch.html>

### E.3 The frontier (published, not yet shipped)

- **Cluster-LOD ray tracing (Nanite-style continuous LOD in the BVH)** (HPG 2023 / NVIDIA samples, 2026; evidence weak). Per-frame cluster LOD selection with cluster BLASes built each frame lets billions of source triangles be traced at pixel-sized detail within a memory budget, replacing discrete LOD chains and fallback meshes.
  Evidence: Benthin and Peters HPG 2023: per-frame cluster BVH build reaching more than 74% of peak memory bandwidth (no ms in the abstract). NVIDIA vk_lod_clusters sample (Zorah scene with 1.63B triangles, 8-12 GB VRAM, no ms). Mega Geometry 2.0 (Sept 2026) adds streaming cluster LOD. UE native Nanite RT still experimental. Could beat: RT2-38's discrete LOD chains plus per-view ray LOD, and the planned far-field stand-ins, for dense assets. Cost: not published Risk: NVIDIA-only on Vulkan; large preprocessing and streaming system; trace slowdown from overlapping clusters.
  Sources: <https://momentsingraphics.de/HPG2023.html>; <https://github.com/nvpro-samples/vk_lod_clusters>; <https://videocardz.com/newz/nvidia-rtx-mega-geometry-2-0-adds-geometry-streaming-for-detailed-ray-traced-scenes>
- **Partitioned TLAS for large, mostly static instance sets** (Khronos proposal / NVIDIA sample, 2025; evidence moderate). Rebuilding only changed partitions makes TLAS cost scale with change, not with instance count.
  Evidence: Vulkan NV proposal and the vk_partitioned_tlas sample (1.2M static plus 170k dynamic instances), qualitative timing only. The NVIDIA GDC 2026 foliage system is built on it; the Witcher 4 integration is announced, not shipped. Could beat: A full TLAS rebuild each frame at the 120k to 1M instances RT2-39 targets. Cost: not published Risk: Vendor lock on Vulkan; partition layout needs a general rule (spatial grid by pixel footprint or TLAS bounds), not a scene-tuned one.
  Sources: <https://docs.vulkan.org/features/latest/features/proposals/VK_NV_partitioned_acceleration_structure.html>; <https://github.com/nvpro-samples/vk_partitioned_tlas>; <https://www.nvidia.com/en-us/geforce/news/gdc-2026-nvidia-geforce-rtx-announcements/>
- **Cross-vendor clusters + PTLAS + indirect AS ops (DXR 'Part 2', SM 6.10)** (Microsoft DirectX-Specs, 2026; evidence weak). Standardises Mega Geometry-style building blocks, with cluster IDs readable from RayQuery.
  Evidence: Microsoft spec preview v0.38 (8 Sept 2026); no shipping drivers or Vulkan equivalent found. Could beat: The NV-only extensions, by removing the vendor-lock objection if a Vulkan EXT follows. Cost: not published Risk: The Vulkan timeline is unknown; the feature set may change before release.
  Sources: <https://microsoft.github.io/DirectX-Specs/d3d/Raytracing2.html>
- **RT pipeline + SER for divergent hit shading, versus inline + deferred hit shading** (Khronos blog / NVIDIA, 2025; evidence strong). Hardware reordering gives 24-270% speedups in divergent path tracers.
  Evidence: Indiana Jones -24% TraceMain (RTX 5080); Wukong 15.1 to 4.08 ms; Alan Wake 2 with OMM 16.8 to 10.2 ms; vk_gltf_renderer +47.8% (RTX 40 at 4K). All are path tracers with heavy material divergence; no numbers exist for single-bounce reflections with a simple BRDF. Could beat: Inline ray queries in compute for RageV's reflection and GI passes, but only if their hit shading is divergent enough. Cost: Pipeline, SBT and compile infrastructure; per-pass A/B needed. Risk: RageV's traced passes were measured 90-99% bound by pixel count (RT-14), so hit divergence may not be the bottleneck; the gain could be zero.
  Sources: <https://www.khronos.org/blog/boosting-ray-tracing-performance-with-shader-execution-reordering-introducing-vk-ext-ray-tracing-invocation-reorder>; <https://forums.developer.nvidia.com/t/path-tracing-optimization-in-indiana-jones-shader-execution-reordering-and-live-state-reductions/333330>
- **Dense Geometry Format (compressed geometry consumed directly by RT hardware)** (AMD GPUOpen, 2024; evidence weak). Block-compressed meshes cut geometry and BVH memory and latency, with future hardware decoding them directly.
  Evidence: AMD HPG 2024 paper plus SDK; no shipping hardware support; animated DGF encoding in 2025. Could beat: The planned cooked vertex streams (RT2-37) and BLAS memory, on future AMD hardware only. Cost: not published Risk: No NVIDIA path; irrelevant to an RTX 50 target today.
  Sources: <https://gpuopen.com/dgf/>
- **GPU work graphs** (Microsoft / Khronos registry, 2024; evidence weak). GPU-generated work without CPU round trips or worst-case allocation, which could replace indirect-dispatch chains for tile lists and material dispatch.
  Evidence: D3D12 shipped in March 2024 (SM 6.8); Vulkan VK_AMDX_shader_enqueue is provisional and AMD-only (rev 2, July 2024, adds mesh nodes); no ray-tracing nodes; no shipped game found. Could beat: Indirect dispatch plus compaction for tile lists (RT2-20). Cost: not published Risk: Not available on NVIDIA through Vulkan, so it is unusable for RageV today.
  Sources: <https://devblogs.microsoft.com/directx/d3d12-work-graphs/>; <https://docs.vulkan.org/refpages/latest/refpages/source/VK_AMDX_shader_enqueue.html>
- **Tracing-feedback-driven BLAS updates** (UE docs, 2025; evidence weak). Updating only dynamic geometry that rays actually hit last frame cuts BLAS update cost without visible change.
  Evidence: A shipped UE option (r.RayTracing.Scene.UseTracingFeedback, used by Lumen and MegaLights); no published numbers. Could beat: A budget-and-priority refit policy alone. Cost: not published Risk: One-frame lag when geometry first becomes visible to rays (a stale shadow or reflection for a frame).
  Sources: <https://dev.epicgames.com/documentation/unreal-engine/ray-tracing-performance-guide-in-unreal-engine>

### E.4 The survey's recommended baseline and candidates

The survey's view before the comparison looked at RageV's code. The roadmap's items took these as their starting baselines where the comparison and verification agreed.

- **Baseline: TLAS rebuilt (fast-to-trace flag, no ALLOW_UPDATE refits) on any frame where an instance changed, and skipped when nothing changed; one TLAS per frame shared by all views; instance descriptors written by a compute pass from the GPU scene.** Why: UE, NVIDIA and AMD all rebuild the TLAS rather than refit it. RageV's refit-with-changed-contents path leaves a degraded tree for up to 64 frames (RT2-35). Shipped by: Unreal Engine 5, Battlefield V, AMD guidance, NVIDIA guidance. Measured by: TLAS build GPU ms and CPU packing ms at 1k/20k/60k/120k instances (still, and 1%/10%/100% moving) on the 5070 Ti Laptop; trace ms of a fixed reflection pass after 64 frames of refit versus rebuild, A,B,B,A.
- **Baseline: TLAS instance culling by projected solid angle (scale-invariant; no metre radius), plus far-field stand-ins under their own mask bit, traced only after a near-field miss.** Why: Shipped by UE, BFV and Unity; far field is how UE covers km scale. The solid-angle form satisfies the owner's general-solutions rule where a distance in metres does not. Shipped by: Unreal Engine 5, Battlefield V, Unity HDRP. Measured by: Per-pixel diff (shadows, reflections, GI) against unculled at thresholds of 0.5/1/2/4 degrees on the bridge (Headland, Cliff) and the garage; TLAS plus BLAS ms and instance count; popping checked over a 60-frame swing.
- **Baseline: BLAS manager: static BLASes built at load under a global per-frame budget, fast-to-trace plus compaction, pooled with 256-byte alignment; one multi-geometry BLAS per mesh LOD and one instance per placed model; dynamic BLASes refit under a triangle budget ordered by screen size and age, skipped when inputs are unchanged, rebuilt on a cadence or after large deformation; compaction kept for refit-only BLASes.** Why: UE, BFV, Indiana Jones and NVIDIA practice. Compaction took Indiana Jones' BLAS memory from 1027 to 606 MB; BFV's policy took builds from 14.5 to 1.15 ms. Shipped by: Unreal Engine 5, Indiana Jones and the Great Circle, Battlefield V. Measured by: BLAS MB before and after compaction by category; worst frame while 200 meshes spawn; update ms of the skinned test scene; trace-cost drift over 60 s with and without rebuild cadence; car reflection trace with 150 instances vs 1.
- **Baseline: Opacity micromaps for alpha-tested geometry: 4-state baked offline (OMM SDK inside private files), exact for shadow and visibility rays; forced 2-state adopted per ray type only by diff image; OMM memory budget.** Why: Shipped in Indiana Jones (TraceMain 7.90 to 3.58 ms on an RTX 5080) and Alan Wake 2; the EXT version works inside ray-query proceed loops, which is RageV's cutout path. Shipped by: Indiana Jones and the Great Circle, Alan Wake 2, DOOM: The Dark Ages. Measured by: Trace ms A,B,B,A on the RT2-2c foliage scene and the bridge; per-pixel shadow diff exactly 0 for 4-state; count of proceed-loop alpha tests before and after; OMM MB.
- **Baseline: Ray passes as compute (8x8 or 16x8 groups, thread-group swizzle), with GPU tile classification and indirect dispatch over tiles with work, explicit texture LOD at hits, and deferred hit shading (packed hit records, per-material indirect dispatch) wherever hit shading is heavy.** Why: id Tech 8's GI, BFV and AMD all do this; it is the inline-ray-query answer to SER, and it enables async compute and shared-memory filters. Shipped by: DOOM: The Dark Ages, Battlefield V, AMD FidelityFX. Measured by: RT2-14's fragment-vs-compute A,B,B,A with output diff 0; then inline-shading vs deferred-hit-shading on reflections (ms, and Nsight active threads per warp); swizzle on vs off (L2 hit rate).
- **Baseline: A scheduling render graph: declared access, pass culling, transient aliasing, batched and split barriers, TLAS build as a graph pass, and an async compute queue for AS builds and independent compute, with timeline semaphores.** Why: Frostbite FrameGraph, UE RDG and Granite are the standard; id's async GI saved about 0.5 ms on consoles. Shipped by: Frostbite, Unreal Engine 5, DOOM: The Dark Ages. Measured by: Short-lived VRAM before and after; per-pass GPU timings and inter-pass gaps; async on vs off on the 5070 Ti Laptop for the TLAS build plus one trace, A,B,B,A; sync validation clean.
- **Baseline: Camera-relative (translated-world) rendering: doubles on the CPU, camera-relative float matrices, and TLAS instances plus ray origins in the same camera-relative frame; world caches keyed to a stepped origin.** Why: UE5 LWC ships this; Unity's TLAS build takes a relative origin. Shipped by: Unreal Engine 5, Unity HDRP. Measured by: RT2-41's fixture at 10 and 30 km: per-pixel diff against the same content at the origin, covering shadows, reflections, reprojection and cache-cell stability.
- **Baseline: Memory budget via VK_EXT_memory_budget and VMA budget flags, with degrade-not-crash; BLAS residency tied to TLAS references inside a pool.** Why: UE does reference-based BLAS residency; VMA supports within-budget allocation; the target GPU has 12 GB. Shipped by: Unreal Engine 5, Indiana Jones and the Great Circle. Measured by: VRAM by category for the three scenes at 2560x1600; a forced low-budget arm degrading without crashing; BLAS eviction count and hitch ms.
- **Candidate: Raygen + SER arm for the reflection (and possibly GI) trace, using ray queries inside raygen plus hitObjectRecordFromQueryEXT and reorderThreadEXT keyed by material, against compute + deferred hit shading.** Why better: Hardware regrouping removes shading divergence without hit-record bandwidth; it gave 24-270% in shipped path tracers. Evidence: Indiana Jones -24% TraceMain on an RTX 5080; Wukong 15.1 to 4.08 ms; AW2 SER plus OMM 16.8 to 10.2 ms (Khronos, RTX 40 at 4K). No single-bounce reflection data. Risk: Needs the RT pipeline, SBT and stack-size work; gains could be near zero if RageV's passes stay bound by pixel count (RT-14 found 90-99%). Measured by: Reflection trace ms A,B,B,A on the garage (chrome, puddle) and the bridge at 2560x1600; Nsight active threads per warp; output diff 0.
- **Candidate: Partitioned TLAS (VK_NV_partitioned_acceleration_structure) behind the RT world module's interface.** Why better: Rebuild cost scales with change, not with instance count; per-partition translation helps precision in large worlds. Evidence: NVIDIA sample and proposal only; no published ms. Risk: NVIDIA-only in Vulkan; keep it behind the RT world interface with a full rebuild as fallback. Measured by: TLAS update ms and trace ms against full rebuild at 120k and 1M instances with 1%/10%/100% moving (RT2-39's scaling test); partition layout from a general rule.
- **Candidate: Cluster BLAS (VK_NV_cluster_acceleration_structure) for skinned and animated meshes, and later for cluster LOD.** Why better: Much faster BLAS builds for deforming geometry, with smaller memory. Evidence: Alan Wake 2: 5-20% FPS and 300 MB (NVIDIA); +13% and about 1 GB on an RTX 4090 at 4K (secondary). No ms breakdown. Risk: Vendor lock; a cluster preprocessing pipeline is needed; trace cost may rise where clusters overlap. Measured by: Skinned test scene: BLAS update ms, trace ms and BLAS MB, cluster vs refit-with-rebuild-cadence, over 60 s; shadow diff against a full-rebuild reference.
- **Candidate: Tracing-feedback-driven dynamic BLAS updates.** Why better: Skips refits for geometry no ray touched. Evidence: Shipped option in UE; no numbers. Risk: A stale frame on first visibility. Measured by: Dynamic update ms at 60k objects with 10% moving, and a one-frame-lag diff when an object first enters ray view.
- **Candidate: VK_KHR_ray_tracing_position_fetch to replace part of the per-frame hit table (geometric normal and position at the hit).** Why better: Removes vertex fetches and per-instance hit-row data for geometric terms. Evidence: Spec only; no published cost. Risk: The data-access flag may enlarge BLASes; it does not supply UVs or material, so the hit table does not fully disappear. Measured by: Hit-table bytes and CPU rebuild ms before and after; trace-pass ms A,B,B,A; BLAS MB with the data-access flag.

### E.5 RageV against them, topic by topic

- **TLAS build policy (rebuild vs refit, flags).** Comparison: behind. Verified: behind. Confidence: medium.
  RageV: Refit in place when the instance count equals the last build and fewer than 64 refits in a row; contents (BLAS refs, masks, flags) may change under a refit. Always built with PREFER_FAST_TRACE|ALLOW_UPDATE. Code: `RageV/src/Platform/Vulkan/VulkanResources.cpp:1308-1329`, `RageV/src/Platform/Vulkan/VulkanResources.cpp:1126-1127`, `RageV/src/Platform/Vulkan/VulkanResources.cpp:1314-1315`
  Top engines: UE/Lumen rebuild the TLAS every frame (Lumen 2022: 'Rebuilt every frame', 100K-instance limit found for Matrix Awakens). NVIDIA and AMD guidance: TLAS rebuild with fast-trace, no refit. Sources: `build/rt_research/sources/lumen_2022.txt:1945-1960`; <https://developer.nvidia.com/blog/best-practices-for-using-nvidia-rtx-ray-tracing-updated/>; <https://gpuopen.com/learn/rdna-performance-guide/>
  Why: A refit keeps a tree fitted to where things were, and ALLOW_UPDATE on every build can cost trace speed and memory even when no refit happens. RageV has never measured either.
- **Skipping the TLAS build when nothing changed.** Comparison: equal. Verified: equal. Confidence: high.
  RageV: Build recorded every frame. The content key exists but is computed only under --measured-change, and only after the build. Code: `RageV/src/RageV/Renderer/RayShadows.cpp:434`, `RageV/src/RageV/Renderer/RayShadows.cpp:439-467`
  Top engines: UE rebuilds every frame without a skip (Lumen 2022). NVIDIA advises skipping only for BLASes that do not change. Sources: `build/rt_research/sources/lumen_2022.txt:1945-1950`
  Why: Neither skips. RageV already has the key that would let it skip, which UE lacks.
- **Where TLAS instances come from.** Comparison: behind. Verified: behind. Confidence: high.
  RageV: The ECS is walked every frame for every view, with no culling. Each AddInstance copies three shared_ptrs and 128-byte params into a record. The CPU packs every instance into mapped memory; this cost 0.219 ms for 1021 instances. The custom index is build order. Code: `RageV/src/RageV/Scene/Scene.cpp:2872-2951`, `RageV/src/RageV/Renderer/RayShadows.cpp:261-345`, `RageV/src/Platform/Vulkan/VulkanResources.cpp:1233-1275`, `RageV/src/RageV/Renderer/RayShadows.cpp:270`
  Top engines: UE: the GPU Scene with a GPU-built instance list and instance-list compaction (phase 1, UE docs). NVIDIA: write instance descriptors on the GPU. Sources: <https://dev.epicgames.com/documentation/unreal-engine/ray-tracing-performance-guide-in-unreal-engine>
  Why: The scale scene shows a 20 ms shadow phase at 60k objects with rays on, against 3 ms with rays off (inferred to be the RT instance work). CPU cost grows with object count, not with change.
- **TLAS instance culling.** Comparison: not comparable. Verified: not comparable. Confidence: medium.
  RageV: None: every mesh entity and every terrain chunk goes in, by design, so off-screen occluders still shadow. Blended materials are left out. Code: `RageV/src/RageV/Scene/Scene.cpp:2854-2862`, `RageV/src/RageV/Scene/Scene.cpp:2909-2910`, `RageV/src/RageV/Scene/Scene.cpp:2937-2949`
  Top engines: UE culls by distance OR solid angle (100 m / 1 degree by default). BFV culled at a 4-degree projected angle (20.2k to 2.8k instances). Lumen limited the trace distance to 200 m, which cost it sky occlusion. Snowdrop leaves tiny objects such as grass out of the RT world. Sources: `build/rt_research/sources/lumen_2022.txt:1960-1968`; `build/rt_research/sources/snowdrop_gdc2024.txt:734-737`; <https://developer.download.nvidia.com/video/gputechconf/gtc/2019/presentation/s91023-it-just-works-ray-traced-reflections-in-battlefield-v.pdf>
  Why: RageV's choice is more correct at demo scale, where instance counts are small. The industry's culling is a scale lever that RageV lacks for 100k-1M instances. It trades correctness for cost, so it is an owner decision.
- **Static BLAS build, compaction, pooling.** Comparison: behind. Verified: behind. Confidence: high.
  RageV: Built lazily the first time the TLAS walk asks for it, inside frame recording, each with its own scratch and a blocking ImmediateSubmit. Flags are PREFER_FAST_TRACE only: no ALLOW_COMPACTION and no compaction anywhere. Each BLAS has its own buffer, and scratch alignment is not honoured. Code: `RageV/src/RageV/Renderer/Mesh.cpp:209-231`, `RageV/src/Platform/Vulkan/VulkanResources.cpp:988-1051`, `RageV/src/Platform/Vulkan/VulkanResources.cpp:903-935`
  Top engines: Built at load in batches, compacted and pooled at 256-byte alignment (NVIDIA; up to about 50% smaller). Indiana Jones went from 1027 to 606 MB. Lumen on consoles uses pre-built, streamed BLASes. Sources: <https://developer.nvidia.com/blog/best-practices-for-using-nvidia-rtx-ray-tracing-updated/>; <https://developer.nvidia.com/blog/path-tracing-optimizations-in-indiana-jones-opacity-micromaps-and-compaction-of-dynamic-blass/>; `build/rt_research/sources/lumen_2022.txt:1826-1840`
  Why: The first ray-traced frame and every spawn cost one blocking GPU round trip per new mesh; a 4097-sample terrain would take about 4,096 (inferred). BLAS memory stays at the driver's worst case.
- **Skinned and dynamic BLAS policy.** Comparison: behind. Verified: behind. Confidence: high.
  RageV: Built once, then refit forever with ALLOW_UPDATE|PREFER_FAST_BUILD. Nothing skips an unchanged pose, and nothing sets a budget. Each caster gets its own dispatch, buffer barrier and serialized build, followed by a full AS-to-AS barrier. The meshes are skinned twice (the raster vertex shader and a compute pass), and no normals are written. Code: `RageV/src/Platform/Vulkan/VulkanResources.cpp:1055-1100`, `RageV/src/RageV/Renderer/RayShadows.cpp:393-426`, `RageV/src/RageV/Renderer/RayShadows.cpp:130-175`
  Top engines: Refits under a triangle budget ordered by priority, with skips for unchanged inputs (BFV compared bone matrices: 400 to 50 rebuilds) and periodic rebuilds (UE, BFV). Indiana Jones compacts its refit-only BLASes. Refit is about 10x cheaper than a rebuild (id). Sources: <https://dev.epicgames.com/documentation/unreal-engine/ray-tracing-performance-guide-in-unreal-engine>; <https://developer.download.nvidia.com/video/gputechconf/gtc/2019/presentation/s91023-it-just-works-ray-traced-reflections-in-battlefield-v.pdf>
  Why: The cost is negligible with one fox, but it grows serially with crowd size, and the trees degrade without limit.
- **BLAS granularity (sections, parts).** Comparison: behind. Verified: behind. Confidence: medium.
  RageV: Every glTF primitive is its own Mesh and single-geometry BLAS, and every part its own entity and TLAS instance (the car has about 150). Code: `RageV/src/RageV/Asset/AssetManager.cpp:814-824`, `RageV/src/RageV/Renderer/Mesh.cpp:218-227`, `RageV/src/RageV/Scene/Scene.cpp:2873-2925`
  Top engines: One multi-geometry BLAS per mesh LOD, with the material found through the geometry index (NVIDIA; UE segments per mesh). Sources: <https://developer.nvidia.com/blog/best-practices-for-using-nvidia-rtx-ray-tracing-updated/>
  Why: 150 overlapping top-level boxes per car, and every per-object cost multiplied by the part count. The traversal effect is unmeasured.
- **Opaque flags, cutouts and emitter shells in traversal.** Comparison: behind. Verified: behind. Confidence: high.
  RageV: BLAS geometry is OPAQUE by default. Masked materials AND every emitter instance are FORCE_NO_OPAQUE, so every ray crossing a lamp fitting runs the candidate loop, which fetches the 144-byte row. The cutout test reads indices, UVs and a mip-0 texture. Shadow rays use TerminateOnFirstHit. The emitter pass-through compares a folded 1..1021 identity. Code: `RageV/src/Platform/Vulkan/VulkanResources.cpp:979`, `RageV/src/RageV/Renderer/RayShadows.cpp:285-289`, `RageVEditor/assets/shaders/include/ray_shadow_trace.glsl:100-203`, `RageVEditor/assets/shaders/include/ray_shadow_trace.glsl:245-253`
  Top engines: Opaque by default, with alpha tests in the ray-query proceed loop (id Tech shadow rays). Opacity micromaps in Indiana Jones (TraceMain 7.90 to 3.58 ms on an RTX 5080) and Alan Wake 2. Lumen skips translucent geometry and tests masked materials against surface-cache opacity. Nobody forces emitter shells non-opaque. Sources: <https://developer.nvidia.com/blog/path-tracing-optimizations-in-indiana-jones-opacity-micromaps-and-compaction-of-dynamic-blass/>; `build/rt_research/sources/lumen_2022.txt:1757-1771`
  Why: The opaque path is equal. Cutouts lack OMM. The emitter-shell tax is RageV-only, and it exists because emitters are sampled on bounding rectangles inside the housing.
- **Instance masks.** Comparison: equal. Verified: equal. Confidence: high.
  RageV: Two bits: static 0x01 and moving 0x02. Runtime rays use 0xFF, the bake solve uses static only, and the subtractive shadow uses moving only. No far-field or coarse bit. Code: `RageV/src/RageV/Renderer/RayShadows.h:83-93`, `RageVEditor/assets/shaders/include/ray_shadow_trace.glsl:217-230`, `RageVEditor/assets/shaders/direct_trace.rvshader:1331`, `RageVEditor/assets/shaders/include/pbr_fragment.glsl:5415-5418`
  Top engines: UE: masks per ray effect plus a far-field mask. Unity HDRP: masks per effect. Sources: `build/rt_research/sources/lumen_2022.txt:1987-1992`; <https://docs.unity3d.com/Packages/com.unity.render-pipelines.high-definition@17.0/manual/Ray-Tracing-Settings.html>
  Why: Both use masks to split what different rays see. RageV's split is a principled bake/moving split.
- **Far field and ray LOD.** Comparison: behind. Verified: behind. Confidence: medium.
  RageV: No LOD chain, no proxies and no far field. Terrain rays always trace the finest level (ForRays), so raster and rays see identical geometry. Code: `RageV/src/RageV/Scene/Scene.cpp:2927-2949`
  Top engines: Snowdrop's RT world is LOD1-2 with one untextured material per mesh, and its cliff LOD caused 'a lot of self intersections'. Lumen traces Nanite fallback meshes with a short-then-long ray against self-intersection, and puts the far field in the same TLAS under a mask: 44% near-field penalty (PS5: 1.65 to 2.37 ms), 1.77 ms with a translation. Lumen called multiple TLASes 'a proper solution'. Sources: `build/rt_research/sources/snowdrop_gdc2024.txt:40-60`; `build/rt_research/sources/snowdrop_gdc2024.txt:733-737`; `build/rt_research/sources/lumen_2022.txt:1886-1925`; `build/rt_research/sources/lumen_2022.txt:2040-2068`
  Why: The industry scales to asset density RageV cannot. RageV's exactness is a real quality win at today's scale, so this is a scale-only verdict.
- **Where rays are traced (fragment vs compute, dispatch shape).** Comparison: behind. Verified: behind. Confidence: high.
  RageV: Every trace, accumulate, resolve, blur and budget pass is a full-screen fragment pass. The per-texel ray allocation was measured at 1.0-1.3 ms for no gain because of warp divergence, so it is per 16x16 tile. Traces run at output resolution. Code: `RageVEditor/assets/shaders/direct_trace.rvshader:43`, `RageVEditor/assets/shaders/reflection_trace.rvshader:43`, `RageVEditor/assets/shaders/rtgi_trace.rvshader:37`, `RageVEditor/assets/shaders/rtao_compute.rvshader:31`, `RageVEditor/assets/shaders/reflection_resolve.rvshader:33`, `RageV/src/RageV/Renderer/FrameGraphBuilder.cpp:2381-2386`
  Top engines: Lumen chunks traces into tiles of rays and prefers indirect dispatch. It compacts rays after screen tracing (up to 50% faster tracing), and order-preserving compaction is 21% faster than local atomics. Snowdrop dispatches world-space rays indirectly after its screen trace and varies the tracing resolution per 16x16 tile (-28% specular). id traces in compute. Sources: `build/rt_research/sources/lumen_2022.txt:311-334`; `build/rt_research/sources/lumen_2022.txt:1790-1806`; `build/rt_research/sources/snowdrop_gdc2024.txt:606-612`; `build/rt_research/sources/snowdrop_gdc2024.txt:941-968`
  Why: Only compute allows compaction, indirect dispatch sized by the GPU, shared-memory filters and async. Per-ray traversal speed is probably equal; nobody has published fragment-vs-compute numbers.
- **Hit shading.** Comparison: behind. Verified: behind. Confidence: medium.
  RageV: Inline in the trace shader: a full material fetch at mip 0 (five textures) and every light walked per hit (24 in the garage, 58-70 on the bridge). The hit table is 144 B a row, rebuilt on the CPU every frame for every view. ReflectionTrace costs about 5x DirectTrace per ray (3.82 ms / 1.39 M against 3.18 ms / 5.85 M), which suggests shading dominates (inferred). Code: `RageVEditor/assets/shaders/include/pbr_fragment.glsl:2615-2711`, `RageVEditor/assets/shaders/include/pbr_fragment.glsl:2763-2791`, `RageV/src/RageV/Renderer/Renderer3D.cpp:129-151`, `RageV/src/RageV/Renderer/Renderer3D.cpp:5571-5704`
  Top engines: Lumen: surface-cache hits are cheap, and full hit lighting cost 11.54 ms against 2.44 ms (PS5, Matrix Awakens). Snowdrop: separate miss and hit lighting passes (about -8%), and hits reproject to last frame's lighting. id: packed hit records with an indirect dispatch per shader type. Sources: `build/rt_research/sources/lumen_2022.txt:1860-1880`; `build/rt_research/sources/snowdrop_gdc2024.txt:626-682`; <https://advances.realtimerendering.com/s2025/content/SOUSA_SIGGRAPH_2025_Final.pdf>
  Why: Every shipped engine keeps hit shading cheap, deferred or cached. RageV pays full lighting per hit.
- **RT pipeline and SER.** Comparison: equal. Verified: equal. Confidence: medium.
  RageV: Ray queries only. Only the acceleration-structure, ray-query and deferred-host-operations extensions are enabled; no SBT. Code: `RageV/src/Platform/Vulkan/VulkanDevice.cpp:562-583`
  Top engines: SER ships in path tracers (Indiana Jones -24% TraceMain, AW2 16.8 to 10.2 ms with OMM). Lumen uses inline tracing on consoles for a 'noticeable speedup' and DXR 1.1 on PC. SER only reorders in raygen. Sources: `build/rt_research/sources/lumen_2022.txt:1808-1816`; <https://www.khronos.org/blog/boosting-ray-tracing-performance-with-shader-execution-reordering-introducing-vk-ext-ray-tracing-invocation-reorder>
  Why: For single-bounce signals, inline queries are the shipped choice. SER is a path-tracer tool whose payoff here is unproven.
- **Render graph.** Comparison: behind. Verified: behind. Confidence: high.
  RageV: An ordered list with pooled attachment targets. No barrier derivation, aliasing, culling or reordering, by design. Compute and standalone passes may not write targets, and the TLAS is not a graph resource. Rebuilt and compiled every frame. Code: `RageV/src/RageV/Renderer/RenderGraph.h:38-46`, `RageV/src/RageV/Renderer/RenderGraph.h:56-97`, `RageV/src/RageV/Renderer/RenderGraph.cpp:37-52`, `RageVRuntime/src/RuntimeLayer.cpp:621-632`
  Top engines: Frostbite FrameGraph, UE RDG and Granite: declared access, culling, transient aliasing, batched and split barriers, async queues. Sources: <https://www.gdcvault.com/play/1024612/FrameGraph-Extensible-Rendering-Architecture-in>; <https://dev.epicgames.com/documentation/unreal-engine/render-dependency-graph-in-unreal-engine>; <https://themaister.net/blog/2017/08/15/render-graphs-and-vulkan-a-deep-dive/>
  Why: A prerequisite for compute ray passes, aliasing and async. The gain from its barriers alone is unmeasured.
- **Barriers.** Comparison: behind. Verified: behind. Confidence: medium.
  RageV: Implicit: one vkCmdPipelineBarrier2 per image at every render-pass begin and end, buffer barriers with sync1 and wide masks. The TLAS build ends in one global AS-write to fragment|compute|AS-build barrier. Code: `RageV/src/Platform/Vulkan/VulkanCommandList.cpp:139-173`, `RageV/src/Platform/Vulkan/VulkanCommandList.cpp:297-323`, `RageV/src/Platform/Vulkan/VulkanCommandList.cpp:557-580`, `RageV/src/Platform/Vulkan/VulkanResources.cpp:1342-1353`
  Top engines: Barriers batched per pass boundary and derived by the graph (RDG, Granite). Sources: <https://themaister.net/blog/2017/08/15/render-graphs-and-vulkan-a-deep-dive/>
  Why: Mostly real dependencies. The idle cost is unmeasured, so this is low priority on its own.
- **Queues and async compute.** Comparison: behind. Verified: behind. Confidence: medium.
  RageV: Graphics and present queues only. The transfer family is found and never created. No compute queue, no timeline semaphores, one submit per frame. The AS work is recorded on the graphics command buffer before the graph. Code: `RageV/src/Platform/Vulkan/VulkanDevice.cpp:474-489`, `RageV/src/Platform/Vulkan/VulkanDevice.cpp:695-696`, `RageVRuntime/src/RuntimeLayer.cpp:496-505`
  Top engines: Snowdrop updates probes async on the compute queue during the depth pass. id's async GI saves about 0.4-0.5 ms on consoles. BFV overlapped BLAS updates with the G-buffer. Sources: `build/rt_research/sources/snowdrop_gdc2024.txt:1127-1146`; <https://advances.realtimerendering.com/s2025/content/SOUSA_SIGGRAPH_2025_Final.pdf>
  Why: Standard in shipped engines. The gain on a power-limited laptop is unknown and can be negative.
- **Memory budget, transient aliasing, residency.** Comparison: behind. Verified: behind. Confidence: high.
  RageV: VMA without the budget flag. The only figure the engine knows is the summed device-local heap size. No aliasing, no degrade path; RV_CORE_ASSERT compiles out in Release. Code: `RageV/src/Platform/Vulkan/VulkanDevice.cpp:707-727`, `RageV/src/Platform/Vulkan/VulkanDevice.cpp:820-824`
  Top engines: VK_EXT_memory_budget through VMA with within-budget allocation (UE and others). BLAS residency tied to TLAS references (UE). Transient aliasing (Frostbite: 40-50%, secondary citation). Sources: <https://gpuopen-librariesandsdks.github.io/VulkanMemoryAllocator/html/staying_within_budget.html>; <https://dev.epicgames.com/documentation/unreal-engine/ray-tracing-performance-guide-in-unreal-engine>
  Why: Histories are about 440 B/pixel, about 1.8 GB at native, on a 12 GB GPU, and nothing is seen or budgeted.
- **Pipelines and shader caches.** Comparison: behind. Verified: behind. Confidence: high.
  RageV: The SPIR-V cache is never enabled (SetCacheDirectory has no caller). There is no VkPipelineCache and no spirv-opt, and trace pipelines are created mid-frame on first use. Code: `RageV/src/RageV/Renderer/RHI/ShaderCompiler.cpp:214`, `RageV/src/RageV/Renderer/RHI/ShaderCompiler.cpp:493`, `RageV/src/Platform/Vulkan/VulkanPipeline.cpp:129`, `RageV/src/Platform/Vulkan/VulkanPipeline.cpp:418`
  Top engines: Pipeline caches and load-time permutation builds are universal. Sources: <https://developer.nvidia.com/blog/best-practices-for-using-nvidia-rtx-ray-tracing-updated/>
  Why: Only launch time and first-use hitches suffer, not the steady frame.
- **Binding model.** Comparison: behind. Verified: behind. Confidence: medium.
  RageV: Bindless for sampled textures only (4096 texture-sampler pairs). Buffers are reached by device address on the RT path. Descriptor sets are rewritten per pass and per frame, and that has caused at least five recorded bugs. Code: `RageV/src/Platform/Vulkan/VulkanDevice.h:253`, `RageV/src/Platform/Vulkan/VulkanDevice.cpp:598-613`, `RageV/src/Platform/Vulkan/VulkanPipeline.cpp:584-691`
  Top engines: Full bindless (UE, id), moving to descriptor heaps. Sources: <https://docs.vulkan.org/refpages/latest/refpages/source/VK_EXT_descriptor_heap.html>
  Why: The ray-traced hit path is not blocked, because it uses device addresses. The risk is correctness and friction for GPU-driven and threaded work.
- **GPU scene and transform updates.** Comparison: behind. Verified: behind. Confidence: high.
  RageV: No persistent scene. Per object per frame the engine writes a draw item, a cull record, a 272-byte instance row, a TLAS entry, a CPU record and a 144-byte hit row. The transform walk visits every entity 3-7 times, and the draw list is flagged dirty unconditionally. Code: `RageV/src/RageV/Scene/Scene.cpp:350-371`, `RageV/src/RageV/Scene/Scene.cpp:302-348`, `RageV/src/RageV/Renderer/Renderer3D.cpp:5571-5704`
  Top engines: UE GPU Scene with delta uploads; data-oriented transforms (Frostbite, UE). Sources: <https://advances.realtimerendering.com/s2021/Karis_Nanite_SIGGRAPH_Advances_2021_final.pdf>
  Why: This is the measured CPU wall at 60k objects (40.8 ms frame against 7.5 ms of GPU work).
- **Large-world precision.** Comparison: behind. Verified: behind. Confidence: medium.
  RageV: Absolute float32 everywhere, TLAS transforms included. Ray offsets are a fixed 2 mm*(1+slope), in metres. tMax literals of 1e4 m. Code: `RageV/src/Platform/Vulkan/VulkanResources.cpp:891-900`, `RageVEditor/assets/shaders/include/ray_shadow_trace.glsl:245-247`, `RageVEditor/assets/shaders/include/pbr_fragment.glsl:2641-2648`
  Top engines: UE LWC: doubles on the CPU and translated-world shaders. Unity: a TLAS build with a relative origin. Sources: <https://dev.epicgames.com/documentation/en-us/unreal-engine/large-world-coordinates-rendering-in-unreal-engine-5>; <https://docs.unity3d.com/ScriptReference/Rendering.RayTracingAccelerationStructure.Build.html>
  Why: Safe for today's scenes (the bridge spans about 1.35 km either side of the origin), not for open worlds.
- **Ray budget allocation.** Comparison: not comparable. Verified: not comparable. Confidence: medium.
  RageV: History-driven: a tile's extra rays come from the accumulator's young share, held by a dead band and a dwell. It only ever adds rays (at least 1 per texel), and runs as fragment passes. Code: `RageVEditor/assets/shaders/tile_budget.rvshader:1-49`, `RageVEditor/assets/shaders/reflection_budget.rvshader:1-17`, `RageV/src/RageV/Renderer/FrameGraphBuilder.cpp:2398-2437`
  Top engines: Snowdrop: the resolution varies per 16x16 tile (1/16 to full) from content-tuned weights (luminance, variance, hit distance, roughness, a 50 m ray range) with an 0.85 temporal lerp; -28% specular. Sources: `build/rt_research/sources/snowdrop_gdc2024.txt:887-968`; `build/rt_research/sources/snowdrop_gdc2024.txt:1194-1198`
  Why: RageV's demand signal is more general, and Snowdrop's constants would break the owner's rules. Snowdrop's variable rate cuts cost below 1 ray per pixel, which RageV cannot do.
- **Robustness tooling for acceleration structures.** Comparison: better (claimed). Verified: E-OB1, not comparable (unsupported): runs only with validation on; the other half is unpublished. Confidence: low.
  RageV: A GPU address-range registry, with each TLAS instance reference checked against it (names a freed BLAS before the fault), plus NV checkpoints and device fault. The TLAS is shared across views with a list-size integrity guard. Code: `RageV/src/Platform/Vulkan/VulkanResources.cpp:1155-1184`, `RageV/src/RageV/Renderer/RayShadows.cpp:357-384`
  Top engines: Not published beyond vendor tools (Aftermath, GPU crash breadcrumbs). Sources: none given
  Why: A cheap, precise use-after-free detector aimed at the AS failure mode. The industry's in-house equivalents are unpublished.
- **Per-view frame index for traced randomness.** Comparison: behind. Verified: behind. Confidence: high.
  RageV: The frame counter that traced passes seed from advances once per scene draw. Probe captures and a second view make it skip values. Code: `RageV/src/RageV/Renderer/Renderer3D.cpp:3944`
  Top engines: A per-view frame index (UE view state). Sources: <https://dev.epicgames.com/documentation/unreal-engine/render-dependency-graph-in-unreal-engine>
  Why: A RageV defect that breaks sequence stratification per view (the RT2-16 audit row).
- **Mega Geometry (cluster BLAS, partitioned TLAS), OMM, position fetch, work graphs.** Comparison: not comparable. Verified: not comparable. Confidence: medium.
  RageV: None used. Code: `RageV/src/Platform/Vulkan/VulkanDevice.cpp:562-566`
  Top engines: Clusters and PTLAS ship in Alan Wake 2 only, and are NV-only on Vulkan (the D3D standard is a preview). OMM ships in Indiana Jones, AW2 and Doom. Work graphs are not on NVIDIA Vulkan. Sources: <https://docs.vulkan.org/features/latest/features/proposals/VK_NV_partitioned_acceleration_structure.html>; <https://microsoft.github.io/DirectX-Specs/d3d/Raytracing2.html>
  Why: Only OMM is a portable shipped baseline, and it matters only once cutout-heavy content exists.

### E.6 Candidates that could beat both

- **E-BB1: A change-driven TLAS. A compute pass writes the instance descriptors from the GPU-scene slots. The build is SKIPPED on any frame whose change list is empty, and FULLY REBUILT (MODE_BUILD, PREFER_FAST_TRACE, no ALLOW_UPDATE) on any frame where anything changed. One TLAS, with its WAR barrier derived by the graph; double-buffered only if the async-build arm is adopted.**
  Why better than both: Better than UE and Lumen, which rebuild every frame even when parked: the cost follows change, and a parked frame costs 0 ms of build. Better than RageV today, which refits a tree fitted to old contents for up to 64 frames and pays for ALLOW_UPDATE on every build. It also drops the per-slot bookkeeping, and the TLAS memory for 1M instances is not doubled. Evidence: The content key already exists (RayShadows.cpp:439-467). The refit rule (VulkanResources.cpp:1308-1329) accepts changed contents. Lumen 2022 states that the TLAS is rebuilt every frame (lumen_2022.txt:1945-1950). NVIDIA and AMD recommend a TLAS rebuild over a refit. Risk: A full rebuild at 100k+ moving instances may exceed the build budget. The fallback is a measured refit-for-transforms-only arm, not the default. Cost: 1 session for the RT2-4 arm (flags plus skip on today's CPU path). The GPU-written version is part of RT2-35.
  The comparison's gate: bench_scale.py RT arms (RT2-2c) at 1k/20k/60k/120k objects:; Still arm: 0 TLAS builds in the pass list.; Moving arms (1%/10%/100%): TLAS GPU ms recorded.; All RT passes' GPU ms after 64 moving frames, rebuild against today's refit, A,B,B,A: rebuild no slower beyond the spread.; Demo scenes diff 0 through the RT2-2a harness.
  Verification: **partly true.** 'Better than both' holds only for frames where nothing changes. Any moving instance or skinned caster makes every frame a full rebuild, because a refit BLAS changes its instance bounds. At the claimed 1M instances, a full per-frame rebuild is exactly what capped Lumen at about 100K. The shipped high end at that scale is a partitioned TLAS that rebuilds only the changed partitions (FINDINGS:4390); the claim does not compare against it. 'Memory not doubled' is framed wrongly: today's code already holds one TLAS per frame in flight, so the design halves it. Rebuild-vs-refit trace cost at scale is unmeasured (the gate exists and is needed).
  In the roadmap: RT2-4's arm, RT2-35, and RT2-39's partitioned-TLAS arm.
- **E-BB2: Per-texel ray lists as the single dispatch mechanism for every traced signal:; Each signal's classification writes a per-texel ray count, 0..N. The demand comes from the signal's own accumulator (history length, and later its variance), never from content weights.; An order-preserving compaction (a prefix sum inside large Z-order groups, atomics only across groups) builds a dense ray list.; An indirect dispatch traces it in compute. Texels given 0 rays reuse their history or their neighbours, as the signal's reconstruction decides (area B/D).**
  Why better than both: Better than RageV: the per-texel allocation it measured as right in principle cost 1.0-1.3 ms in fragment warps, because scattered young texels were paid for by their neighbours; compaction removes exactly that. Better than Snowdrop, whose 16x16-tile resolution levels are driven by hand-tuned constants (50 m range, 0.85 lerp, luminance bounds), and than Lumen, which compacts only screen-trace misses: the demand here is general and per texel, and it can go below 1 ray per pixel. Evidence: FrameGraphBuilder.cpp:2381-2386 and reflection_budget.rvshader:1-17 record the per-texel fragment cost. Lumen 2022: compaction 'up to 50% tracing speedup', and order-preserving compaction '21% faster reflection tracing than local atomics' (lumen_2022.txt:311-334). Snowdrop's mixed resolution saved 28% on specular (snowdrop_gdc2024.txt:1194-1198). Risk: The compaction pass has a fixed cost (Lumen's win was on sparse lists). Zero-ray texels need the signal's reconstruction to cope, which couples this to RT2-23 and RT2-24. The ray order must stay coherent, or traversal slows. Cost: 3-4 sessions inside RT2-20b, after the render graph can hold storage buffers and indirect arguments.
  The comparison's gate: 1. At equal rays per texel: compute plus list against today's fragment trace, output diff 0, A,B,B,A at 2560x1600 in the garage and at Headland; not slower beyond the spread. 2. Variable demand at the owner-speed swing: spin_measure.py and speckle_count.py give motion speckle no worse than the 16-ray reference (today 513/85 dots against 494), a grain score no worse than today, and a pole edge-jitter score no worse than 9.51. Trace ms no higher than the RT-9 tile version. 3. Parked garage: diff within the parked noise floor (parked_stats.py), and rays per frame lower.
  Verification: **partly true.** Four corrections. (1) RageV measured per-texel allocation as wrong, not right in principle: no picture gain (11.0% vs 10.6% blobs), and about 0.55 of the 1.33 ms is the rays themselves, which compaction does not remove. (2) '50 m' is Snowdrop's ray range for roughness 0.1, not an input to tile quality. (3) Snowdrop already goes below 1 ray per pixel (1/16-resolution tiles, 1/4-res tracing), so that is not a differentiator. (4) Lumen also compacts near-field misses for the far-field retrace (lumen:2019-2023), not only screen-trace misses. The idea stays plausible, but it must first show a picture gain from per-texel accumulator demand before cost.
  In the roadmap: RT2-20 step 20b (a picture gain first, then cost).
- **E-BB3: If far-field stand-ins are ever needed, they go in a SECOND TLAS, not in the same TLAS under a mask. The near query runs first; on a miss, a second rayQuery runs against the far TLAS from the near query's end. Shadow and visibility rays test far first, because it is cheaper. Membership is decided by projected size (the instance's world size over the pixel footprint from the primary camera), never by metres.**
  Why better than both: UE shipped far field in the same TLAS under a mask. Lumen measured that the overlap slowed ALL near-field traversal by 44% (PS5: 1.65 to 2.37 ms, 1.77 ms with a translation hack), and named multiple TLASes 'a proper solution' it could not take on mid-production. With ray queries, two TLASes are just two bindings. RageV has no far field today. Evidence: lumen_2022.txt:2040-2068 (the overlap penalty and the multiple-TLAS remark). lumen_2022.txt:2000-2012 (ordered traversal: near then far, with shadows reversed). Risk: It needs proxies (an LOD chain) first. A second query on misses adds cost where misses are common (sky). The seam at the membership boundary needs its self-intersection guard. Cost: 2 sessions on top of RT2-38's LOD chain.
  The comparison's gate: Only built if the RT2-38 measurement shows long rays at Headland cost more than the spread over a near-only structure. Then:; Near-field trace ms with the far TLAS present equals near-only (within the spread).; Far shadow and reflection diff against full-detail rays: within grain.; The total trace ms lower than full detail, A,B,B,A.; No seam visible in a 60-frame swing diff.
  Verification: **plausible.** Whether current UE (5.5+) already puts the far field in a separate TLAS or scene layer is unverified: the UE 5.8 docs fetched do not say. If it does, this is 'equal', not 'better'. Beating RageV is vacuous, since RageV has no far field. The argument rests on one 2022 measurement, so it needs its own gate.
  In the roadmap: RT2-38, built only if Headland's reflection trace misses its section-6 line with near-field-only content and the rays reaching far-field instances take more than half of that trace time.

| E-C15 | industry | minor | partly true | https://advances.realtimerendering.com/s2025/content/SOUSA_SIGGRAPH_2025_Final.pdf; https://developer.download.nvidia.com/video/gputechconf/gtc/2019/presentation/s91023-it-just-works-ray-traced-reflections-in-battlefield-v.pdf |
| E-C16 | industry | minor | partly true | Ray Tracing Gems (2019), chapter 6 |
| E-OB1 | verdict | major | unsupported | RageV/src/Platform/Vulkan/VulkanResources.cpp:1155-1184; RageV/src/RageV/Renderer/RayShadows.cpp:357-384 |
| E-BB1 | beats_both | major | partly true | The content key already exists (RayShadows.cpp:439-467). The refit rule (VulkanResources.cpp:1308-1329) accepts changed contents. Lumen 2022 states that the TLAS is rebuilt every frame (lumen_2022.txt:1945-1950). NVIDIA and AMD recommend a TLAS rebuild over a refit. |
| E-BB2 | beats_both | major | partly true | FrameGraphBuilder.cpp:2381-2386 and reflection_budget.rvshader:1-17 record the per-texel fragment cost. Lumen 2022: compaction 'up to 50% tracing speedup', and order-preserving compaction '21% faster reflection tracing than local atomics' (lumen_2022.txt:311-334). Snowdrop's mixed resolution saved 28% on specular (snowdrop_gdc2024.txt:1194-1198). |
| E-BB3 | beats_both | major | plausible | lumen_2022.txt:2040-2068 (the overlap penalty and the multiple-TLAS remark). lumen_2022.txt:2000-2012 (ordered traversal: near then far, with shadows reversed). |

- **E-C1** (confirmed). Claim: The TLAS refits whenever the instance count equals the last build and fewer than 64 refits have run, even if BLAS references, masks or flags changed. Every build uses PREFER_FAST_TRACE|ALLOW_UPDATE.
  Evidence: VulkanResources.cpp:1126-1127 (ctor flags PREFER_FAST_TRACE|ALLOW_UPDATE); 1308-1320 refit = m_TopBuilt && m_TopInstances==written && m_TopRefits<64, flags again PREFER_FAST_TRACE|ALLOW_UPDATE. Nothing compares BLAS references, masks or ForceNoOpaque. RayShadows.cpp:96-99 creates one TLAS per frame in flight, each with its own refit counter.
  Correction: Because each frame-in-flight TLAS keeps its own counter, a given slot is rebuilt every 64 of its own builds, which is every 64 x framesInFlight frames. The drift window is therefore longer than 64 frames.
- **E-C2** (confirmed). Claim: The TLAS build is recorded every frame. The content key is computed only under EngineConfig::MeasuredChange, and only after the build, so RT2-4's skip cannot use it as-is.
  Evidence: Scene.cpp:2951 calls RayShadows::Build every frame. RayShadows.cpp:357-384 has only a per-view BuiltThisFrame guard and no skip. The key is computed at 441-467, after cmd.BuildTopLevelAS at 434, inside if (MeasuredChange).
  Correction: MeasuredChange is on by default (EngineConfig.h:563), so the key is normally computed. It is still computed after the build. It also mixes s_Data->Builds whenever a skinned caster exists (463-464), so any skinned mesh changes the key every frame. With per-frame-in-flight TLASes, a skip would also leave the other slot stale.
- **E-C3** (confirmed). Claim: Static BLASes are built lazily at first trace through a blocking ImmediateSubmit, with no ALLOW_COMPACTION and no compaction anywhere.
  Evidence: Mesh.cpp:209-231: the BLAS is created on the first GetAccelerationStructure call. Its only caller is RayShadows.cpp:338 (AddInstance during the frame's ray-world gather). VulkanResources.cpp:988-990 sets flags to PREFER_FAST_TRACE only. 1046-1049 runs ImmediateSubmit (blocking). A grep for ALLOW_COMPACTION, CopyAccelerationStructure or COMPACTED_SIZE across RageV/src finds nothing.
  Correction: 'At first trace' more precisely means the first frame the mesh is gathered into the ray instance list.
- **E-C4** (partly true). Claim: Every ray pass (trace, resolve, accumulate, blur, budget) is a fragment shader over a full-screen triangle. Only culling and skinning are compute.
  Evidence: direct_trace:43, reflection_trace:43, rtgi_trace:37, rtao_compute:31, tile_budget:61, reflection_budget:29, reflection_resolve:33, irradiance_fill:29 and water_shade:32 are all '#type fragment' after a full-screen vertex. None of the compute shaders use rayQuery.
  Correction: Two parts are wrong. First, the forward lit pass (pbr_fragment.glsl ~5415-5420, TraceShadow / TraceShadowFromMasked) traces rays from raster geometry fragments, not from a full-screen triangle. Second, compute is not only culling and skinning: luminance_histogram/average, particle_sim/sort, voxel_clear/inject/mip and water_foam are compute too.
- **E-C5** (confirmed). Claim: Every emitter instance is FORCE_NO_OPAQUE, so any ray crossing a fitting runs RayCandidateIsThere and fetches the 144-byte ray-instance row.
  Evidence: RayShadows.cpp:283-289: ForceNoOpaque = emitter || Masked. ray_shadow_trace.glsl:100-117: RayCandidateIsThere loads u_RayInstances.Instances[customIndex] and tests the emitter identity, then returns true for non-masked. Renderer3D.cpp:151 static_assert(sizeof(GpuRayInstance)==144).
  Correction: This applies only to shader variants that carry the instance table (RV_RAY_REFLECTIONS/GI/REFRACTION/CUTOUT_TEST). The shadows-only variant uses gl_RayFlagsOpaqueEXT (ray_shadow_trace.glsl:207). The compiler normally loads only the fields used (Flags, Identity), not all 144 bytes.
- **E-C6** (confirmed). Claim: Instance masks ARE used: the bake solve traces static only, and the subtractive shadow traces moving only. This contradicts the findings survey.
  Evidence: ray_shadow_trace.glsl:217-230 (RV_RAY_MASK_SCENE = STATIC under RV_IRRADIANCE_FILL, else 0xFF). direct_trace.rvshader:1331,1351 DirectVisibility(..., RV_RAY_MASK_MOVING). pbr_fragment.glsl:5413-5418 subtractive TraceShadowFromMasked(..., RV_RAY_MASK_MOVING). RT-SERIES-2-FINDINGS.md:4392 says masks are 'written into the TLAS and then ignored (every query passes 0xFF)'.
  Correction: The findings document contradicts itself: GEO-16 (FINDINGS:2949) correctly says two mask bits are used. Line 4392 is the one that is wrong.
- **E-C7** (confirmed). Claim: The TLAS is built only when ShadowsEnabled is true: shadows off means no rays at all.
  Evidence: FrameGraphBuilder.cpp:210-218: ResolveRayTracing returns false when !ShadowsEnabled. The reflection and GI resolvers go through it (238, 278, 328). Scene.cpp:2540-2541 returns before the ray-world gather and RayShadows::Build (2951) when shadows are off. It is the only live TLAS build call site.
- **E-C8** (partly true). Claim: Per-texel reflection ray allocation was measured costing 1.0-1.3 ms for no gain in fragment warps. That is the evidence for compacted ray lists.
  Evidence: FrameGraphBuilder.cpp:2381-2386 and reflection_budget.rvshader:8-14 record 1.0-1.3 ms 'for no gain'. The full record is docs/RT-SERIES.md:367-373: +1.05 ms driving, +1.33 ms parked, blobs 11.0% against 10.6% for the motion rule, and '5% of texels asking for four rays cost 1.33 ms where their share is 0.55'.
  Correction: The measurement is real. It only partly supports compacted ray lists. By the record's own estimate, divergence explains about 0.78 of the 1.33 ms; the rays themselves (~0.55 ms) remain. The per-texel allocation also produced no picture gain (slightly worse), and compaction does not fix that. RT-SERIES calls per-texel allocation 'measured, and wrong', not right in principle.
- **E-C9** (confirmed). Claim: Skinned BLASes are refit forever, each with its own build call and a full AS-to-AS barrier, serialized per caster.
  Evidence: VulkanResources.cpp:1055-1100: BuildBottomLevel uses MODE_UPDATE after the first build and never rebuilds. Each call is its own vkCmdBuildAccelerationStructuresKHR followed by an AS-build to AS-build memory barrier. RayShadows.cpp:417-423 loops one BuildBottomLevelAS per skin. There is no pose-unchanged check, so every skinned caster refits every frame.
- **E-C10** (confirmed). Claim: Lumen (Matrix Awakens) rebuilds its TLAS every frame and hit a 100K-instance limit. Putting far-field HLODs in the same TLAS cost near-field traversal 44% (PS5 1080p: 1.65 to 2.37 ms; 1.77 ms with a translation), and Lumen named multiple TLASes the proper solution.
  Evidence: lumen_2022.txt:1945-1960: TLAS 'Rebuilt every frame', and early experiments suggested a 100K-instance limit (content approaching 500K, aiming for 1M). 2040-2068: the overlap caused a 44% penalty to all near-field traversal, near field 1.65 -> 2.37 ms, translated 1.77 ms, PS5 1080p, and 'A proper solution would be to support multiple top-level acceleration structures', not taken mid-production.
  Correction: The 100K limit was a projection from early experiments, not a hard API limit.
- **E-C11** (confirmed). Claim: Lumen: compacting rays after screen tracing gave up to 50% tracing speedup, and order-preserving compaction was 21% faster than local atomics. Inline tracing gave a noticeable speedup on consoles, and indirect dispatch is preferred.
  Evidence: lumen_2022.txt:314-334: compaction after screen traces gives 'up to 50% tracing speedup'. Z-order within 128x128 groups, wave prefix sum plus atomics for the global offset, '21% faster reflection tracing than local atomics'. 1790-1816: 'Prefers indirect dispatch where supported'. Inline tracing is used on consoles for 'a noticeable speedup on certain platforms'.
  Correction: The inline speedup is stated for certain console platforms, not for consoles in general.
- **E-C12** (confirmed). Claim: Lumen hit lighting with complex materials cost 11.54 ms against 2.44 ms for surface-cache reflection tracing (PS5, 1080p).
  Evidence: lumen_2022.txt:1860-1880: Matrix Awakens, PS5 1080p, reflection tracing 2.44 ms with HWRT + surface cache against 11.54 ms with HWRT + hit lighting. The slides also say hit lighting gave no substantial quality benefit there, because the skylight dominated.
- **E-C13** (partly true). Claim: Snowdrop's RT world uses LOD1-2 with one untextured material per mesh and alpha by dithering. World-space rays are dispatched indirectly after the screen trace. Miss and hit lighting are separate passes (about -8%). Probes update async on compute during the depth pass. Per-16x16-tile mixed resolution saved 28% on specular (Xbox Series X, 1440p).
  Evidence: snowdrop_gdc2024.txt:40-60: LOD1-2, one untextured material, dithered alpha. 606-612: indirect dispatch after the screen-space trace. 626-640: two lighting passes. 1129-1146: probes async on compute during depth. 1155-1198 table: specular total 4.480 -> 3.246 ms (-28%) with mixed resolution, Xbox Series X, 1440p, traced at 1/4 resolution.
  Correction: The '-8%' is only the diffuse lighting pass (0.931 ms, -8%); specular lighting is -9%. Whole-signal totals move only -2% (3.966 -> 3.877 and 4.573 -> 4.480 ms). The speaker rounds it to 'about 10%' of the lighting part.
- **E-C14** (confirmed). Claim: VK_EXT_ray_tracing_invocation_reorder needs VK_KHR_ray_tracing_pipeline and reorders only in raygen. Whether NVIDIA driver 591.91 exposes it (and VK_KHR_opacity_micromap) on the RTX 5070 Ti Laptop is unverified.
  Evidence: The Vulkan refpage lists VK_KHR_ray_tracing_pipeline as a required dependency. GLSL_EXT_shader_invocation_reorder says reorderThreadEXT and hitObjectReorderExecuteEXT are 'available only in ray-generation shader stage'. hitObjectRecordFromQueryEXT can record a rayQuery result, but only inside a raygen pipeline. Driver support on the laptop was not checked (read-only; vulkaninfo not run).
  Correction: Driver 591.91 support for the extension and for opacity micromaps remains unverified, as the claim itself says.
- **E-C15** (partly true). Claim: id Tech 8 GI stores 128-bit packed hit records and shades them in per-shader-type async indirect dispatches. BFV's AS build went from 64 ms to 1.15 ms. Neither is in the primary texts read here.
  Evidence: Now checked in the primary PDFs. id Tech 8 (SOUSA_SIGGRAPH_2025 slides 17 and 20): 128-bit packed hit data (SBT index, instance, primitive, barycentrics, hitDistance), and 'Async Indirect dispatch per shader' (<10 shaders). BFV GTC 2019 slides 57-65: 'TLAS + BLAS build (GPU): 64 ms -> 14.5 ms' through 4-degree projected-angle culling (5000 -> 400 BLAS rebuilds, 20000 -> 2800 instances; cons: popping, missing objects), then '14.5 ms -> 1.15 ms' through staggered incremental/full BLAS rebuilds, PREFER_FAST_BUILD, skipping rebuilds with unchanged bone matrices (400 -> 50) and overlap with graphics.
  Correction: Both facts are in primary sources. The id Tech 8 deferred shading is for about 20k world radiance-cache entries per frame (probe rays), not per-pixel screen rays. BFV's 64 -> 1.15 ms is TLAS+BLAS combined, and part of the gain came from culling that accepts popping and missing objects.
- **E-C16** (partly true). Claim: The float-precision-scaled ray offset (Wächter and Binder, 'A Fast and Robust Method for Avoiding Self-Intersection', Ray Tracing Gems 2019 ch. 6) removes metre-based offsets at any distance from the origin.
  Evidence: The Ray Tracing Gems (2019) ch.6 method (known source; not in sources/) offsets the origin along the geometric normal by an integer ULP amount scaled to the position's exponent, with a small fixed float offset near the origin. It is scale-independent for the float rounding of a hit point computed from the triangle. RageV's trace passes build origins from depth instead (direct_trace.rvshader:671-695, reflection_trace.rvshader:653).
  Correction: It removes metre offsets only where the origin is a precisely computed triangle hit. Depth-reconstructed or G-buffer origins, and raster-vs-ray LOD mismatch, carry errors far larger than float ULPs, and it does not cover them. It needs RT2-21's accurate origins or an explicit error term.
- **E-OB1** (unsupported). Claim: RageV is BETTER than the top engines at: Robustness tooling for acceleration structures. Ours: A GPU address-range registry, with each TLAS instance reference checked against it (names a freed BLAS before the fault), plus NV checkpoints and device fault. The TLAS is shared across views with a list-size integrity guard. Theirs: Not published beyond vendor tools (Aftermath, GPU crash breadcrumbs). Why: A cheap, precise use-after-free detector aimed at the AS failure mode. The industry's in-house equivalents are unpublished.
  Evidence: VulkanResources.cpp:1155-1184 VerifyInstanceReference exists, but returns immediately unless m_Device.ValidationEnabled(), so it is a debug-build tool. RayShadows.cpp:357-384 is a count-only guard that logs once and does not prevent anything. VulkanDevice.cpp:657-693 and 1700 confirm device fault and NV checkpoints.
  Correction: RageV's half is true, but only under validation. The 'theirs' half cannot be shown: the claim itself says the in-house equivalents are unpublished, and the public Khronos validation layer has GPU-assisted acceleration-structure build checks (not verified here for KHR). 'Better than the top engines' is not established. At most this is a good debug practice RageV has.
- **E-BB1** (partly true). Claim: Could beat both RageV and the shipped baseline: A change-driven TLAS. A compute pass writes the instance descriptors from the GPU-scene slots. The build is SKIPPED on any frame whose change list is empty, and FULLY REBUILT (MODE_BUILD, PREFER_FAST_TRACE, no ALLOW_UPDATE) on any frame where anything changed. One TLAS, with its WAR barrier derived by the graph; double-buffered only if the async-build arm is adopted.. Why: Better than UE and Lumen, which rebuild every frame even when parked: the cost follows change, and a parked frame costs 0 ms of build. Better than RageV today, which refits a tree fitted to old contents for up to 64 frames and pays for ALLOW_UPDATE on every build. It also drops the per-slot bookkeeping, and the TLAS memory for 1M instances is not doubled.
  Evidence: UE half: lumen_2022.txt:1945-1950, and the UE 5.8 Ray Tracing Performance Guide (fetched) still says 'The Top Level Acceleration Structure is rebuilt every frame'. NVIDIA RTX best practices (fetched): 'For TLAS, consider the PREFER_FAST_TRACE flag and perform only rebuilds'. The AMD recommendation was not checked. The RageV refit rule is confirmed (E-C1). The cited key exists but is computed after the build (E-C2).
  Correction: 'Better than both' holds only for frames where nothing changes. Any moving instance or skinned caster makes every frame a full rebuild, because a refit BLAS changes its instance bounds. At the claimed 1M instances, a full per-frame rebuild is exactly what capped Lumen at about 100K. The shipped high end at that scale is a partitioned TLAS that rebuilds only the changed partitions (FINDINGS:4390); the claim does not compare against it. 'Memory not doubled' is framed wrongly: today's code already holds one TLAS per frame in flight, so the design halves it. Rebuild-vs-refit trace cost at scale is unmeasured (the gate exists and is needed).
- **E-BB2** (partly true). Claim: Could beat both RageV and the shipped baseline: Per-texel ray lists as the single dispatch mechanism for every traced signal:; Each signal's classification writes a per-texel ray count, 0..N. The demand comes from the signal's own accumulator (history length, and later its variance), never from content weights.; An order-preserving compaction (a prefix sum inside large Z-order groups, atomics only across groups) builds a dense ray list.; An indirect dispatch traces it in compute. Texels given 0 rays reuse their history or their neighbours, as the signal's reconstruction decides (area B/D).. Why: Better than RageV: the per-texel allocation it measured as right in principle cost 1.0-1.3 ms in fragment warps, because scattered young texels were paid for by their neighbours; compaction removes exactly that. Better than Snowdrop, whose 16x16-tile resolution levels are driven by hand-tuned constants (50 m range, 0.85 lerp, luminance bounds), and than Lumen, which compacts only screen-trace misses: the demand here is general and per texel, and it can go below 1 ray per pixel.
  Evidence: The Lumen compaction numbers are confirmed (E-C11). The Snowdrop constants are confirmed: 0.85 lerp (snowdrop:955), luma/variance/distance bounds (893-897), 1/16 to full tile resolution (944-948). RT-SERIES.md:367-373 is the RageV record.
  Correction: Four corrections. (1) RageV measured per-texel allocation as wrong, not right in principle: no picture gain (11.0% vs 10.6% blobs), and about 0.55 of the 1.33 ms is the rays themselves, which compaction does not remove. (2) '50 m' is Snowdrop's ray range for roughness 0.1, not an input to tile quality. (3) Snowdrop already goes below 1 ray per pixel (1/16-resolution tiles, 1/4-res tracing), so that is not a differentiator. (4) Lumen also compacts near-field misses for the far-field retrace (lumen:2019-2023), not only screen-trace misses. The idea stays plausible, but it must first show a picture gain from per-texel accumulator demand before cost.
- **E-BB3** (plausible). Claim: Could beat both RageV and the shipped baseline: If far-field stand-ins are ever needed, they go in a SECOND TLAS, not in the same TLAS under a mask. The near query runs first; on a miss, a second rayQuery runs against the far TLAS from the near query's end. Shadow and visibility rays test far first, because it is cheaper.
