Membership is decided by projected size (the instance's world size over the pixel footprint from the primary camera), never by metres.. Why: UE shipped far field in the same TLAS under a mask. Lumen measured that the overlap slowed ALL near-field traversal by 44% (PS5: 1.65 to 2.37 ms, 1.77 ms with a translation hack), and named multiple TLASes 'a proper solution' it could not take on mid-production. With ray queries, two TLASes are just two bindings. RageV has no far field today.
  Evidence: lumen_2022.txt:2040-2068 (44% overlap penalty, multiple TLASes named 'a proper solution'). 1995-2003 (near then far for ordered rays; reversed for shadow rays because far is cheaper). Two TLAS bindings are legal with ray queries. RageV has no far field (the target_design gates it on measured need).
  Correction: Whether current UE (5.5+) already puts the far field in a separate TLAS or scene layer is unverified: the UE 5.8 docs fetched do not say. If it does, this is 'equal', not 'better'. Beating RageV is vacuous, since RageV has no far field. The argument rests on one 2022 measurement, so it needs its own gate.

### E.8 Design changes the verification required

Each was applied to the roadmap's items on 2026-09-25 (section 9 lists them).

- **RT world: TLAS.** Six changes. (1) Add a partitioned-TLAS arm (VK_NV_partitioned_acceleration_structure / RTX Mega Geometry) and a 1M-instance moving arm to the gates. A full rebuild on every changed frame is the cost that capped Lumen at about 100K, so 'full rebuild on any change' must be measured against partition-only rebuilds at scale, not only against today's refit. (2) Any skinned or dynamic BLAS refit is a change, so the skip is only worth something if the BLAS manager's pose-hash skip also lands; state that dependency. (3) Stop citing RayShadows.cpp:439-467 as an existing content key: it runs after the build on the CPU pack and always changes with a skinned caster. (4) Reword 'memory not doubled': today holds one TLAS per frame in flight, and the single persistent TLAS the skip requires halves it. (5) Cite NVIDIA only (AMD's recommendation is unverified). (6) Measure the claim that 'a parked frame costs 0 ms of build' only on fully static frames. Rests on: E-BB1, E-C2, E-C1.
- **Trace execution: compute passes, ray lists, hit shading.** Five changes. (1) Replace the premise that per-texel allocation was 'right in principle': the RT-9 record shows no picture gain. Order the ray-list gates so that a picture gain from per-texel demand (speckle/spin against the 16-ray reference) is shown before cost is compared, and expect compaction to recover at most the ~0.78 ms divergence share of 1.33 ms. (2) Add the forward lit pass's own traces (pbr_fragment.glsl ~5415-5420, raster fragments) to the 'replaces' list, or state explicitly that they stay. (3) Drop 'can go below 1 ray per pixel' as an advantage over Snowdrop, and fix 'Lumen compacts only screen-trace misses'. (4) Drop the '50 m' Snowdrop constant: it is a ray range, not a tile-quality input. (5) For deferred hit shading, note that id Tech 8's per-shader async dispatch is for about 20k radiance-cache entries, not per-pixel rays, so the per-pixel case is unproven. Rests on: E-BB2, E-C8, E-C4, E-C15.
- **Large-world precision.** State that the Wächter-Binder offset replaces the 2 mm*(1+slope) offset only for origins computed from the triangle (or from RT2-21's accurate position lane). Depth-reconstructed origins in direct_trace, reflection_trace and rtgi_trace need RT2-21 to land first, or an explicit reconstruction-error term. Make RT2-21 a hard prerequisite for removing the metre offsets, not a soft dependency. Rests on: E-C16.
- **Ray LOD and far field.** No structural change. Before calling the second-TLAS far field 'better than UE', check whether current UE (5.5+) already separates the far field into its own TLAS or scene layer. Keep it built only on measured need. Rests on: E-BB3.

### E.9 New findings from the verification

- **E-N1.** The TLAS is per frame in flight (RayShadows.cpp:96-99), and each slot has its own refit counter. Today's effective full-rebuild period is 64 x framesInFlight frames, and a build-skip design must move to one persistent TLAS (the target design does).
- **E-N2.** The measured-change geometry key mixes s_Data->Builds whenever any skinned caster exists (RayShadows.cpp:463-464), so it changes every frame in any scene with a skinned mesh. Skinned BLASes are refit every frame with no pose-unchanged check. BFV cut 400 -> 50 BLAS rebuilds per frame just by checking the bone-matrix input (GTC 2019 slide 64).
- **E-N3.** EngineConfig::MeasuredChange defaults to true (EngineConfig.h:563). The compare phase's 'only under MeasuredChange' implied it was optional.
- **E-N4.** VerifyInstanceReference (VulkanResources.cpp:1155-1158) is a no-op unless validation is enabled, so the 'robustness tooling' verdict describes a debug-only check.
- **E-N5.** docs/RT-SERIES-2-FINDINGS.md:4392 wrongly says the instance masks are ignored (every query passes 0xFF). It contradicts GEO-16 at line 2949 and the code (direct_trace:1331,1351; pbr_fragment:5415-5418; irradiance fill). Line 4392 should be corrected.
- **E-N6.** The forward lit fragment shader (pbr_fragment.glsl ~5413-5420) still traces shadow rays itself. Not every ray pass is full-screen, and the compute-port plan does not list it.
- **E-N7.** The RT-9 per-texel measurement (RT-SERIES.md:367-373) had a worse picture metric (11.0% vs 10.6% blobs), and about 0.55 ms of its 1.33 ms is the ray cost itself, not divergence.
- **E-N8.** UE 5.8's official Ray Tracing Performance Guide still states the TLAS is rebuilt every frame. That supports the UE half of E-BB1 for current UE, not only for Lumen 2022.
- **E-N9.** Snowdrop's separate lighting passes save only 2% of the whole signal (the lighting pass saves 8-9%). The mixed-resolution 28% is the specular total, measured at 1/4-resolution tracing.

### E.10 The comparison's corrections to the survey

- RT-SERIES-2-FINDINGS.md section 5.7 (the reference survey, 'RAGEV TODAY') says instance masks 'are written into the TLAS and then ignored (every query passes 0xFF)'. Wrong. The bake solve traces the static half only (ray_shadow_trace.glsl:226-227 under RV_IRRADIANCE_FILL), and the subtractive shadow traces the moving half only (direct_trace.rvshader:1331,1351; pbr_fragment.glsl:5415-5418).
- Phase 1 said Snowdrop's GDC 2024 details 'could not be extracted'. The text now available (Kuenlin, 'An optimized raytracing pipeline for consoles') is a different talk from the BVH one (Koshlo). It CONFIRMS:; an RT world at LOD1-2 with one untextured material per mesh, alpha by dithering, and terrain ray-marched separately;; world-space rays dispatched indirectly after the screen trace;; separate miss and hit lighting passes;; async probe updates on the compute queue during the depth pass;; per-16x16-tile variable mixed resolution. It does NOT support 'a custom BVH on consoles and DXR on PC', which stays unverified.
- Phase 1 omitted Lumen 2022's own measurement: putting the far field in the same TLAS under a mask cost 44% of near-field traversal (PS5: 1.65 to 2.37 ms, 1.77 ms with a translation), and Lumen called multiple TLASes the proper solution. The 200 m near-field distance is confirmed by lumen_2022.txt:1960-1966; the '1 km' far-field figure is not in the 2022 talk (UE docs only).
- Phase 1's inline-ray-query entry misses Lumen 2022's statement that inline tracing gave a 'noticeable speedup' on consoles, and that Lumen prefers DXR 1.1 plus indirect dispatch on PC (lumen_2022.txt:1790-1816). This supports RageV's ray-query choice.
- The brief lists tools/scripts/garage/sharp_study.py as an existing instrument. No such file exists in the working tree; the garage folder has spin_measure.py, spin_timings.py, speckle_count.py, watch_arm.py and ghost_map.py.
- RT-SERIES-2.md section 1 and GEO-04 describe the TLAS refit correctly but leave out two facts. Every TLAS build carries ALLOW_UPDATE (VulkanResources.cpp:1126-1127, 1314-1315). The per-slot refit compares against that slot's build two frames back, so any RT2-4 skip must key on the slot, not on the previous frame.
- The comment at Renderer3D.cpp:5621 ('a linear scan over at most sixteen owners') is stale since RT-24 removed kMaxAreaEmitters. The EmitterOwners scan is now O(casters x emitters) per frame per view.

### E.11 Questions left open

As the survey recorded them. Where the verification later read a primary talk the survey could not fetch, the verification's verdict above supersedes the line here.

- No published head-to-head of inline ray queries in fragment vs compute shaders was found; RT2-14's measurement stays the only evidence source.
- The Frostbite FrameGraph aliasing saving (40-50%) comes from a secondary summary; the primary slides were not read.
- The Snowdrop GDC 2024 BVH talks (custom BVH on consoles, DXR on PC) could not be read: the slides PDF was too large to fetch.
- Metro Exodus Enhanced Edition and Cyberpunk 2077 BVH management (refit policy, instance counts, async) were not found; Cyberpunk's use of OMM is unconfirmed.
- Mega Geometry figures are NVIDIA marketing (100x builds, 5-20%, 300 MB) or secondary press (Tom's Hardware); the samples publish no ms for PTLAS or CLAS builds or traces.
- No RTX 5070 Ti Laptop-specific numbers exist for any technique; every cost above is from desktop RTX 20-50 or consoles, and may scale differently on a power-limited laptop.
- That UE builds TLAS instance transforms in translated-world (camera-relative) space is from memory of the engine source, not verified this session; the LWC docs cover shaders only.
- Whether NVIDIA's current Windows drivers expose VK_EXT_ray_tracing_invocation_reorder and VK_KHR_opacity_micromap on RTX 50 laptop GPUs was not verified; the Khronos blog lists RTX 40/50 support for SER.
- The 'Pushing 60hz: Shipping Indiana Jones' talk (Vahl 2025) and the Doom visibility-buffer slides (Graphics Programming Conference 2025, link returned 404) were not read, so the VB-vs-forward costs in id Tech 8 are unknown.
- Whether storage-image writes from compute lose framebuffer compression relative to fragment render-target writes on RTX 50 was not verified.
- VK_EXT_descriptor_heap's release date is unclear (the refpage shows a 2024 modification date); its NVIDIA driver status was not checked.
- The web search budget ran out partway through the research, which limited coverage of H-PLOC and other GPU BVH-builder papers, Alan Wake 2's GDC path-tracing talk, and UE's GPU-side instance-buffer build.
- Claims the verification could not settle without a measurement or a source: E-OB1, E-BB3 (each is gated in the roadmap item named above).

## F. Materials, G-buffer, composition

**In short.** The GGX and split-sum core matches Unreal's and Filament's, and RageV ships Tokuyoshi and Kaplanyan's geometric specular AA for analytic lights. It is behind on the rest: three Smith terms, no energy compensation, normal-map variance discarded, no geometric normal, ids in half floats, and ray hits with no normal maps at the sharpest mip. The verified verdicts, with the candidates tied to their items, are in the roadmap's section 11.

**Counts.** Comparison topics: 10 behind, 8 equal, 5 missing, 4 not comparable, 3 better (claimed). Verification of 24 claims: 13 confirmed, 5 plausible, 3 partly true, 2 refuted, 1 unsupported.

**The survey, in its own summary.** What top engines ship. Almost everyone still uses metal/rough GGX specular. Unreal Engine 5.7 made Substrate the default for new projects: materials are built from 'slabs' stored in a per-pixel adaptive G-buffer, with a default budget of 80 bytes per pixel, and a compute tile pass sorts screen tiles into Simple, Single, Complex and Complex Special. Every other engine checked keeps a small fixed G-buffer of 16-24 bytes per pixel with a few feature bits: Unity HDRP, Battlefield V (Frostbite) and Cyberpunk. Doom: The Dark Ages (2025) moved to a visibility buffer, material work in compute and deferred lighting in compute. Most shipped engines add energy compensation for rough GGX by scaling the specular lobe, using a small table they already have (Turquin 2019; the same form in Filament and HDRP). For the diffuse term, Unreal's older path and Filament use Lambert, HDRP uses Burley, Call of Duty WWII shipped a multiple-scattering diffuse, and OpenPBR (2024) chose EON (2025). Specular aliasing is fixed without TAA in two ways. The first widens roughness from how fast the normal changes across the pixel (Tokuyoshi-Kaplanyan 2021; shipped in HDRP; about 0.1 ms extra at 1080p on an Intel Iris Plus). The second bakes normal-map variation into the roughness mips when textures are built (Toksvig, the vMF method from The Order: 1886, Unreal's Composite Texture). At secondary ray hits, Minecraft RTX shipped ray cones that pick both the texture mip and a simpler shading level from the footprint. Engines split three ways on shading ray hits. Battlefield V generated its hit shaders automatically from the same raster material graphs, wrote a hit record in exactly the G-buffer's format, and checked primary rays against the raster G-buffer for a zero difference. Unreal's Lumen hit lighting runs the real material and costs about 5x its surface cache (secondary source). Metro Exodus Enhanced and Snowdrop shade hits with one average material per object. Shader Execution Reordering in Vulkan works only in ray-generation shaders, so RageV's inline queries from fragment and compute shaders cannot use it. The usual way to get coherent hit shading there is to write a hit record, sort or bin it, then shade in compute. Newer methods: neural materials were 1.6-4.1x faster than layered reference materials at 1080p on an RTX 4090, but they are a demo. Neural texture compression is still a beta SDK, and its filtering adds noise that has to be averaged over frames. Neither suits RageV's simple materials today. The recommended baseline: one BSDF module with energy compensation used by every path, plus specular AA from the pixel's footprint in place of the fixed 0.045 roughness floor (which is Filament's desktop precision guard, not an aliasing fix). Also a compact G-buffer with an octahedral normal plus roughness and a geometric normal, one material-decode function shared by raster and hits with a zero-difference test, and composition in compute. Candidates to beat it: a visibility buffer (which gives parity by construction), EON diffuse, and better GGX sampling (spherical-cap or bounded VNDF).

**The comparison, in its own summary.** Where RageV is already at or above the shipped baseline. The GGX and split-sum core (a 128x128 table, a GGX-convolved probe chain) matches Unreal's and Filament's. RageV already ships Tokuyoshi-Kaplanyan geometric specular anti-aliasing with the paper's own constants, which phase 1 missed. It is applied to analytic lights only. The 16-bit octahedral normal lane is more precise than the 10-12-bit lanes HDRP, Frostbite and NRD use, and that matters on chrome. Ray hits read the real material record through bindless textures, and the macro variation is bit-identical to raster. That is richer than Snowdrop, whose ray world uses one untextured material per mesh (confirmed in the GDC 2024 text), and richer than Metro or Lumen's surface cache. It also avoids the cost of Lumen's hit lighting, confirmed at 11.54 against 2.44 ms on PS5. Where the top engines are ahead. RageV has no energy compensation for rough GGX, which HDRP and Filament ship. It uses three different Smith terms: one for lights and hits, another for the table, and the exact one for the reflection sampling density. Hits drop normal maps and read mip 0. GI and bake hits are shaded matte only, and terrain hits see layer 0 only. The texture cook throws away normal-map variance, and roughness mips are filtered in the wrong space. The fixed 0.045 roughness floor also serves as the 'no surface here' marker in about 17 places. There is no geometric normal. Ids are kept in half floats, and flags share bits with values in float lanes. In RT mode a second raster composes the frame, and the reflection weight is passed in the colour's alpha. What could beat both:; one anti-aliasing budget for specular (normal-map variance baked at cook, plus the pixel's footprint, plus the ray cone's footprint), used by every consumer including reflection rays;; one material function shared by the G-buffer and every hit, proven by a zero-difference test on primary rays;; a smooth geometry normal for mirror curvature beside a face normal for ray offsets;; EON diffuse, as a measured arm only. Roadmap changes:; RT2-21 splits into five steps: the lane contract, one BSDF, the roughness pipeline, material detail at hits, and ray origins. The BSDF step moves before RT2-10, because RT2-10's area-light tables are fitted to one BRDF.; The new G-buffer stays at today's 53 bytes a pixel: the dead indirect lane pays for the new geometry lane, and F0 is computed rather than stored.; RT2-5 lands the contract's 64-bit integer identity lane early.; RT2-22 keeps the owner's order but composes through one shared material-factors function and never uses the colour alpha.; RT2-6 and RT2-44 stay, with small amendments.; The owner decides how MSAA works once RT mode composes in compute.

**The verification, in its own summary.** Of the 16 code and industry claims, 13 hold outright (F-C1 to C7, C9 to C13, C15). C8, C14 and C16 are only partly true. C8: the indirect lane is dead only when ray-traced GI is on, not in 'RT mode' as such; with RT on and GI off, SSGI still reads it. C14: Filament gives specular aliasing as a reason for its roughness clamp as well as precision. C16: the constants match the JCGT 2021 paper, not the cited I3D 2019 one, which uses sigma = 0.5. All three verdict claims fail. F-OB2 is refuted: the half-float normal is coarser than HDRP's 12:12 over half the range, and its '10 bits moved chrome highlights' evidence is about roughness packing. F-OB3 is refuted: hits have no normal maps and sample mip 0, which puts them behind Battlefield V and UE Hit Lighting. F-OB1 is unsupported: the mechanism is real, but 'better' rests on absence of evidence, and equivalent roughness widening ships elsewhere. All five beats_both ideas are plausible but need corrections. BB1 wrongly says HDRP has no ray cones and uses RMSE figures from a different filter. BB3's zero-byte premise depends on C8. BB5's cited in-code evidence is misattributed. EON needs a separate diffuse-roughness source. The design changes centre on: making the indirect-lane replacement conditional on ray GI, fixing the sources and novelty claims of the specular-AA pipeline, recasting hit material detail as catching up, and not deriving compute-composition mip selection from filtered roughness.

### F.1 What the top engines ship

- **Unreal Engine 5.x - Substrate (default for new projects from 5.7).** Materials are built from slabs (an interface plus a medium: F0/F90, roughness, haziness, SSS, fuzz, glints, specular profile) joined by horizontal mix, vertical layering, add and coverage. There are two storage formats. 'Blendable' is close to the old G-buffer. 'Adaptive' is a bitstream whose layout changes from pixel to pixel, with a default budget of 80 bytes per pixel (r.Substrate.BytesPerPixel). A material over the budget is simplified automatically by blending its slabs' parameters. A compute tile classification sorts tiles into Simple (default lit), Single (F90, fuzz, SSS), Complex (anisotropy, specular profile) and Complex Special (glints). The docs say Substrate conserves energy automatically. Path tracer support is beta.
  Numbers: 80 bytes/pixel default budget. 160 bytes/pixel allows about 7-8 layers. A complex material is 108 bytes/pixel, or 28 bytes/pixel with parameter blending (UE 5.3 overview, via a search snippet). Epic says the second slab costs more and each slab after that adds almost linearly. No ms figures published in the docs.
  Status: Shipped engine feature. Production-ready in 5.7 per the docs, though the landing page still says Beta in places. Platform coverage is incomplete.
  Sources: <https://dev.epicgames.com/documentation/en-us/unreal-engine/overview-of-substrate-materials-in-unreal-engine>; <https://dev.epicgames.com/documentation/unreal-engine/programming-with-substrate-gbuffer-formats>; <https://docs.unrealengine.com/5.3/en-US/overview-of-substrate-materials-in-unreal-engine/>; <https://advances.realtimerendering.com/s2023/index.html>
- **Unreal Engine 5.x - legacy G-buffer, Nanite, Lumen hit shading.** Legacy G-buffer (community source, secondary): GBufferA = world normal plus 2 bits of per-object data. GBufferB = metallic, specular, roughness, 4-bit ShadingModelID and a selective mask. GBufferC = base colour plus AO. GBufferD = custom data. GBufferE = precomputed shadow factors. Plus velocity, SceneColor and depth-stencil. Nanite rasterises a visibility buffer, then material passes write the G-buffer. Lumen shades ray hits from its Surface Cache by default. 'Hit Lighting' runs the real material at hits and is recommended for accurate mirrors. A per-material 'Ray Tracing Quality Switch Replace' node supplies a cheaper material for ray tracing, for example a flat normal map for secondary rays. Specular AA: the 'Normal Curvature to Roughness' material node, and Composite Texture, which bakes normal-map detail lost at each mip into the roughness mips.
  Numbers: Matrix Awakens on PS5, reflection tracing: Hit Lighting 11.54 ms against Surface Cache 2.44 ms. This comes from a secondary summary of the SIGGRAPH 2022 Lumen talk and is not verified here.
  Status: Shipped
  Sources: <https://dlss.download.nvidia.com/uebinarypackages/Documentation/UE5+Raytracing+Guideline+v5.4.pdf>; <https://medium.com/@lordned/unreal-engine-4-rendering-part-4-the-deferred-shading-pipeline-389fc0175789>; <https://docs.unrealengine.com/4.27/en-US/RenderingAndGraphics/Textures/Composite>; <https://advances.realtimerendering.com/s2021/Karis_Nanite_SIGGRAPH_Advances_2021_final.pdf>; <https://medium.com/@GroundZer0/lumen-core-technology-how-unreal-handles-real-time-global-illumination-09ed6a0f62be>
- **Unity HDRP.** Fixed G-buffer (from the Lit.hlsl source): GBuffer0 = baseColor plus specular occlusion. GBuffer1 = octahedral normal at 12:12 bits plus perceptual roughness. GBuffer2 = f0.rgb plus a 3-bit feature id and a 5-bit coat mask. GBuffer3 = baked diffuse lighting. SSS, anisotropy and iridescence variants reuse GBuffer2's channels. Burley (Disney) diffuse is the default. GGX energy compensation follows Turquin 2019 (LIT_USE_GGX_ENERGY_COMPENSATION). Geometric specular AA uses Tokuyoshi-Kaplanyan 2021 projected-space filtering. Texture normal variance uses the vMF method from The Order: 1886.
  Numbers: About 16 bytes per pixel in colour targets, plus depth, stencil and motion. The specular AA default is screen-space variance at most 0.25 (a half-pixel standard deviation). No ms published.
  Status: Shipped
  Sources: <https://raw.githubusercontent.com/Unity-Technologies/Graphics/master/Packages/com.unity.render-pipelines.high-definition/Runtime/Material/Lit/Lit.hlsl>; <https://raw.githubusercontent.com/Unity-Technologies/Graphics/master/Packages/com.unity.render-pipelines.core/ShaderLibrary/CommonMaterial.hlsl>; <https://docs.unity3d.com/Packages/com.unity.render-pipelines.high-definition@17.3/manual/Geometric-Specular-Anti-Aliasing.html>
- **Frostbite - Battlefield V RT reflections (2018).** The G-buffer is R10G10B10A2 (normal), RGBA8 sRGB (base colour), RGBA8, and R11G11B10F. Hit shaders were generated automatically from the same vertex and pixel material graphs. The closest-hit shader writes a record in exactly the G-buffer's format, and deferred 'light combine' then lights it. Correctness check: shoot primary rays and compare the hit record with the raster G-buffer; any nonzero difference is a bug. Rays were binned for coherence. Hits first sampled texture mip 0 (SampleLevel 0) because hit shaders have no derivatives. Frostbite's 2014 PBR move used a renormalised Disney diffuse.
  Numbers: Reflection pipeline 6.29 ms in total: variable-rate tracing 0.37, ray generation 0.19, ray binning 0.15, screen-space hybrid 0.36, intersect plus material data 1.98, defrag 0.08, lighting 0.46, spatial filter 1.45, temporal filter 0.24, image filter 1.00 ms. The slide text does not state the GPU or resolution.
  Status: Shipped (Battlefield V)
  Sources: <https://developer.download.nvidia.com/video/gputechconf/gtc/2019/presentation/s91023-it-just-works-ray-traced-reflections-in-battlefield-v.pdf>; <https://www.ea.com/frostbite/news/moving-frostbite-to-pb>; <https://seblagarde.wordpress.com/2015/07/14/siggraph-2014-moving-frostbite-to-physically-based-rendering/>
- **Cyberpunk 2077 (REDengine 4), including RT Overdrive.** Raster G-buffer (secondary RenderDoc analysis): RT0 albedo R10G10B10A2, with alpha as an object mask. RT1 normal R10G10B10A2, stored as xyz shifted by 0.5x+0.5, not octahedral. RT2 RGBA8 metal, roughness and other values, with alpha flagging emissive or transparent. RT3 RG16F motion. D32S8, with stencil classes for body, face, hair and foliage. RT Overdrive adds path tracing with RTXDI/ReSTIR, NRD or DLSS Ray Reconstruction, SER and opacity micromaps. How materials are shaded at hits was not found in public text.
  Numbers: About 16 bytes per pixel in colour targets, plus 8 for depth-stencil (secondary). No hit-shading costs found.
  Status: Shipped
  Sources: <https://zhangdoa.com/rendering-analysis-cyberpunk-2077/>; <http://c0de517e.blogspot.com/2020/12/hallucinations-re-rendering-of.html>; <https://www.nvidia.com/en-us/on-demand/session/gdc24-gdc1002/>
- **id Tech 8 - DOOM: The Dark Ages (2025).** Moved from clustered Forward+ to a visibility buffer, with material dispatch in compute, deferred texturing into a G-buffer, tile classification and deferred lighting in compute. Software variable-rate compute shading (VRCS) lowers the shading rate in the compute passes. A path-tracing mode exists (separate GPC 2025 talk, not read: the PDF was too large to fetch).
  Numbers: VRCS at about 85% dynamic resolution on Xbox Series S saved about 1 ms in deferred texturing, 0.4 ms in the G-buffer update and 0.8 ms in deferred lighting: about 1-2 ms in total, or roughly 30% of the heaviest deferred passes.
  Status: Shipped
  Sources: <https://developer.microsoft.com/en-us/games/articles/2026/04/variable-rate-compute-shaders-doom-the-dark-ages/>; <https://static.graphicsprogrammingconference.com/public/2025/talks/visibility-buffer-and-deferred-rendering-in-doom-the-dark-ages/Lazarek-Hammer-visibility-buffer-and-deferred-rendering-in-doom-the-dark-ages.pdf>; <https://static.graphicsprogrammingconference.com/public/2025/talks/a-beautiful-hell-path-tracing-in-doom-the-dark-ages/Khan-Stack-a-beautiful-hell-path-tracing-in-doom-the-dark-ages.pdf>
- **4A Engine - Metro Exodus Enhanced Edition.** GI and reflection hits use a low-detail material: one average colour per object, with no texture loads, to save bandwidth. Reflections use that same material but the full PBR lighting model. GI hits use N·L with inverse-square falloff. A DDGI-style probe grid supplies the extra bounces.
  Numbers: Not published
  Status: Shipped
  Sources: <https://www.4a-games.com.mt/4a-dna/in-depth-technical-dive-into-metro-exodus-pc-enhanced-edition>
- **Snowdrop - Avatar: Frontiers of Pandora.** Ray tracing uses an 'average material' computed from each artist-authored material, often depending on runtime inputs. Consoles use a custom BVH; PC uses DXR. The hit-shading details are in the GDC 2024 slides, which were too large to fetch.
  Numbers: Not retrieved
  Status: Shipped
  Sources: <https://gdcvault.com/play/1034404/Ray-Tracing-in-Snowdrop-Scene>; <https://media.gdcvault.com/gdc2024/Slides/GDC+slide+presentations/Kuenlib_Quentin_Raytracing_In_Snowdrop.pdf>
- **Minecraft with RTX (NVIDIA/Mojang).** Ray cones pick the texture LOD at every hit and choose between two shading levels for indirect rays. The low level is one prefiltered value per quad with no textures. The high level does texture mapping, local shading and shadow rays.
  Numbers: Indirect lighting fell from 2.0 ms to 1.75 ms, and the low-detail shading took 1.5 ms; GPU not stated for this figure. Bistro scene on an RTX 2080: 98 ms per frame at mip 0, 90 ms with isotropic cones, 96 ms with anisotropic cones.
  Status: Shipped
  Sources: <https://www.jcgt.org/published/0010/01/01/paper-lowres.pdf>
- **Call of Duty: WWII (Activision).** Multiple-scattering diffuse built from Lambertian microfacets, fitted with rational functions. Normal and gloss mips are generated by tying gloss to the average normal length. Cavity maps are generated automatically. The split-integral precomputation is reused for diffuse energy.
  Numbers: Not retrieved (the Activision CDN refused the connection)
  Status: Shipped (2017)
  Sources: <https://research.activision.com/publications/archives/material-advances-in-call-of-dutywwii>; <https://www.activision.com/cdn/research/MaterialAdvancesInWWII.pdf>
- **Filament (Google; reference PBR engine).** Lambert diffuse: Filament judges Burley's quality gain not worth its cost. GGX specular with multiple-scattering energy compensation of the form 1 + f0(1/r - 1), read from the DFG table. Clear coat is a second isotropic GGX lobe with Kelemen visibility. Cloth uses Charlie sheen. Minimum perceptual roughness is 0.045 on desktop and 0.089 on mobile; the mobile value is documented as an fp16 precision guard.
  Numbers: The energy compensation is described as having negligible cost.
  Status: Shipped (open source)
  Sources: <https://google.github.io/filament/main/filament.html>; <https://raw.githubusercontent.com/google/filament/main/shaders/src/surface_material.fs>
- **Alan Wake 2 (Northlight).** Path tracing with DLSS Ray Reconstruction. The GDC 2024 talk covers how material data is represented and accessed at hits, but no detail could be retrieved.
  Numbers: Not retrieved
  Status: Shipped
  Sources: <https://www.nvidia.com/en-us/on-demand/session/gdc24-gdc1003/>
- **NVIDIA RTX stack (NRD, RTXDI, PSR, SER, RTX Kit).** NRD needs view Z, motion (2.5D recommended) and normal plus roughness. It recommends an octahedral normal in R10G10B10A2, with a 2-bit material ID. It denoises radiance with material factored out: diffuse divided by albedo, specular divided by the pre-integrated BRDF (NRD_MaterialFactors). Roughness must match between the G-buffer and ray tracing. RTXDI's RAB_Surface reads both the current and the previous-frame G-buffer. Primary Surface Replacement (PSR) swaps the reflected surface into the G-buffer at perfect mirrors (Quake II RTX, Minecraft RTX). Vulkan SER (VK_EXT_ray_tracing_invocation_reorder, ratified; last modified 2026-04-02) works only in ray-generation shaders. hitObjectRecordFromQueryEXT can wrap a ray query's result, but compute and fragment shaders are excluded. The RTX Neural Texture Compression SDK is at v0.10 beta.
  Numbers: See the techniques for the neural numbers.
  Status: SDKs are public. PSR shipped in Q2RTX and Minecraft RTX. Neural materials are a demo.
  Sources: <https://raw.githubusercontent.com/NVIDIA-RTX/NRD/master/README.md>; <https://raw.githubusercontent.com/NVIDIA-RTX/RTXDI/main/Doc/RtxdiApplicationBridge.md>; <https://developer.nvidia.com/blog/rendering-perfect-reflections-and-refractions-in-path-traced-games/>; <https://docs.vulkan.org/features/latest/features/proposals/VK_EXT_ray_tracing_invocation_reorder.html>; <https://github.com/NVIDIA-RTX/RTXNTC>
- **Material standards (OpenPBR, Standard Surface, Enterprise PBR, MaterialX).** OpenPBR 1.0 (August 2024; 1.1 exists) layers fuzz, then coat, then a metal or dielectric base, plus thin film. It uses GGX with α = r², F82-tint for metals and EON diffuse, and requires directional albedo at most 1. Autodesk Standard Surface (2019) uses Oren-Nayar and GGX. Enterprise PBR (Dassault) uses GGX with multiple-scattering compensation and Kulla-Conty albedo scaling. Its real-time advice is to merge the metal and dielectric lobes and read albedo from tables. On adoption (secondary): Omniverse supports OpenPBR natively. Unreal 5.6 imports OpenPBR through Substrate. Unity has no clear support. These are interchange formats, not runtime G-buffer formats.
  Numbers: Not applicable
  Status: The specs are shipped. Real-time engine adoption is limited to importing.
  Sources: <https://academysoftwarefoundation.github.io/OpenPBR/index.html>; <https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.23696>; <https://autodesk.github.io/standard-surface/>; <https://dassaultsystemes-technology.github.io/EnterprisePBRShadingModel/spec-2022x.md.html>; <https://blog.4dpipeline.com/customer-insights-where-are-openpbr-and-material-x-supported?hs_amp=true>
- **AMD FidelityFX Hybrid Reflections.** Combines stochastic screen-space reflections with ray tracing, and reshades reprojected pixels so the material evaluation is accurate. How it shades ray hits could not be retrieved; the docs URLs returned 404.
  Numbers: Not retrieved
  Status: Public SDK
  Sources: <https://gpuopen.com/fidelityfx-hybrid-reflections/>

### F.2 The techniques behind them

- **GGX energy compensation by scaling the single-scattering lobe (Turquin 2019; Filament form)** (shipped; shipped in Unity HDRP (LIT_USE_GGX_ENERGY_COMPENSATION), Filament, Pixar RenderMan RIS, Isotropix Clarisse, Mitsuba (offline); confidence high). Multiply the single-scattering GGX lobe by 1 + F0·(1-E)/E. E is the directional albedo for a white surface, which Karis's split-sum DFG table already holds (scale + bias). Filament writes the same thing as 1 + f0(1/r - 1). The only extra cost is one multiply-add with a table value already fetched.
  Fixes: Rough GGX loses energy: about 60% at α = 1 (Turquin). Rough metals come out too dark and desaturated. Cost: Negligible; no ms published. Turquin uses a 32×32 table (cos θo, roughness). Filament calls the cost negligible. Fails: Not reciprocal, because it depends only on ωo. Turquin considers this unlikely to cause artefacts outside bidirectional transport. The saturation of coloured metals at high roughness is approximate. It must be applied in every path (direct lights, split-sum, reflection resolve, GI and bake hits), or the paths disagree.
  Leans on TAA or an upscaler: no. Deterministic change to the BRDF. Fit with Vulkan ray queries: Good. Pure arithmetic plus one sample from the table the renderer already has. Works the same in fragment, compute and hit code.
  Needs: The split-sum DFG table (or the E table) must be reachable from every shader that evaluates the BSDF.
  Sources: <https://blog.selfshadow.com/publications/turquin/ms_comp_final.pdf>; <https://google.github.io/filament/main/filament.html>; <https://raw.githubusercontent.com/Unity-Technologies/Graphics/master/Packages/com.unity.render-pipelines.high-definition/Runtime/Material/Lit/Lit.hlsl>
- **Kulla-Conty 2017 energy compensation and albedo scaling** (shipped; shipped in Sony Imageworks renderer (offline), Enterprise PBR spec (albedo scaling); confidence high). Adds a reciprocal multiple-scattering lobe, (1-E(μo))(1-E(μi)) / (π(1-Eavg)), using a 2D table E(μ, roughness) and a 1D table Eavg(roughness), with a Fresnel-average correction for coloured metals. The same directional albedo scales the layers below a coat or sheen by (1 - E), so layers conserve energy.
  Fixes: Energy loss in rough GGX while staying reciprocal. It also stacks layers (coat, sheen) without adding energy. Cost: Not published for real-time; two extra table reads plus arithmetic. Fails: The added lobe has a diffuse shape, while Heitz et al. 2016 showed the true lobe resembles the single-scattering one. The table size and dimensions grow with IOR for dielectrics.
  Leans on TAA or an upscaler: no. Deterministic. Fit with Vulkan ray queries: Good. Most useful as the albedo-scaling rule for RageV's coat and cloth shading models.
  Needs: E and Eavg tables per lobe type.
  Sources: <https://blog.selfshadow.com/publications/s2017-shading-course/imageworks/s2017_pbs_imageworks_slides_v2.pdf>; <https://blog.selfshadow.com/publications/s2017-shading-course/>; <https://dassaultsystemes-technology.github.io/EnterprisePBRShadingModel/spec-2022x.md.html>
- **Fdez-Aguera 2019 multiple-scattering split-sum IBL** (paper; shipped in not shipped; confidence high). The existing split-sum tables already contain what is needed for the missing bounces. The result is Lo = Fss·Ess·radiance + Fms·Ems·irradiance, with Ems = 1 - Ess and Fms from the average Fresnel, and the missing-energy term lit by the stored irradiance. The paper's dielectric extension claims energy is both conserved and preserved.
  Fixes: Dark, desaturated rough metals under environment and probe lighting, which is exactly RageV's split-sum composition term. Cost: Described as low overhead on top of split-sum; no ms published. Fails: Assumes the multi-bounce light is spread fairly evenly, so irradiance can stand in for it. Covers only image-based lighting; direct lights need the Turquin or Kulla-Conty form, and the two must agree.
  Leans on TAA or an upscaler: no. Deterministic. Fit with Vulkan ray queries: Good for the compute composition, which already has the DFG table and irradiance.
  Needs: The DFG table plus an irradiance value at the pixel (probes or the diffuse GI result).
  Sources: <https://jcgt.org/published/0008/01/03/paper.pdf>; <https://blog.selfshadow.com/2018/05/13/multi-faceted-part-1/>
- **Diffuse models: Lambert / Burley (Disney) / Chan multiple-scattering diffuse / EON** (shipped; shipped in Lambert: Unreal legacy, Filament, Burley: Unity HDRP default, Renormalised Burley: Frostbite (2014), Chan: CoD WWII, EON: OpenPBR specification (offline renderers); confidence medium). Lambert is albedo/π. Burley 2012 adds grazing retro-reflection that depends on roughness; Frostbite renormalised it for energy. Chan 2018 (CoD WWII) models rough Lambertian microfacets with multiple scattering, fitted with rational functions. EON (Portsmouth, Kutz, Hill; JCGT 2025) is Fujii's Oren-Nayar plus an analytic energy term, so it passes the white furnace test. EON ships GLSL for both evaluation and sampling (CLTC sampling).
  Fixes: Lambert has no roughness response; Burley and qualitative Oren-Nayar gain or lose energy. EON fixes the energy and gives an exact sampling routine for path-traced GI. Cost: Lambert is free. Filament rejected Burley because the extra runtime cost did not justify the small quality gain (no ms given). EON is described as efficient; no ms found. Chan: not retrieved. Fails: Burley is not energy-conserving without Frostbite's renormalisation. Any model other than Lambert has to be matched in the probe, irradiance and bake integration too, or GI and direct lighting will disagree.
  Leans on TAA or an upscaler: no. Deterministic. Fit with Vulkan ray queries: All four are ALU-only. EON's CLTC sampling suits the RTGI hit loop and the bake.
  Needs: The same diffuse function in raster, DirectTrace, GI and bake hits.
  Sources: <https://arxiv.org/abs/2410.18026>; <https://jcgt.org/published/0014/01/06/paper-lowres.pdf>; <https://media.disneyanimation.com/uploads/production/publication_asset/48/asset/s2012_pbs_disney_brdf_notes_v3.pdf>; <https://research.activision.com/publications/archives/material-advances-in-call-of-dutywwii>; <https://google.github.io/filament/main/filament.html>
- **Geometric specular AA - projected-space NDF filtering (Kaplanyan 2016, then Tokuyoshi 2019, then Tokuyoshi-Kaplanyan JCGT 2021)** (shipped; shipped in Unity HDRP (ProjectedSpaceGeometricNormalFiltering), Amazon Lumberyard (2016 version), Unreal (the related 'Normal Curvature to Roughness' material node); confidence high). Estimate the spread of normals across the pixel footprint from screen-space derivatives and add it to the NDF variance. The deferred isotropic form is α'² = α² + min(2λ, κ). λ comes from derivatives of the (quad-averaged) normal in projected space. Unity's 'screen-space variance' 0.25 corresponds to a half-pixel standard deviation. κ = 0.18 clamps derivative-estimation error.
  Fixes: Sparkle and crawl of sharp highlights on curved or finely tessellated shiny surfaces (chrome poles, puddle edges, car body) without relying on TAA. The paper notes that TAA tends to delete these outlier highlights, which changes the material's appearance. RMSE fell from 12631 (slope-space) to 0.134 against the reference on the paper's α = 0.01 test. Cost: About 0.1 ms for the normal-based isotropic (deferred) variant, 0.4 ms axis-aligned and 0.5 ms non-axis-aligned, in forward shading at 1920×1080 on Intel Iris Plus (JCGT 2021, Table 3). On an RTX-class GPU in a G-buffer pass it is expected to be memory-bound and close to free (not measured). Fails: Over-blurs, because the filter is approximated as a Gaussian. Handles only specular aliasing, not geometric edge aliasing. Needs good shading normals. Derivatives exist only in fragment shaders, so a compute composition must either receive the filtered roughness from the G-buffer or compute analytic derivatives. κ and the 0.25 variance are fixed constants tied to the pixel filter and derivative error, not to any scene; the owner should rule on them.
  Leans on TAA or an upscaler: no. Designed to remove specular aliasing before any temporal pass. Fit with Vulkan ray queries: Good in the G-buffer raster, where it writes the filtered roughness into the normal+roughness lane. Ray hits have no derivatives, so they need the ray-cone footprint instead.
  Needs: Fragment derivatives of the normal (the geometric or shading normal, depending on the variant). The G-buffer's roughness lane stores the filtered value.
  Sources: <https://www.jcgt.org/published/0010/02/02/paper.pdf>; <https://www.jp.square-enix.com/tech/library/pdf/ImprovedGeometricSpecularAA.pdf>; <https://dl.acm.org/doi/10.5555/2977336.2977356>; <https://raw.githubusercontent.com/Unity-Technologies/Graphics/master/Packages/com.unity.render-pipelines.core/ShaderLibrary/CommonMaterial.hlsl>
- **Normal-map variance baked into roughness mips (Toksvig / vMF / LEAN / LEADR / CoD gloss-from-normal-length / Unreal Composite Texture)** (shipped; shipped in The Order: 1886, Call of Duty: WWII, Unreal Engine (Composite Texture), Unity HDRP (TextureNormalVariance); confidence high). When mips are built, the shortened average normal (or LEAN second moments) measures how much normal-map detail a texel of that mip covers. That variance is added to α² in the matching roughness mip. Because mip selection follows the pixel's or ray cone's footprint, the widening follows the footprint automatically. The Order: 1886 baked vMF-derived roughness into mips offline. Unity computes a vMF variance at runtime from the average normal length.
  Fixes: Distant normal-mapped surfaces (wet concrete, puddle ripples) that shimmer or look too glossy because averaging normals throws away their spread. It also fixes roughness maps being filtered in the wrong space. Cost: Zero at runtime when done offline at cook (The Order, Unreal Composite Texture). A few ALU when done in-shader. No ms published. Fails: Isotropic versions lose anisotropy; LEAN and LEADR keep it but need more channels. Filtering perceptual roughness linearly is not the same as filtering α² (NDF variance). Needs the normal map and roughness map to share UVs, or an equivalent per-texel mapping.
  Leans on TAA or an upscaler: no. Prefiltering removes the aliasing at the source. Fit with Vulkan ray queries: Excellent. Once baked, the ray-cone mip chosen at hits (RT2-21) inherits the prefiltered roughness for free.
  Needs: Offline mip generation in the texture/material cook that couples each normal map to its roughness map.
  Sources: <https://developer.download.nvidia.com/whitepapers/2006/Mipmapping_Normal_Maps.pdf>; <https://www.csee.umbc.edu/~olano/papers/lean/>; <https://mynameismjp.wordpress.com/2013/07/28/siggraph-follow-up/>; <https://docs.unrealengine.com/4.27/en-US/RenderingAndGraphics/Textures/Composite>; <https://research.activision.com/publications/archives/material-advances-in-call-of-dutywwii>
- **Ray cones for texture LOD, roughness widening and shading LOD at hits (Akenine-Möller et al. 2019/2021)** (shipped; shipped in Minecraft with RTX; confidence high). Each ray carries a cone. It starts from the pixel's footprint (from G-buffer data, or from curvature estimates that need no G-buffer), widens with surface curvature at each hit, and widens further with BRDF roughness through a Gaussian bound on the lobe (one standard deviation). The cone's footprint picks the mip (isotropic, or anisotropic through hardware filtering) and can pick a shading LOD.
  Fixes: Hits that read mip 0: aliasing, cache thrash and noise in reflections and GI. It also gives a footprint for specular AA at hits and a general, non-authored rule for choosing hit shading LOD. Cost: Bistro on an RTX 2080, whole frame: 98 ms at mip 0, 90 ms with isotropic cones, 96 ms with anisotropic cones. Minecraft RTX shading LOD: indirect lighting 2.0 ms to 1.75 ms, with 1.5 ms for low-detail shading only. Fails: Isotropic cones over-blur at grazing angles. Using a fixed cone for diffuse rays turns into a distance threshold. Roughness widening has to be capped below the lobe's bounding angle, or reflections over-blur.
  Leans on TAA or an upscaler: no. Reduces variance at the source. Fit with Vulkan ray queries: Excellent. Pure arithmetic carried with the query. Needs textureLod or textureGrad from compute, and a per-instance texel-density term or triangle UV area.
  Needs: The cone's starting spread from the camera, triangle UV and world areas at hits, and roughness.
  Sources: <https://www.jcgt.org/published/0010/01/01/paper-lowres.pdf>; <https://media.contentapi.ea.com/content/dam/ea/seed/presentations/2019-ray-tracing-gems-chapter-20-akenine-moller-et-al.pdf>
- **Same-material hit shading with a zero-difference parity test (Battlefield V pattern; Unreal Hit Lighting)** (shipped; shipped in Battlefield V, Unreal Engine 5 (Lumen Hit Lighting); confidence high). Hit code is built from the very material code that writes the raster G-buffer. Battlefield V auto-converted its vertex and pixel shader graphs into hit groups. The hit writes a record in exactly the G-buffer's lane formats, and one lighting function lights both. Verification: trace primary rays, decode the material, and diff against the raster G-buffer; any nonzero result is a bug.
  Fixes: Drift between how raster and hits shade (RageV counts five different BSDFs today). Reflections and GI then show the same material as the primary view. Cost: Battlefield V intersect plus material data: 1.98 ms of a 6.29 ms reflection pipeline (GPU and resolution not stated in the slide text). Unreal Hit Lighting is about 4.7x the Surface Cache cost (11.54 against 2.44 ms, PS5, secondary source). Fails: Hits have no derivatives, so mip selection needs ray cones (Battlefield V fell back to SampleLevel 0). A full material at every hit is expensive, so LOD is needed. Divergence comes from mixing materials in one wave.
  Leans on TAA or an upscaler: no. Parity is deterministic. Hit noise is left to the per-signal denoisers. Fit with Vulkan ray queries: Good. With inline queries the hit is just a function call, so one GLSL module shared by the raster material pass and compute hit shading gives parity at no cost. The zero-difference test can run as a compute pass.
  Needs: Material decode factored into one function, fed either by interpolated raster attributes or by barycentrics fetched at a hit, plus a tangent frame both paths can rebuild the same way.
  Sources: <https://developer.download.nvidia.com/video/gputechconf/gtc/2019/presentation/s91023-it-just-works-ray-traced-reflections-in-battlefield-v.pdf>; <https://dlss.download.nvidia.com/uebinarypackages/Documentation/UE5+Raytracing+Guideline+v5.4.pdf>
- **Simplified hit materials: average material per object, authored material switch, surface cache** (shipped; shipped in Metro Exodus Enhanced Edition, Avatar: Frontiers of Pandora, Unreal Engine 5; confidence medium). Metro EE uses one average colour per object with no textures. Snowdrop uses an 'average material' derived per material. Unreal's Ray Tracing Quality Switch lets artists author a cheaper hit material. Lumen's Surface Cache stores lit material cards and looks them up instead of shading.
  Fixes: The cost of evaluating textured materials at each incoherent hit. Cost: Metro: not published. Lumen Surface Cache: 2.44 ms against 11.54 ms for hit lighting on PS5 in reflections (secondary source). Fails: Mirrors show the simplification: flat colours, missing normal detail, wrong materials (the Unreal guide shows the surface cache failing on mirrors and on materials with world position offset). An authored switch breaks the owner's no-special-case rule, and a fixed average is scene-agnostic but loses texture detail.
  Leans on TAA or an upscaler: no. Reduces cost, not noise. Fit with Vulkan ray queries: Easy: a per-instance constant in the instance buffer. The ray-cone LOD version (see above) is the general form of the same idea.
  Needs: A per-instance or per-material average (albedo, roughness, F0) computed at cook time.
  Sources: <https://www.4a-games.com.mt/4a-dna/in-depth-technical-dive-into-metro-exodus-pc-enhanced-edition>; <https://gdcvault.com/play/1034404/Ray-Tracing-in-Snowdrop-Scene>; <https://dlss.download.nvidia.com/uebinarypackages/Documentation/UE5+Raytracing+Guideline+v5.4.pdf>
- **Deferred hit shading with ray binning, instead of SER** (shipped; shipped in Battlefield V; confidence high). Trace, write a compact hit record (instance id, primitive id, barycentrics, t, or a G-buffer-format record), bin or sort the records by material or instance, then shade and light them in compute. Battlefield V binned rays (0.15 ms) and deferred the lighting (0.46 ms). NVIDIA's SER reorders inside ray-generation shaders only. The Vulkan EXT is ratified but excludes compute and fragment shaders; hitObjectRecordFromQueryEXT still has to be called from ray generation.
  Fixes: Divergence and incoherent texture fetches when hits are shaded inline in the tracing shader. Cost: Battlefield V: binning 0.15 ms, defrag 0.08 ms, lighting 0.46 ms. GPU and resolution not stated. Fails: Extra bandwidth for the hit records. Needs a second pass. Coherence gains depend on how many materials are on screen.
  Leans on TAA or an upscaler: no. Changes cost only. Fit with Vulkan ray queries: This is the natural pattern for inline queries: trace in compute, write records, shade in compute. With one uber-BSDF and bindless textures, divergence is in the data, not the code, so sorting mostly helps texture locality.
  Needs: A hit-record buffer, plus a prefix-sum or bin pass.
  Sources: <https://developer.download.nvidia.com/video/gputechconf/gtc/2019/presentation/s91023-it-just-works-ray-traced-reflections-in-battlefield-v.pdf>; <https://docs.vulkan.org/features/latest/features/proposals/VK_EXT_ray_tracing_invocation_reorder.html>; <https://docs.vulkan.org/refpages/latest/refpages/source/VK_EXT_ray_tracing_invocation_reorder.html>
- **Compact fixed G-buffer with feature or shading-model bits (HDRP / Battlefield V / Cyberpunk style)** (shipped; shipped in Unity HDRP, Battlefield V, Cyberpunk 2077, Unreal legacy; confidence high). A few 32-bit targets. An octahedral normal (12:12, or 10:10 in R10G10B10A2) packed with roughness. Base colour in sRGB8. F0 or specular plus a small feature or shading-model id. Emissive or baked lighting in R11G11B10F. Motion in RG16F. Previous-frame normal, roughness and depth are kept for temporal reuse (RTXDI RAB_GetGBufferSurface(prevFrame)).
  Fixes: Bandwidth. It also gives one well-defined contract shared by composition, denoisers and ReSTIR. Cost: About 16 bytes per pixel of colour targets in HDRP, Battlefield V and Cyberpunk (layouts from source code and captures), plus 4-8 bytes of depth-stencil and 4 of motion. Arithmetic, not a measurement: at 2560×1600 (4.096 M pixels) each 4-byte lane is 16.4 MB per full write or read. RageV's planned 9 lanes (about 37 bytes per pixel) come to about 152 MB per full write. Fails: Eight-bit roughness and normal precision can band on mirrors (curved chrome). Plain xyz normals as in Cyberpunk waste bits compared with octahedral encoding. Storing F0, albedo and metalness together is redundant. Under MSAA, averaged lanes break decoding.
  Leans on TAA or an upscaler: no. N/A Fit with Vulkan ray queries: Fits. Hits can write a record in the same format (Battlefield V), and NRD's packing helpers target these formats.
  Needs: Pack and unpack functions defined once and shared by every writer and reader.
  Sources: <https://raw.githubusercontent.com/Unity-Technologies/Graphics/master/Packages/com.unity.render-pipelines.high-definition/Runtime/Material/Lit/Lit.hlsl>; <https://zhangdoa.com/rendering-analysis-cyberpunk-2077/>; <https://raw.githubusercontent.com/NVIDIA-RTX/NRD/master/README.md>; <https://raw.githubusercontent.com/NVIDIA-RTX/RTXDI/main/Doc/RtxdiApplicationBridge.md>
- **Substrate adaptive G-buffer with tile classification** (shipped; shipped in Unreal Engine 5.7+ (default for new projects); confidence medium). A per-pixel bitstream holding a variable number of closures, capped by a bytes-per-pixel budget (default 80) with automatic simplification when over it. Compute tile classification then runs cheaper lighting shaders on Simple tiles and costlier ones on Complex tiles.
  Fixes: Supports arbitrary layered materials without paying worst-case lanes on every pixel. Cost: 80 bytes per pixel budget by default. A complex material is 108 bytes per pixel, or 28 with parameter blending. Per-slab cost rises about linearly. No ms figures in the docs. Fails: Much more complex. Every consumer (denoisers, SSR) must decode the bitstream. The budget forces simplification. Platform support is incomplete.
  Leans on TAA or an upscaler: unknown. Not documented. Fit with Vulkan ray queries: Possible, but over-engineered for RageV's four shading models (standard, coat, cloth, anisotropic). A fixed layout plus a shading-model id and tile classification gives the Simple/Complex split without the bitstream.
  Needs: Variable-length G-buffer, classification pass, indirect dispatch.
  Sources: <https://dev.epicgames.com/documentation/en-us/unreal-engine/overview-of-substrate-materials-in-unreal-engine>; <https://dev.epicgames.com/documentation/unreal-engine/programming-with-substrate-gbuffer-formats>
- **Geometric normal lane plus robust ray-origin offset; shadow-terminator fixes** (shipped; shipped in Common in production path tracers; RTXDI sample (geometric normal in the G-buffer); confidence medium). Store the triangle's geometric normal next to the shading normal. Offset ray origins along the geometric normal by an amount that scales with the position's floating-point spacing (Wächter-Binder: offset in integer ULPs, with a small constant fallback near the origin). Where the shading normal and geometry disagree (normal maps, low-poly smooth normals), apply a terminator fix: Hanika's shading-point bump, or the microfacet shadowing term of Chiang/Estevez.
  Fixes: Shadow acne and light leaking. Shadow rays leaving along the normal-mapped normal. Mirror curvature coming from normal-map detail. Precision loss far from the origin (bridge scene at km scale). Cost: Not published; a few ALU per ray and one 32-bit lane (octahedral RG16). Fails: The fallback constants near the origin are fixed numbers. Terminator fixes are heuristics with small bias.
  Leans on TAA or an upscaler: no. Deterministic. Fit with Vulkan ray queries: Excellent. Hits get the geometric normal from triangle positions; raster writes it from a cross product of position derivatives or from interpolated face data.
  Needs: A geometric-normal lane, or a way to rebuild it (e.g. from depth), and per-triangle positions at hits.
  Sources: <https://link.springer.com/chapter/10.1007/978-1-4842-4427-2_6>; <https://link.springer.com/chapter/10.1007/978-1-4842-7185-8_4>; <https://dl.acm.org/doi/10.1145/3306307.3328172>
- **Visibility buffer with compute material evaluation (deferred texturing)** (shipped; shipped in DOOM: The Dark Ages, Unreal Engine 5 (Nanite), Call of Duty (per the Doom talk abstract, secondary); confidence high). Raster writes only depth plus an instance/triangle id. Compute passes rebuild the attributes from barycentrics with analytic derivatives, run the materials (binned per material with prefix sums, or classified by tile), then light. Nanite writes a visibility buffer and then emits the G-buffer; Doom: The Dark Ages goes visibility buffer, deferred texturing, then compute lighting.
  Fixes: Quad overshading on small triangles, and the double raster. It also gives one reconstruct-then-shade routine that primary pixels and ray hits can share (both start from an instance, a primitive and barycentrics), so parity comes by construction. Cost: Hable, RTX 3070, 1920×1080. Two-triangle meshes: forward 2.38, deferred 2.57, visibility buffer 3.01 ms (the visibility buffer loses). About 8-10 pixels per triangle: 5.15 / 4.97 / 4.15 ms. One pixel per triangle: 14.99 / 11.16 / 8.85 ms. Doom TDA VRCS savings on top: about 1-2 ms on Series S. Fails: Loses when triangles are large. MSAA and alpha-tested geometry are harder. Analytic derivatives are needed for mips and specular AA. Every material path must be rewritten.
  Leans on TAA or an upscaler: no. VRCS lowers the shading rate; whether Doom relies on TAA to hide that is not stated. Fit with Vulkan ray queries: Very good. Hit reconstruction and visibility-buffer reconstruction are the same code, and everything runs in compute.
  Needs: Bindless vertex, index and material access in compute; a material classification pass.
  Sources: <https://filmicworlds.com/blog/visibility-buffer-rendering-with-material-graphs/>; <https://developer.microsoft.com/en-us/games/articles/2026/04/variable-rate-compute-shaders-doom-the-dark-ages/>; <https://advances.realtimerendering.com/s2021/Karis_Nanite_SIGGRAPH_Advances_2021_final.pdf>; <http://diaryofagraphicsprogrammer.blogspot.com/2018/03/triangle-visibility-buffer.html>
- **Deferred composition in compute with tile classification** (shipped; shipped in DOOM: The Dark Ages, Unreal Engine 5 (Substrate tiles), Frostbite (tiled deferred in compute since Battlefield 3); confidence high). One full-screen compute pass reads the G-buffer lanes plus each lighting signal's own denoised output (direct, diffuse indirect, specular) and combines them: emissive + direct + albedo·indirect + specular-albedo·specular, with AO applied to stored or probe light only, then fog. Tiles are classified by shading model or complexity and dispatched indirectly (Substrate, Doom TDA).
  Fixes: The second raster in RT mode. It also makes the specular weight come from one formula instead of two shaders. Cost: Doom TDA deferred lighting VRCS saving: 0.8 ms at about 85% dynamic resolution on Series S. The pass itself is bandwidth-bound: lanes read × 16.4 MB per 4-byte lane at 2560×1600. Fails: No derivatives in compute, so everything that needs them has to be stored earlier. The demodulation factors must exactly match those used before denoising.
  Leans on TAA or an upscaler: no. Composition only. Fit with Vulkan ray queries: Excellent.
  Needs: The G-buffer contract, and a specular-albedo function shared with the denoisers (NRD_MaterialFactors pattern).
  Sources: <https://developer.microsoft.com/en-us/games/articles/2026/04/variable-rate-compute-shaders-doom-the-dark-ages/>; <https://dev.epicgames.com/documentation/en-us/unreal-engine/overview-of-substrate-materials-in-unreal-engine>; <https://raw.githubusercontent.com/NVIDIA-RTX/NRD/master/README.md>
- **Material demodulation contract for denoisers (NRD_MaterialFactors)** (public_sdk; shipped in NRD integrations (Cyberpunk 2077 and other RTX titles); confidence high). Before denoising, divide diffuse radiance by albedo and specular radiance by the pre-integrated BRDF (split-sum DFG with F0 and roughness, ideally including energy compensation). After denoising, multiply the same factors back. The G-buffer's roughness must equal the roughness used by the rays.
  Fixes: Texture detail blurred by the denoisers. It also fixes brightness or hue mismatches when demodulation and remodulation use different formulas: RageV's 7% reflection-weight discrepancy is this kind of defect. Cost: Negligible (one DFG fetch). Fails: Division by near-zero albedo (black materials). The factors differ between writer and reader if the roughness floor or filtering is applied in only one place.
  Leans on TAA or an upscaler: no. Supports per-signal denoisers without relying on TAA. Fit with Vulkan ray queries: Fits.
  Needs: One function computing the specular-albedo and diffuse-albedo factors, called by trace, denoiser and composition.
  Sources: <https://raw.githubusercontent.com/NVIDIA-RTX/NRD/master/README.md>
- **VNDF sampling variants for the BSDF module: spherical caps (Dupuy-Benyoub 2023), bounded VNDF (Eto-Tokuyoshi 2023)** (paper; shipped in not shipped; confidence medium). Spherical caps: sample visible GGX normals via the halfway vector between the incident direction and a point on a spherical cap. It is simpler and faster than Heitz 2018. Bounded VNDF: shrinks the sampled region so fewer samples produce reflections below the horizon, with a matching PDF.
  Fixes: Wasted and high-variance reflection samples at grazing angles on low-roughness surfaces (wet floors). Cost: Spherical caps: the authors report systematic speed-ups over Heitz 2018 (no ms in the abstract). Bounded VNDF: numbers not retrieved (PDF too large). Fails: The PDF must be used consistently in MIS, the reflection resolve's ratio weighting and the denoiser, or the result is biased.
  Leans on TAA or an upscaler: no. Lowers variance at the source. Fit with Vulkan ray queries: Excellent; ALU only.
  Needs: The sample(), pdf() and eval() functions of one BSDF module.
  Sources: <https://arxiv.org/abs/2306.05044>; <https://gpuopen.com/download/publications/Bounded_VNDF_Sampling_for_Smith-GGX_Reflections.pdf>
- **Coat, sheen and metal-edge models used by shipped and standard BSDFs** (shipped; shipped in Filament, Unity HDRP, Unreal Substrate, OpenPBR (spec); confidence medium). Clear coat: a second isotropic dielectric GGX lobe; Filament uses Kelemen visibility, and HDRP keeps a 5-bit coat mask in the G-buffer. Sheen or fuzz: Charlie NDF (Estevez-Kulla 2017; Filament, Imageworks), or the multiple-scattering LTC sheen of Zeltner-Burley-Chiang 2022 (OpenPBR fuzz). Metals: F82-tint edge colour (OpenPBR) or F0/F90 (Substrate). Layer energy uses albedo scaling by (1 - E_top).
  Fixes: RageV's coat, sheen and anisotropy exist only in the raster loop. A single definition lets the trace, hits and bake match. Cost: Not published per lobe. Substrate says each extra slab adds about linearly. Fails: Each lobe needs eval, sample and PDF in the shared module, or the RT paths fall back silently.
  Leans on TAA or an upscaler: no. Deterministic. Fit with Vulkan ray queries: Fits.
  Needs: A shading-model id in the integer lane.
  Sources: <https://google.github.io/filament/main/filament.html>; <https://academysoftwarefoundation.github.io/OpenPBR/index.html>; <https://blog.selfshadow.com/publications/s2017-shading-course/imageworks/s2017_pbs_imageworks_sheen.pdf>
- **Primary Surface Replacement (PSR) for mirrors** (shipped; shipped in Quake II RTX, Minecraft with RTX; confidence high). At perfect-mirror pixels, trace the reflection and write the reflected surface's data (position, normal, material, path length as depth, motion through a chain of reflection transforms) into the G-buffer, so denoisers treat the reflected surface as primary.
  Fixes: Blurry or ghosting mirror reflections in denoisers that assume the primary surface. Cost: Not published Fails: Exact only for perfect mirrors. Treating near-mirrors as mirrors is a threshold (conflicts with the owner's rule 2). Curved or moving mirrors are out of scope for the motion vectors.
  Leans on TAA or an upscaler: partly. Designed to feed denoisers or DLSS. In RageV it would feed the reflection accumulator. Fit with Vulkan ray queries: Fits (a compute pass before denoising).
  Needs: An extended G-buffer (path length, throughput, bounce count).
  Sources: <https://developer.nvidia.com/blog/rendering-perfect-reflections-and-refractions-in-path-traced-games/>
- **Neural materials and neural texture compression (NVIDIA 2023-2025)** (demo; shipped in not shipped; confidence medium). Neural appearance models replace layered BRDFs with learned latent textures (8 channels, fp16) plus small MLP decoders (2-3 layers, 16-64 wide) run in-shader on tensor cores through cooperative vectors, with a learned importance sampler. NTC compresses a material's textures and mips together (about 5 bits per texel); it decodes at load time to BCn, per sample in the shader, or on feedback.
  Fixes: The cost of very complex layered materials; texture memory. Cost: Neural appearance, RTX 4090, 1920×1080, 1 spp path tracing: 3.5-9 ms against 14.5 ms for the reference layered material (1.64-4.14x full frame, 2.3-9.06x material shading). Training takes 4-5 hours per material on an RTX 4090. NTC: a 32 MB raw material becomes about 2.5 MB, against 12 MB for BCn; no decode ms published in the README. Fails: Energy conservation is not guaranteed. Artefacts at grazing angles on near-specular materials. Softer look at coarse mips. Per-material training. NTC inference-on-sample returns unfiltered texels and needs stochastic texture filtering, whose noise must be averaged (by a denoiser or over frames).
  Leans on TAA or an upscaler: partly. NTC inference-on-sample with stochastic filtering needs temporal accumulation or a denoiser, which conflicts with rule 3. Inference-on-load does not. Fit with Vulkan ray queries: VK_NV_cooperative_vector is required for speed (Blackwell supported). The gain applies only to complex layered materials, which RageV does not have.
  Needs: Cooperative vectors, a training pipeline, per-material networks.
  Sources: <https://research.nvidia.com/labs/rtr/neural_appearance_models/>; <https://arxiv.org/html/2305.02678>; <https://github.com/NVIDIA-RTX/RTXNTC>; <https://research.nvidia.com/labs/rtr/neural_texture_compression/>; <https://research.nvidia.com/labs/rtr/publication/pharr2024stochtex/>; <https://arxiv.org/abs/2311.16121>

### F.3 The frontier (published, not yet shipped)

- **Projected-space geometric specular AA replacing fixed roughness floors** (Tokuyoshi & Kaplanyan, JCGT 2021, 2021; evidence strong). Removes specular aliasing from the pixel's own footprint with less error than the 2016 slope-space method, at about 0.1 ms in its isotropic deferred form.
  Evidence: The paper's α = 0.01 test: RMSE 12631.7 (previous slope-space) against 0.134 (projected-space). Overhead 0.1-0.5 ms at 1920×1080 on Intel Iris Plus. It already ships in HDRP. Could beat: RageV's 0.045 roughness floor. That floor equals Filament's desktop minimum perceptual roughness, which Filament documents as a precision guard (the mobile value is an fp16 limit), not an aliasing fix. Cost: About 0.1 ms at 1080p on an Intel iGPU. Probably negligible on an RTX 5070 Ti Laptop (unmeasured). Risk: Over-blurring on smooth curved chrome. Fixed κ = 0.18 clamp. Needs derivatives, which exist in the raster G-buffer pass only.
  Sources: <https://www.jcgt.org/published/0010/02/02/paper.pdf>; <https://raw.githubusercontent.com/google/filament/main/shaders/src/surface_material.fs>
- **EON energy-preserving Oren-Nayar diffuse** (Portsmouth, Kutz, Hill - JCGT 14(1) 2025, 2025; evidence moderate). Energy-preserving rough diffuse with analytic compensation and exact CLTC importance sampling. Adopted as OpenPBR's diffuse.
  Evidence: Analysis and white-furnace results in the JCGT paper, GLSL supplied. Chosen by the OpenPBR committee. No real-time ms published that I found. Could beat: Lambert (Unreal legacy, Filament) and Burley (HDRP): better energy behaviour and one routine usable for raster, GI hits and the bake. Cost: Not published Risk: Changes the look of rough diffuse (flatter, more back-scatter). Probe, irradiance and bake integration must switch too.
  Sources: <https://arxiv.org/abs/2410.18026>; <https://jcgt.org/published/0014/01/06/paper-lowres.pdf>; <https://academysoftwarefoundation.github.io/OpenPBR/index.html>
- **Visibility buffer shared with ray-hit reconstruction** (Hable (Filmic Worlds) 2021; id Software GPC 2025, 2025; evidence strong). Faster than a G-buffer when triangles are small, and gives one material-reconstruction routine for primary pixels and ray hits.
  Evidence: Hable, RTX 3070, 1080p: 8.85 ms against 11.16 ms deferred at 1 pixel per triangle, and 4.15 against 4.97 ms at about 8-10 pixels per triangle. It loses at large triangles (3.01 against 2.57 ms). Shipped at 60 Hz in DOOM: The Dark Ages. Could beat: The planned G-buffer plus compute composition (RT2-22), when on-screen triangles are small (bridge cables, car detail). Cost: A pipeline rewrite. The per-pixel cost depends on content. Risk: RageV's garage and low-poly camp may be dominated by large triangles, where the visibility buffer is slower. MSAA and alpha-test complications.
  Sources: <https://filmicworlds.com/blog/visibility-buffer-rendering-with-material-graphs/>; <https://developer.microsoft.com/en-us/games/articles/2026/04/variable-rate-compute-shaders-doom-the-dark-ages/>
- **Spherical-cap VNDF sampling and bounded VNDF sampling** (Dupuy & Benyoub 2023; Eto & Tokuyoshi 2023, 2023; evidence moderate). Cheaper VNDF sampling, and fewer wasted below-horizon samples, for GGX reflections.
  Evidence: The abstract reports systematic speed-ups over Heitz 2018 (spherical caps). The bounded VNDF numbers were not retrieved. Could beat: Heitz 2018 VNDF in the reflection and GI trace (RageV's memory records VNDF already cutting reflection sparkle by 14%). Cost: At most the cost of the current sampling (claimed). Risk: The PDF change must reach the resolve's ratio weighting and the denoisers.
  Sources: <https://arxiv.org/abs/2306.05044>; <https://gpuopen.com/download/publications/Bounded_VNDF_Sampling_for_Smith-GGX_Reflections.pdf>
- **Real-time neural appearance models** (Zeltner et al., NVIDIA 2023 (ACM TOG 2024), 2023; evidence moderate). Film-quality layered materials 1.6-4.1x faster per frame than the non-neural layered reference.
  Evidence: RTX 4090, 1920×1080, 1 spp: 3.5-9 ms against 14.5 ms. Demo only. Energy is not guaranteed, and training takes hours per material. Could beat: Only complex layered materials. RageV's materials are simple metal/rough, so there is no expected gain. Cost: Tensor cores via cooperative vectors; latent textures of 256 MB per 4k tile. Risk: High: not shipped, and training infrastructure is needed.
  Sources: <https://research.nvidia.com/labs/rtr/neural_appearance_models/>; <https://arxiv.org/html/2305.02678>
- **Neural texture compression, and BC6 neural features without tensor cores** (NVIDIA 2023-2025; Weinreich et al. EG 2024, 2024; evidence weak). About 5 bits per texel, 4x the resolution of BC at 30% less memory (NTC). BC6-stored neural features let the decoder run in ordinary shaders (Weinreich et al.).
  Evidence: NTC SDK README and project page. The decode ms figures were not published in what I read. The SDK is a v0.10 beta. Could beat: BCn texture memory only; not quality per ms. Cost: Not published Risk: Inference-on-sample needs stochastic filtering, whose noise needs temporal resolve (rule 3).
  Sources: <https://github.com/NVIDIA-RTX/RTXNTC>; <https://research.nvidia.com/labs/rtr/neural_texture_compression/>; <https://arxiv.org/abs/2311.16121>; <https://research.nvidia.com/labs/rtr/publication/pharr2024stochtex/>
- **Primary Surface Replacement for the reflection accumulator** (NVIDIA developer blog, 2022; evidence weak). Mirror reflections get primary-quality guides (normal, depth, motion) for the denoisers.
  Evidence: Shipped in Quake II RTX and Minecraft RTX. No cost numbers. Exact only for perfect mirrors. Could beat: Reflection-denoiser history on chrome and still puddles, where RageV's accumulator currently trusts history by lobe similarity. Cost: Not published Risk: Needs a roughness cut-off (conflicts with rule 2). Curved and moving mirrors are out of scope.
  Sources: <https://developer.nvidia.com/blog/rendering-perfect-reflections-and-refractions-in-path-traced-games/>
- **Minimal retroreflective microfacet model** (Portsmouth, Raab, Belcour, Liu - arXiv June 2026, 2026; evidence weak). Turns any microfacet BSDF into a reciprocal, energy-conserving retroreflector by reflecting the view direction about the normal. Adopted by industry standards per the abstract.
  Evidence: Proofs, plus validation against measured retroreflective materials. No real-time numbers. Could beat: Nothing RageV has today. Relevant only if road markings or signs need retroreflection. Cost: Claimed minimal Risk: Niche
  Sources: <https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.08739>

### F.4 The survey's recommended baseline and candidates

The survey's view before the comparison looked at RageV's code. The roadmap's items took these as their starting baselines where the comparison and verification agreed.

- **Baseline: One BSDF module: GGX with the α = r² mapping, the same Smith term, Schlick Fresnel with an F0 source, and energy compensation by lobe scaling (Turquin/Filament form, read from the existing split-sum DFG table). Provide eval, sample and PDF, and call it from the raster G-buffer and composition, DirectTrace, reflection and GI hits, and the bake. For environment/probe light in the composition, use the Fdez-Aguera split so both halves agree.** Why: Unity HDRP and Filament ship this compensation, and Unreal's Substrate docs say energy is conserved. RageV today has five BSDF variants and no compensation, so rough metals are dark and the paths disagree. Shipped by: Unity HDRP, Filament, Unreal Engine 5 Substrate (energy conservation stated in the docs). Measured by: A white-furnace fixture: spheres with F0 = 1 and roughness 0 to 1 under a uniform environment should read 1.0 in raster, DirectTrace and hit paths. Diff images of rough metals before and after. A,B,B,A frame cost, expected under 0.05 ms.
- **Baseline: Specular AA from the pixel footprint in place of the 0.045 floor. (a) Projected-space isotropic NDF filtering in the G-buffer raster pass, storing the filtered roughness in the normal+roughness lane. (b) Normal-map variance baked into roughness mips at texture cook (Toksvig/vMF), with roughness filtered as α², not perceptual. (c) Ray-cone mip plus roughness widening at hits. Keep only a numerical guard for α² underflow, or treat α close to 0 as a delta lobe.** Why: HDRP, The Order: 1886, CoD WWII and Unreal (Composite Texture) ship these. They remove highlight aliasing on wet floors and chrome without TAA, whereas the floor removes true mirrors and does not follow the footprint. Shipped by: Unity HDRP, The Order: 1886, Call of Duty: WWII, Unreal Engine (Composite Texture, Normal Curvature to Roughness), Minecraft with RTX (ray cones). Measured by: An --aa=none camera orbit on the garage chrome poles and puddle: a temporal-flicker metric (frame-to-frame per-pixel difference) and diff images against a 16x SSAA reference, before and after. Cost in the G-buffer pass A,B,B,A (expected at most 0.1 ms).
- **Baseline: G-buffer contract of about 16-24 bytes of colour lanes plus RageV's extras. Octahedral normal + roughness in R10G10B10A2 (NRD packing). A geometric normal as an octahedral RG16 lane. Base colour in sRGB8. One of either (metalness + specular) or F0, not both. An R32UI lane (instance id, flags, shading model). Emissive in R11G11B10F. Motion in RG16F (2.5D). Previous-frame copies of depth, normal+roughness and id for ReSTIR and denoisers. Pack and unpack defined once.** Why: HDRP, Battlefield V and Cyberpunk keep about 16 bytes per pixel, and NRD and RTXDI expect these guides. The geometric normal answers RageV's shadow-ray and mirror-curvature defects. Shipped by: Unity HDRP, Battlefield V, Cyberpunk 2077, NVIDIA NRD/RTXDI integrations. Measured by: Bytes per pixel and bandwidth per pass at 2560×1600 by RT-14's method, before and after. A pixel-identical step when adding lanes. A normal-precision mirror test (a curved chrome pole reflection diffed against a float32-normal build).
- **Baseline: Hit shading from the same material-decode function as the raster G-buffer write, writing a hit record in the same lane formats. Mip and shading LOD from ray cones, not authored switches. Deferred hit shading with binning where coherence matters, because SER is not available to inline queries from compute or fragment shaders.** Why: Battlefield V's auto-generated hit shaders and Unreal's Hit Lighting are the shipped parity approach. Metro, Snowdrop and the Lumen surface cache show that the cheaper alternatives visibly lose material detail in mirrors. Shipped by: Battlefield V, Unreal Engine 5 (Lumen Hit Lighting), Minecraft with RTX (ray-cone shading LOD). Measured by: The Battlefield V test: trace primary rays through the hit path and diff the decoded material lanes against the raster G-buffer (target: zero apart from documented LOD differences). Reflection hit-shading ms before and after, at 2560×1600 on the RTX 5070 Ti Laptop.
- **Baseline: Composition in compute with shading-model tile classification. The specular weight and the diffuse/specular demodulation factors come from one function (NRD_MaterialFactors pattern, including the energy compensation) that the traces, the denoisers and the composition all call. The raster lit pass is deleted in RT mode (RT2-22).** Why: DOOM: The Dark Ages and Unreal Substrate ship compute composition with tile classification. One shared factor settles RageV's 7% brightness discrepancy by construction. Shipped by: DOOM: The Dark Ages, Unreal Engine 5, Frostbite. Measured by: RT2-22's gates: a diff near zero against the resampled-material path on the garage, the bridge's three cameras and the camp. Frame ms A,B,B,A. Check that demodulation followed by remodulation is an identity on a flat-lit test (diff 0).
- **Candidate: A visibility buffer (64-bit depth + instance/triangle id) with compute material evaluation shared by primary pixels and ray hits** Why better: Removes quad overshading and the material work in the second raster. Primary and hit shading run literally the same reconstruct-and-decode code, so parity holds by construction. Evidence: Hable RTX 3070 1080p: 21-32% faster than deferred at 1-10 pixels per triangle, 17% slower with very large triangles. Shipped at 60 Hz in DOOM: The Dark Ages. Risk: Large, low-poly triangles (camp, garage walls) favour the G-buffer. Rewriting the material system; MSAA and alpha-test paths. Measured by: Build the G-buffer path first (baseline), then the visibility-buffer path, and compare ms per pass A,B,B,A on the garage, the bridge's three cameras and the camp at 2560×1600. Also report triangles per pixel on screen for each scene. Adopt only if faster everywhere, or faster overall with no scene slower beyond the spread.
- **Candidate: EON diffuse in place of Lambert across raster, hits and bake** Why better: Energy-preserving, responds to roughness, comes with exact importance sampling for GI, and is the new interchange standard. Evidence: JCGT 2025 analysis, plus adoption by OpenPBR. No real-time timing published. Risk: A visible look change on rough surfaces. The probe, irradiance-SH and bake integration must match.
